Macro Sub-Teams Analysis · Iran / Murrin nuclear endgame

Can we rule out Murrin's nuclear endgame? If not, what do we do?

From: Macro Sub-Teams  ·  To: David, CIO
Bottom line

Cannot rule it out. Capabilities verified; timeline band 6–12 months once Roaring Lion's own assessment is folded in; only the policy conclusion is dismissible as advocacy.

Other players settle on a Bad outcome by default. Iran, Trump, and China–Russia all sit in the Bad row by revealed preference. Only Israel pushes for the Good row — and Israel is constrained by Pakistan-mediated airspace cross-promises.

01 · Due Diligence on Murrin

Background, motif, and verifiability of the Article 2 claims.

Background — institutionally credentialled, not fringe

Career

Physics + Geophysics at Exeter. Seismologist for Shell in Papua New Guinea, 1984. Joined J.P. Morgan as proprietary trader, 1986. Co-founded Emergent Asset Management 1993 — genuine European EM macro hedge fund. 30+ year career as Macro CIO. Author of Breaking the Code of History (2009) and the explicitly speculative Red Lightning: How the West Lost World War III to China in 2025 (2021). Clients include governments, armed forces, large institutions. Presents at the UK Higher Command and Staff Course and Oxford. Not a fringe figure.

Motif — three structural biases to weight against

Commercial

Sells subscriptions (Global Trader, Murrinations) on a "predict tomorrow's headlines" pitch. Dramatic forecasts drive subscriptions; measured ones do not. Soft but real bias.

Ideological

Explicitly frames his work as defending democracy against autocracy. Self-described hawk. Article 2 ends with "GF concludes that the Americans and Israelis could be forced to use nuclear weapons preemptively" — this is advocacy, not analysis. He also explicitly hopes the US "has been working hard to develop just such a weapon" (a rocket-assisted super-GBU-57). Treat the policy conclusion as Murrin's opinion, not a deduced outcome.

Sourcing

OSINT only. Cites ISIS, CSIS, IAEA, Washington Post — the same public sources we can check directly. No evidence of Track-2 access, regional human intelligence, or Iranian sources. Where he goes beyond is in stitching OSINT into a more aggressive timeline, not in obtaining privileged information.

Verifiability of Article 2's specific claims

Claim Tier Verification
Pickaxe Mountain exists, beyond GBU-57 reach, being actively hardenedCorroboratedISIS Feb & May 2026 reports. Depth 80–100m under granite. Tunnel portal blocking confirmed.
Iran HEU stockpile at 60% enrichmentCorroboratedIAEA Grossi: 441kg as of June 2025. Note: Murrin gives both 300kg and 460kg — internal inconsistency.
GBU-57 specs and penetration limitsCorroborated30,000 lb bunker-buster; ~80m penetration; first operational use June 2025.
Iran's "Axis of Resistance" structureCorroboratedHezbollah, Hamas, Iraqi militias, Houthis. Standard mainstream framing.
Russian / North Korean warhead designs transferredInterpretiveMurrin's word is "assumed". Historical Iran-NK links real, but no confirmation of complete warhead design transfer. CSIS notes NK "exercised restraint" since 2025 strikes.
Chinese HGV technology in Fattah-2InterpretiveWestern analysts (Fabian Hinz, IISS) argue Fattah-2 is a MaRV, not a true HGV. Some Chinese transfer likely; HGV link disputed.
"Missile-City-Nuclear-Integration" doctrineInterpretiveMurrin's inference. No evidence Iran has consolidated enrichment + warhead + delivery into single deep site.
"On the cusp" timeline — weeks to monthsAdvocacyOut of consensus. Once Roaring Lion's own "most stringent professional assessment" (~1 year) is folded in, the band is 6–12 months — closer to Murrin than headline framing implied.
US / Israel "could be forced" to use nuclear weapons preemptivelyAdvocacyMurrin's policy recommendation framed as conclusion. No mainstream policy maker publicly recommending this.
02 · Scenario test

Can we rule the scenario out?

No. Three reasons.

One. The capabilities picture is real. Pickaxe Mountain, the HEU stockpile, the underground hardening, the missile delivery improvements — these are not contested. They are documented by ISIS, IAEA, mainstream defence analysts.

Two. The timeline band converges around 6–12 months once Roaring Lion's own internal assessment is included. The Israeli policy paper explicitly states the breakout timeline is approximately one year (6m for fissile material accumulation, additional months for weaponisation). Pape's structural argument — that politically motivated states with denial capability cannot be coerced by airstrikes alone — implies Iran rebuilds regardless.

Three. Israel is the wild card. Even if US diplomacy and Xi-Putin pressure produce a real Iranian commitment, Israeli unilateral action operates on a separate clock with its own intelligence assessment and political constraints.

Verdict

Treat Murrin Article 2 as a live tail risk on a 6–12 month horizon. Trust him on capabilities. Treat his timeline as the aggressive end of a credible 6–12 month range — Roaring Lion's own internal assessment lands within the same band. Treat his policy conclusion as advocacy, not deduced outcome. Israeli unilateral action is the most plausible single trigger.

03 · How other players react

If the scenario activates, what does each major actor do?

Drawing on Roaring Lion, Parsi, and Pape as context — not as separate analyses.

Roaring Lion framework
A (Nuclear Agreement) — Good = stringent, intrusive inspections, no underground enrichment, HEU removal. Bad = legitimising, eases pressure, regime survives (Iran's 14 points).
B (Pressure) — Good = partial blockade as means to soften Iran for A or C. Bad = calcifies into "neither agreement nor strike," regime rebuilds.
C (Operation) — Good = decisive operation, neutralise sites + remove material + rapid diplomatic transition. Bad = symbolic strike (bridges, power stations) that achieves nothing on nuclear.
United States
Defer the trilemma via diplomacy. Trump's revealed preference is off-ramps, not escalation. Most likely actual choice: symbolic strikes.
Bad C (Strike)
Israel
Pushes hardest for Good Option C (decisive operation). Will act unilaterally if window appears to close, regardless of US position.
Good A or C
Iran
Race-to-threshold under negotiation cover. Has restarted enrichment threat. Per Parsi, planning expanded retaliation. Position weakened by loss of Chinese / Russian protective cover.
Bad A (Agreement)
China
Publicly pushes ceasefire — interests align with Bad A. Per Pape, the Iran War has already changed Beijing's read of US resolve.
Bad A (Agreement)
Russia
Aligned with China publicly. Preserves Iran ties privately (drones, missiles, sanctions-busting).
Bad A (Agreement)
GCC / Pakistan
Hedging strategy already undermined by 2025 war. Pakistan-mediated Saudi airspace cross-promise is the binding operational constraint on Israeli unilateral action.
Tilting
Europe
Aligns with Trump–Xi–Putin diplomatic track. Prepares for energy shock if track fails.
Diplomatic
Japan / Korea
Lean on US for resolution. Long-dormant nuclear conversations re-open. ~60% energy via Hormuz.
Hedging
The trap

Three actors (Iran, Trump, China–Russia) sit in the Bad row by revealed preference. Only Israel pushes for the Good row. If Israel doesn't act, the system settles on a Bad outcome.

04 · Scenario and thematic response

What we do across scenario stress, thematic rotation, and the scenario set.

Three process nodes, each with a defined action.

Scenario stress
Dedicated Murrin branch + Bad C (Strike) variant
  • Murrin Article 2 apex. Tactical nuclear first-use. Paradigm-break. S&P toward 3,500. Oil supply absolute disruption. Proliferation cascade.
  • Bad C (Strike) variant — higher probability, lower severity. US symbolic strikes + Iranian retaliation across Parsi's five vectors. Brent $130–160, SPX -15 to -25%.
Stock & thematic rotation
Rotate Iran-axis exposure; recalibrate China sleeve
  • Reduce Iran-axis exposure. Scan portfolio for direct/indirect exposure to Parsi's retaliation vectors.
  • China sleeve — recalibrate for Beijing's read of US weakness. Near-term re-rate up. Beijing reads each US misstep on Iran as confirmation of declining US resolve.
  • Energy paradigm shift drives renewables opportunity set.
Scenario set
Three scenarios with assumption inputs

One Brent note across all scenarios: 9-Apr Brent $115 12M looks too low given Hormuz wasn't relieved by summit. Mark higher; use forward curve as anchor.

  • Benign anchor — Pakistan-mediated framework holds. Modal favourable case. Saudi–Pakistan airspace cross-promise sustains the diplomatic window through Q3 2026. Brent near current forwards.
  • Adverse case — Bad C (Strike) variant. Highest-probability bad outcome. Brent $130–160; SPX -15 to -25%; US 10Y stress at 5.25%+; DXY weaker; direct hits on UAE-linked names, regional fintech, cable-dependent payment processing.
  • Apex tail — Murrin first-use scenario. Low probability, paradigm-break severity. SPX toward 3,500; Brent toward $200+; gold toward $7,000+; US 10Y bifurcated; DXY chaos; proliferation cascade.

02 · Scenario test

Cannot rule it out — but firm assessment places it below the scenario-modelling threshold.

The analytical conclusion (live tail risk on 6–12 month horizon) is unchanged. The firm operational decision is to bear this tail in the long-term allocation rather than model it as a scenario.

Why this is consistent. The modelled scenarios drive the return assumptions for portfolio construction. The apex first-use event is paradigm-break — it would mean SPX toward 3,500, Brent toward $200+, log-Fibonacci breakdown of the 7th mini-bull, EPS reset to 2025 low-to-mid-200s. As David noted: "in short, I probably don't need to know what the damage would look like." The protection is structural, not model-derived. Modelling the apex would produce numbers but not change the long-term allocation prescription.

What this leaves in the modelled scenario set: Stalemate and Resolution — the two scenarios where model-driven return assumptions add value.

What this leaves in the long-term allocation: the nightmare convergence — nuclear Iran + reinvigorated Russia + hedging China + exhausted America + isolated Israel arriving simultaneously around summer 2026 as global oil buffers run dry. Monitored daily at the tip-of-iceberg layer.

Verdict — firm consolidated

Murrin Article 2 cannot be ruled out and remains a live tail risk on 6–12 month horizon. The firm operates against this risk via the long-term allocation, not via dedicated scenario modelling. The apex tail probability is assessed below the threshold where another modelled scenario would change the portfolio prescription. Israeli unilateral action remains the most plausible trigger, with the paradoxical timing flag from Citrinowicz (max risk in deal-closing phase).

Mapping the stakeholder positions onto the firm's bifurcated scenario set.

Two-scenario mapping
Stalemate (modal, Eric/team) — Sustained economic pressure. Maps to persistent Bad B in Roaring Lion terms — neither agreement nor strike, regime survives, energy tightens, fiscal pressure builds.
Resolution (improving probability) — JPOA 2.0 nuclear framework + Hormuz Council governance + HEU export under Security Guarantee. Maps to Good A (stringent agreement) in Roaring Lion terms, plus the Ferguson post-Suez Hormuz architecture.
Apex (held in the long-term allocation) — Murrin first-use scenario or nightmare convergence. Below the scenario-modelling threshold per firm decision.

Khamenei-attributed directive (HEU stays in Iran) denied by another senior source. Hormuz position hardening separately: PGSA territorial expansion onto UAE/Omani waters, $2m per vessel toll system — but Iran now accepting tolls in crypto or Chinese yuan (WSJ), which fits the Ferguson Council framework's revenue-sharing concept.
Split (A)

Publicly pushes Resolution (ceasefire, shipping lanes reopened) — Xi/Putin Beijing meeting reinforced. But pivot is partially performative: CNN (21 May) — PRC continued sending missile components and fuel precursors during the war. Per Pape, Iran War already shifted Beijing's read of US resolve. Resolution serves China interests if it enables face-saving Iranian regime survival + Hormuz access; China-Russia signatories to a post-war Gulf Treaty Organisation would be the cleanest endgame for Beijing.
Pushes Resolution
Russia
Aligned with China publicly. Has formally offered to take Iran's HEU (acknowledged by Araghchi, though "not currently under discussion"). Russia accepting HEU under Security Guarantee architecture would be the technical pathway. Preserves Iran ties privately (drones via Caspian).
HEU custodian role available
GCC / Pakistan Updated
Pakistan Field Marshal Munir in Tehran 21 May. Pakistan-Qatar drafted memo; Saudi/Turkey/Egypt supporting. Mediator coalition is broader and active. GCC structural veto on raw Iranian Hormuz control — but Ferguson Council framework (45% Iran, 30% Oman, balance others) addresses this with co-governance. Saudi-Pakistan airspace cross-promise still binding on Israeli unilateral action.
Active mediators
Europe
Aligns with diplomatic track. Roaring Lion notes Europe refused US coalition request to reopen Strait (already at war 4 years; "you cannot build a coalition retroactively"). Implication: Europe constrained from leading any post-war architecture. NATO contribution to HEU Security Guarantee would be the test.
Diplomatic backer
Japan / Korea
Lean on US for Resolution. Long-dormant nuclear conversations re-open if scenario shifts to apex. ~60% energy via Hormuz.
Hedging
The map — firm consolidated

Stalemate remains modal because US-Iran nuclear divide is wide and Iran's hardliner faction blocks Good A; energy tightens and fiscal pressure builds through July–Sept. Resolution probability is improving because mediator coalition is broader and active, Trump trajectory (per Atlantic / Mearsheimer) is "humble pie," and the Ferguson Council + Security Guarantee architecture answers the previously-unanswered Hormuz and HEU questions. Israeli unilateral action remains the wild card — and the paradoxical timing flag matters most now: as Resolution closes, Israeli strike risk peaks.

04 · Scenario and thematic response — firm consolidated

What we do across scenario stress, thematic rotation, and the scenario set.

The apex is held in the long-term allocation per David's direction (section 02). The scenario set is bifurcated (Stalemate + Resolution) per Eric/team decision.

Scenario stress
Stalemate stress (modal) + apex tail held in the long-term allocation
  • Stalemate stress (primary). Energy tightens July–Sept, inventory draws accelerate, oil higher, fiscal pressure on bond yields, EPS holds in S&P until 2027 demand-destruction phase. Mid-teens equity downside calibration.
  • Apex tail (held in the long-term allocation, not in the stress set). Per firm decision — paradigm-break severity, structural protection rather than scenario modelling.
Stock & thematic rotation
Rotate Iran-axis exposure; recalibrate China sleeve
  • Reduce Iran-axis exposure. Scan portfolio for direct/indirect exposure to retaliation vectors should Stalemate deteriorate (UAE data centres, regional fintech, Persian Gulf cable-dependent payment processing).
  • China sleeve — recalibrate for Beijing's read of US weakness. Near-term re-rate up post-Xi-Putin alignment. Each US misstep on Iran reinforces declining-US-resolve thesis; Asian allies hedging now is the observable signal.
  • Energy paradigm shift drives renewables opportunity set. Rare earths, security, infrastructure long; defensive on regional refining; Singapore refinery study warrants urgency in Stalemate scenario.
Scenario set
Bifurcated: Stalemate (modal) + Resolution (improving) + BAU as third reference baseline

Per Eric/team binding decision (22 May): Two scenarios are modelled, plus the BAU baseline. Escalation/Bad-Strike scenarios judged low enough probability to not warrant separate modelling. Apex Murrin first-use scenario held in the long-term allocation (see the scenario test above).

Scenario 1 — Stalemate (modal, Eric/team):

  • Sustained economic pressure most likely. US-Iran nuclear divide wide; diplomatic option improving as costs rise to both sides.
  • China's stance on reopening Hormuz makes both sides hesitate to escalate.
  • Energy market continues to tighten; higher prices; inventory levels run down through July–Sept.
  • Pressure builds on Trump to pivot. Handouts mitigate but trigger fiscal pressure → bond yields up.
  • S&P EPS holds — index heavyweights still deliver until demand destruction sets in (~2027).
  • Equities: mid-teens downside. Brent: tight, elevated, drawing inventory.

Scenario 2 — Resolution (improving probability, Eric/team + David):

  • Mearsheimer "humble pie" framing — Trump accepts Iran-won-the-war reality in exchange for face-saving concessions. Atlantic / Kagan (21 May): Trump negotiating "letter of intent" with Iran for 30-day formal negotiation period.
  • JPOA 2.0 nuclear architecture — 14-point Iranian framework; rebuild civilian nuclear above-ground (easier for verification, also easier to strike again if needed — the "pearl harbor" risk both sides must address).
  • Ferguson Hormuz Council framework — Iran as Chair of post-war governance body; revenue share 45% Iran / 30% Oman / balance other Gulf states; transit fees capped at cost (~$28k per crossing) rather than rent extraction. Iran already accepting tolls in crypto or yuan (WSJ Binance) — fits the Council revenue-sharing mechanism.
  • HEU export under Security Guarantee — NATO + others provide protection architecture against "pearl harbor" attacks during HEU logistics. Russia available as custodian per Araghchi acknowledgement. This addresses Iranian regime survival concern that any export creates strike window.
  • Post-war Gulf Treaty Organisation — China and Russia signatories; could provide the multilateral framework for the entire post-war Gulf security architecture. Off-ramp for all sides without Iran-as-monopolist or US-bases-as-monopolist.
  • WSJ-Binance reframing: Iran sanctions relief is less of a Western concession than headline suggests — Iran has been moving $10bn+ annually via crypto. Sanctions relief mainly normalises what's already happening. Reduces the political cost of Resolution to US.
  • Equities: mid-single-digit upside. Brent: declines toward $90–110 over months; trust rebuild takes quarters.

Scenario 3 — BAU baseline (pre-meeting tables, reference only). The 9-Apr assumptions continue as the baseline reference per yesterday's tables.

Scenario bifurcation per Eric (CIO layer e) binding decision. David (layer d) confirms and specifies Resolution mechanics — JPOA 2.0 + Ferguson Hormuz Council + HEU Security Guarantee + post-war Gulf Treaty Organisation. The Atlantic / Mearsheimer / WSJ-Binance pieces all converge on the Resolution scenario being structurally more plausible than v1 framing suggested. Apex tail held in the long-term allocation per David's explicit direction that the nightmare convergence sits there for now. Macro sub-teams (layer b) consolidates accordingly; the tip-of-iceberg layer (layer a) takes the narratives.
v3 · Post-v2 inputs refresh · 25 May 2026
2 substantive · refreshed

Six inputs circulated 22–24 May. The v2 architecture is unchanged — Stalemate (modal) / Resolution (improving) / apex held in the long-term allocation — and most of the new material corroborates it. One substantive change: new ISW and Bloomberg reporting reframes Iran's Hormuz demand as sovereignty, not transit revenue, which qualifies the v2 read that the Ferguson Council "addresses" the Gulf veto — raised as an open question for the Resolution mechanic (layer d). Corroboration refreshes: US (Trump dissuaded from striking by the Gulf trio), Israel (Netanyahu's domestic-political stakes), China + Japan/Korea (FT: observable US munitions depletion), GCC (UAE dove-shift; Gulf letter rejecting Iranian Hormuz control). Murrin Article 3 (imperial-decline thesis) is logged as backdrop to the long-term allocation's "exhausted America" leg, not a scenario input.

Accumulated 25 May update. The weekend's market and expert reads are folded in: crude has repriced toward Resolution (Brent ~$99) on Trump's one-page MOU, but Townsend reads that MOU as "April 7, Round 2" — a framework, not a deal, with HEU unresolved — and Global21 quantifies the US/Gulf munitions exhaustion that is partly forcing de-escalation. Net: Resolution's probability and market-pricing rise while its substance stays deferred. (The Atlantic Council Horn-of-Africa piece is registered but not folded — low bearing on the scenario set.)

Pakistan Field Marshal Munir in Tehran 21 May; Pakistan-Qatar memo; Saudi/Turkey/Egypt supporting — mediator coalition broader and active. Bloomberg (22 May): UAE, Saudi and Qatar separately urged Trump not to restart the war (a notable UAE dove-shift post-OPEC-exit); Gargash put a deal at "50-50." But the same reporting shows all GCC bar Oman formally rejecting permanent Iranian control of Hormuz. This pressures the v2 read that the Council "addresses" the veto: if Iran's demand is sovereignty and the GCC won't ratify it, revenue-sharing co-governance may not close the gap. Saudi-Pakistan airspace cross-promise still binding on Israeli unilateral action.
Active mediators

Aligns with diplomatic track. Roaring Lion notes Europe refused US coalition request to reopen Strait (already at war 4 years; "you cannot build a coalition retroactively"). Implication: Europe constrained from leading any post-war architecture. NATO contribution to HEU Security Guarantee would be the test.
Diplomatic backer

Long-dormant nuclear conversations re-open if the scenario shifts to apex.
Hedging
The map — firm consolidated

Stalemate remains modal because the US-Iran nuclear divide is wide and Iran's hardliner faction blocks Good A; energy tightens and fiscal pressure builds through July–Sept. Resolution probability is improving on the diplomatic track — mediator coalition broader and active, Trump trajectory "humble pie" (now corroborated by the Gulf trio dissuading him from striking), WSJ-Binance lowering the US political cost. But the Hormuz leg is harder than the v2 Council framing implied: Iran's demand reads as sovereignty, not revenue, and the GCC has formally rejected Iranian control — so the single richest obstacle to Resolution now sits at the strait, not the warhead. Two cross-currents from the weekend reads: de-escalation is partly forced by materiel exhaustion (Global21 — US down to ~800 Tomahawks / 400 JASSM-ER, Gulf interceptors good for under a week), while the oil rally and Trump's one-page MOU may be front-running a deal whose substance is again deferred (Townsend's "April 7, Round 2"). Israeli unilateral action remains the wild card — as Resolution closes, Israeli strike risk peaks.

04 · Scenario and thematic response — firm consolidated

What we do across scenario stress, thematic rotation, and the scenario set.

The apex is held in the long-term allocation per David's direction (section 02). The scenario set is bifurcated (Stalemate + Resolution) per Eric/team decision.

Scenario stress
Stalemate stress (modal) + apex tail held in the long-term allocation
  • Stalemate stress (primary). Energy tightens July–Sept, inventory draws accelerate, oil higher, fiscal pressure on bond yields, EPS holds in S&P until 2027 demand-destruction phase. Mid-teens equity downside calibration.
  • Apex tail (held in the long-term allocation, not in the stress set). Per firm decision — paradigm-break severity, structural protection rather than scenario modelling.
Stock & thematic rotation
Rotate Iran-axis exposure; recalibrate China sleeve
  • Reduce Iran-axis exposure. Scan portfolio for direct/indirect exposure to retaliation vectors should Stalemate deteriorate (UAE data centres, regional fintech, Persian Gulf cable-dependent payment processing).
  • China sleeve — recalibrate for Beijing's read of US weakness. Near-term re-rate up post-Xi-Putin alignment. The declining-US-resolve thesis now has an observable marker, not just inference: FT (24 May) reports the US fired >1,000 of ~3,100 Tomahawks and is deprioritising Indo-Pacific deliveries to restock the Middle East; Global21 puts residual ready inventory at ~800 Tomahawks / 400 JASSM-ER, with Gulf interceptors good for under a week of high-intensity war — Asian allies hedging is the visible signal. (This is the observed end of Murrin Article 3's decline thesis; the Taiwan-war timeline stays out as extrapolative.)
  • Energy paradigm shift drives renewables opportunity set. Rare earths, security, infrastructure long; defensive on regional refining; Singapore refinery study warrants urgency in Stalemate scenario.
Scenario set
Bifurcated: Stalemate (modal) + Resolution (improving) + BAU as third reference baseline

Per Eric/team binding decision (22 May): Two scenarios are modelled, plus the BAU baseline. Escalation/Bad-Strike scenarios judged low enough probability to not warrant separate modelling. Apex Murrin first-use scenario held in the long-term allocation (see the scenario test above). Architecture unchanged in v3.

Scenario 1 — Stalemate (modal, Eric/team):

  • Sustained economic pressure most likely. US-Iran nuclear divide wide; diplomatic option improving as costs rise to both sides.
  • China's stance on reopening Hormuz makes both sides hesitate to escalate.
  • Energy market continues to tighten; higher prices; inventory levels run down through July–Sept.
  • Pressure builds on Trump to pivot. Handouts mitigate but trigger fiscal pressure → bond yields up.
  • S&P EPS holds — index heavyweights still deliver until demand destruction sets in (~2027).
  • Equities: mid-teens downside. Brent: tight, elevated, drawing inventory.

Scenario 2 — Resolution (improving probability, Eric/team + David):

  • Mearsheimer "humble pie" framing — Trump accepts Iran-won-the-war reality in exchange for face-saving concessions. Atlantic / Kagan (21 May): Trump negotiating "letter of intent" with Iran for 30-day formal negotiation period.
  • JPOA 2.0 nuclear architecture — 14-point Iranian framework; rebuild civilian nuclear above-ground (easier for verification, also easier to strike again if needed — the "pearl harbor" risk both sides must address).
  • Ferguson Hormuz Council framework — Iran as Chair of post-war governance body; revenue share 45% Iran / 30% Oman / balance other Gulf states; transit fees capped at cost (~$28k per crossing) rather than rent extraction. Iran already accepting tolls in crypto or yuan (WSJ Binance) — fits the Council revenue-sharing mechanism.
  • HEU export under Security Guarantee — NATO + others provide protection architecture against "pearl harbor" attacks during HEU logistics. Russia available as custodian per Araghchi acknowledgement. This addresses Iranian regime survival concern that any export creates strike window.
  • Post-war Gulf Treaty Organisation — China and Russia signatories; could provide the multilateral framework for the entire post-war Gulf security architecture. Off-ramp for all sides without Iran-as-monopolist or US-bases-as-monopolist.
  • WSJ-Binance reframing: Iran sanctions relief is less of a Western concession than headline suggests — Iran has been moving $10bn+ annually via crypto. Sanctions relief mainly normalises what's already happening. Reduces the political cost of Resolution to US.
  • Equities: mid-single-digit upside. Brent: declines toward $90–110 over months; trust rebuild takes quarters. The market is already there: Brent fell 4.2% to ~$99 on 25 May (>5% on the week) on the deal headlines — inside the band. Rates channel to watch: a deal → lower oil → disinflation → Fed-easing space (Hassett), though bond strategists see yields structurally high regardless (the fiscal-overstretch thread).
v3 · Open question · layer d

Hormuz: sovereignty vs revenue. The Resolution mechanic frames the strait as a revenue problem solved by the Ferguson Council (Iran as Chair, 45% revenue, $28k cost cap). New reporting suggests the real gap is status, not money. ISW (23 May, "Not Just About Fees"): Iran's primary objective is recognised sovereignty over Hormuz, and the fee scheme itself likely fails on sanctions compliance while Iran retains coercive control regardless. Bloomberg (22 May): all GCC states bar Oman have formally rejected permanent Iranian control. If Iran wants sovereignty and the GCC won't ratify it, revenue-sharing co-governance may not close the gap. This does not change the scenario architecture — it flags that the Hormuz leg, not the warhead, is now the binding constraint on Resolution, and the Council design may need a sovereignty / face-saving dimension rather than only a revenue split. Surfaced for David (layer d), who owns the Resolution mechanic.

v3 · Deal-watch · 25 May

Is the deal real, or is the market front-running it? Trump announced a "largely negotiated" one-page, 14-point MOU on 23 May (declare the war over, gradually reopen Hormuz, lift the US blockade), and crude repriced toward Resolution — Brent ~$99. Townsend supplies the discipline: this is "April 7, Round 2" — a negotiation-and-Strait-reopening framework, not a nuclear deal, with the ~440kg HEU stockpile still the unresolved core, and the oil gap-down possibly "selling into a misunderstanding." Tehran (Tasnim) says the draft could still collapse over frozen-asset clauses, and the US blockade stays until signing. Net: Resolution's probability and market-pricing are rising, but its substance — HEU, Hormuz sovereignty, sanctions sequencing — is again deferred. Reinforces the Hormuz flag above; no change to the architecture.

Scenario 3 — BAU baseline (pre-meeting tables, reference only). The 9-Apr assumptions continue as the baseline reference.

v2 architecture (Stalemate + Resolution + BAU; apex in the long-term allocation) holds unchanged. v3 adds one substantive flag — the Hormuz sovereignty/revenue tension above (ISW 23 May + Bloomberg 22 May), raised as an open question for the Resolution mechanic (David, layer d). Other v3 changes are corroboration refreshes to the actor map (US, Israel, China, GCC, Japan/Korea) and a backdrop note on Murrin Article 3. No new modelled scenario; the Taiwan / US-default-by-2030 claims are held as extrapolative per prior firm decision.
v4 · Escalation–de-escalation whipsaw · 12 Jun 2026
US chip flipped · architecture held

The architecture holds — Stalemate (modal) / Resolution (improving) / apex in the long-term allocation. The 8–12 Jun whipsaw validates the v3 read rather than overturning it: a violent escalation ran straight into an off-ramp, and Trump's revealed preference stayed off-ramp even after he struck. One position chip moves — the US, from "Pivoting" to "Strikes, then off-ramp." Resolution's probability is intact and arguably firmer (the off-ramp held under maximum pressure); its substance is deferred for a third time, and Hormuz is now the live crux of any signing.

What moved from v3: the US chip flips; the Israeli trigger window is shown firing (8 Jun strikes inside Iran); the Hormuz sovereignty question moves from side-flag to the gate any signing must pass, because the 12 Jun MOU turns on restarting Strait shipping. No new modelled scenario — the kinetic exchange falls in Stalemate-stress, below the apex.

The 8–12 Jun whipsaw

Weekend
The two-month ceasefire collapses as Iran downs a US military helicopter.
8 Jun
Iran–Israel direct exchange. After an Iranian barrage, confirmed Israeli strikes inside Iran — air defences and ballistic-missile sites (ISW), a petrochemical site (WSJ). First direct exchange since the April ceasefire.
9–10 Jun
US forces strike ~20 targets in southern Iran on 9 Jun, with further strikes on 10 Jun. Trump threatens to seize Kharg Island and "assume total control" of Iran's oil.
12 Jun
Trump scraps the threatened third day and declares a deal "close" — "a very strong MOU that is a little conceptual" to restart Hormuz shipping, with a possible European signing this weekend. Iran (Fars) says no text is agreed.
What it confirms: the off-ramp preference survives contact with real war, but the route is now episodic kinetic exchange. What it does not change: the bifurcated architecture, the apex held in the long-term allocation, the Murrin DD. Watch-list: the confirmed-Israeli-strike trigger fired on 8 Jun; mediation-breakdown did not — talks are bad-tempered and stalled but ongoing, with Trump still claiming a deal is close.
The map — post-escalation

Stalemate stays modal because the US–Iran nuclear divide is wide and the hardliner faction blocks Good A. Resolution's probability is intact and arguably firmer — the off-ramp held under maximum pressure — but its substance is deferred a third time: the MOU is "a little conceptual," Iran denies any agreed text, and the HEU and Hormuz-sovereignty cores are untouched. The richest obstacle to Resolution now sits at the strait; the warhead is the lesser problem. The unresolved question is whether a "conceptual" MOU's revenue-sharing Council design can satisfy Iran's sovereignty demand; if it cannot, a signing this weekend buys only a pause, and the strait reopens as the binding constraint within weeks.

Aligned with China publicly; has formally offered to take Iran’s HEU (acknowledged by Araghchi, “not currently under discussion”). Accepting HEU under the Security Guarantee architecture would be the technical pathway. Preserves Iran ties privately (drones via Caspian).
HEU custodian role available
GCC / Pakistan
Mediation delivered the ceasefire — Pakistan’s PM announced the deal first, Qatar brokered a reported $12bn financial channel, and the UAE reopened a direct security line to Tehran. The mediator coalition is broad and active. All GCC bar Oman reject permanent Iranian control of Hormuz, which keeps pressure on the management question. The Saudi–Pakistan airspace cross-promise still binds Israeli unilateral action.
Active mediators
Europe
Aligns with the diplomatic track; refused the US request to reopen the Strait, so it is constrained from leading any post-war architecture. A NATO contribution to the HEU Security Guarantee would be the test. Switzerland is the named venue for the 19 Jun signing; the post-war security architecture would sit with other parties.
Diplomatic backer
Japan / Korea
Lean on the US for Resolution; ~60% of energy via Hormuz. The US warned Japan of up-to-two-year Tomahawk delays as the Pentagon prioritises Middle East restocking — US overstretch landing on an Indo-Pacific ally, reinforcing the hedge. Dormant nuclear conversations re-open if the scenario shifts to apex.
Hedging

The 8–15 Jun whipsaw

8–10 Jun
Ceasefire collapses (Iran downs a US helicopter); Iran–Israel direct exchange and confirmed Israeli strikes inside Iran on 8 Jun; US forces strike ~20 targets in southern Iran (9–10 Jun); Trump threatens to seize Kharg Island.
12 Jun
Trump scraps the threatened third day and declares a deal “close” — “a very strong MOU that is a little conceptual.” Mehr publishes a 14-provision draft; Iran (Fars) says no text is agreed.
13 Jun
Araghchi lays out a two-stage MOU — Stage 1 ceasefire + Hormuz reopening + economic relief, Stage 2 the nuclear file. ISW: Iran insists on “management” of the strait; CNN reports Iran is collapsing tunnels and mining its HEU stores to defeat seizure.
14 Jun
Israel bombs Beirut as the signing nears, putting the deal at risk; Trump warns Netanyahu off further Beirut strikes.
15 Jun
Trump declares the deal “complete”; the US naval blockade lifts and the war ends on all fronts including Lebanon. The finalised MOU is set to sign in Switzerland on 19 Jun, with Hormuz reopening after the signing. Brent falls toward $84.
What it confirms: the off-ramp preference produced a signed ceasefire, on an unreconciled core. What it does not change: the bifurcated architecture, the apex held in the long-term allocation, the Murrin DD. Watch-list: three standing triggers fired this weekend — a deal announced/agreed, Iran’s formal acceptance, and a confirmed Israeli strike (Beirut, 14 Jun). The 19 Jun signing is the next live date; the mediation-breakdown trigger has not fired, though Tehran’s hardline camp has yet to ratify.
The map — ceasefire to sign

Stalemate stays modal because the US–Iran nuclear divide is wide and the hardliner faction blocks Good A. The ceasefire is a Stage-1 instrument: it ends the shooting, lifts the blockade and reopens Hormuz, while the nuclear core, the HEU stockpile and sanctions sequencing are pushed to a 60-day track. The Hormuz sovereignty question — open since v3 — resolves, and in Iran’s favour: the strait reopens toll-free but under Iranian management, a reopening Iran controls and can reverse. The unresolved question is whether a Stage-1 ceasefire that front-loads Iran’s wins survives an unmade nuclear settlement; if it does not, the strait returns as the binding constraint within weeks.

05 · Market & thematic implications

Scenario views across equities, Brent, rates, and rotation.

Views only. Brent bands and the scenario architecture are unchanged from v4; the market has run to the bottom of the Resolution-quick band on the ceasefire.

ScenarioEquitiesBrentRatesSector & thematic rotation
Stalemate
Modal		 Mid-teens downside; EPS holds until ~2027 demand destruction. Tight, elevated, drawing inventory. Brent fell toward $84 on the 15 Jun ceasefire, below the Resolution-quick band. Per Carlyle, the move is destocking-driven — US SPR near its operational floor, Cushing near the settlement floor — not a fundamentals rebalance, and fragile to any reopening slippage. Fiscal pressure lifts yields; energy tightening Jul–Sep adds to the curve. Iran-axis exposure screens unattractive on the retaliation vectors (UAE data centres, regional fintech, Gulf cable-dependent payments). China sleeve re-rates up on the Beijing read of US weakness; munitions depletion (FT, Global21) is the observable marker. Energy-paradigm shift favours rare earths, security and infrastructure; defensive on regional refining. A new non-oil vector: Iran is extending the transit-control playbook to the Europe–Asia undersea cables — IRGC-linked demands for transit fees on the hyperscalers and veiled threats to the fibre, reaching toward SWIFT. Low feasibility, but it sharpens the cable-dependent-payments screen.
Resolution
Improving		 Mid-single-digit upside. Declines toward $90–110 over months; market below the band at ~$84 on the ceasefire, pricing a clean reopening the substance does not yet support; trust rebuild takes quarters. Yields ease as the energy premium unwinds. Iran-axis exposure screens better selectively; the energy/renewables opportunity set persists on the structural energy-paradigm shift; the China sleeve recalibrates on a firmer Hormuz-access outcome.

Apex / tail: escalation toward $130–160+ Brent is held in the long-term allocation; first-use ($200+, SPX ~3,500) sits below the scenario-modelling threshold as structural tail protection. The deal re-times this tail to the 60-day nuclear window rather than removing it — the HEU stays in Iran and is being hardened. The Taiwan-war and US-default extrapolations are held as backdrop only, outside the modelled set.

Resolution mechanics

The Resolution path follows the framework David specified: Mearsheimer’s “humble pie” off-ramp; a JPOA 2.0 nuclear architecture; the Ferguson Hormuz Council (Iran as Chair, a 45% / 30% / balance revenue split, transit fees at cost); HEU export under a Security Guarantee with Russia available as custodian; and a post-war Gulf Treaty Organisation with China and Russia as signatories. The realised Stage-1 MOU diverges from the Council mechanic on Hormuz: rather than a chaired revenue split with fees at cost, the strait reopens toll-free but under Iranian and Omani management — Iran’s sovereignty claim conceded operationally, the revenue-sharing design bypassed. The deeper cores the framework was built to settle — HEU ~440kg, enrichment, sanctions sequencing — remain for the 60-day track.

v6 · MoU signed · 18 Jun 2026
US chip flipped · MoU signed 17 Jun · architecture held

The architecture holds — Stalemate (modal) / Resolution (improving) / apex in the long-term allocation. The US and Iran signed the memorandum of understanding on 17 June, two days ahead of the scheduled 19 June Switzerland ceremony, with a senior US official reading the text aloud. The blockade lifts and oil sanctions waive on signing; every binding constraint carries into the 60-day negotiating track. One chip moves: the US, to “MoU signed.”

What moved from v5: the signed text is the version Bloomberg published on 16 June, with Iran-secured edits to clauses 1 and 5 that appear only in the copy briefed to US media, so the two sides already read those clauses differently. Clause 5 time-boxes Hormuz toll-free passage to 60 days, then opens fee talks with the Gulf states; Clause 1 adds a Lebanese-sovereignty guarantee Tehran reads as a phased Israeli withdrawal — a new and contested vector. The nuclear undertaking has Iran reiterate it will never produce a weapon, with the enriched-uranium stockpile left to the 60-day track (the read-aloud text names on-site down-blending under IAEA as the baseline method); the $300bn clarifies as external Gulf investment, not a US outlay. v6 also sets out the signed MoU Article by Article (§04) as a reference for the 60-day track.

Inputs (16–18 Jun intake): ISW Iran Update Special Report 17 Jun — MoU signed (Gary via Eric) · ISW Iran Update 16 Jun — leaked-text comparison · David signed-text note (18 Jun) · WSJ “Tehran to sell oil immediately” / “stocks have room if Hormuz reopens” · ST “oil slides on Iran supply” · SCMP “no victory, no defeat” / “permanent Lebanon ceasefire” · BT $300bn Gulf-investment · Mehr ~$10bn/60-days · Axios 30-day waiver

Yellow marks what is new or changed in v6 — the 17 Jun signing and the US chip flip, the signed Clause 5 60-day Hormuz toll-box, the new Clause 1 Lebanon vector, the signed nuclear wording, and the $300bn clarification, with a new Article-by-Article reference of the signed MoU itself at §04. The scenario architecture, Resolution mechanics, Murrin due diligence and the Brent bands carry from v5 unmarked.

Bottom line · ceasefire signed, settlement deferred

The scenario set stays bifurcated: Stalemate (modal) and Resolution (improving probability), with the apex tail held in the long-term allocation below the scenario-modelling threshold. The Stage-1 ceasefire MOU ends the war on all fronts, lifts the blockade, and reopens Hormuz under Iranian arrangements, while the nuclear core — HEU and enrichment — is deferred to a 60-day negotiation that Tehran’s own media is already calling a pause to rebuild. It is now signed — executed on 17 June, two days early, the blockade lifting and oil sanctions waiving — with the durable settlement still gated on the 60-day track, extendable by mutual consent. Iran-secured edits to clauses 1 (Lebanon) and 5 (Hormuz) carry deliberate ambiguity the 60-day track has to resolve. Murrin’s Article 2 nuclear scenario remains the analytical foundation under structural tail protection; the deferral keeps breakout capacity intact, so the apex is re-timed to the 60-day window rather than retired. Full scenario stances, drivers, equity and Brent views are in section 03.

01 · Due Diligence on Murrin

Verdict unchanged — cannot rule it out.

Capabilities corroborated (Tier 1); interpretive layers held as hypotheses (Tier 2); the policy conclusion treated as advocacy (Tier 3). The full claim-by-claim verifiability table sits in the v1 tab.

Murrin’s capabilities case is sound and OSINT-sourced. Timeline sits at the aggressive end of a 6–12 month band that overlaps Roaring Lion’s own assessment. The preemptive-nuclear-use conclusion is his advocacy. The Gallant “440 Kilograms” piece restates the ~440kg 60% HEU stockpile that anchors the capabilities case, and ISW, citing five US-intelligence sources via CNN, reports Iran is physically hardening that stockpile — collapsing tunnels and mining storage entrances to defeat any seizure — so the fissile core is intact, deferred, and being entrenched. The signed text has Iran “reiterate” it will never produce a weapon and names on-site down-blending under IAEA supervision as the baseline for the stockpile — but that is gated to the final agreement and runs against the tunnel-hardening, so breakout capacity is deferred, not removed. “Cannot rule out the nuclear scenario” holds as the analytical foundation; the apex is handled via the long-term allocation rather than modelled scenarios.

02 · Scenario test

Live tail risk on a 6–12 month horizon — below the scenario-modelling threshold.

The conclusion is unchanged; the operational decision is to bear this tail in the long-term allocation rather than model it.

The apex first-use event is paradigm-break, so protection is structural rather than model-derived. As David noted, “in short, I probably don’t need to know what the damage would look like.” The deal does not lower the tail — it re-times it. The MOU defers the nuclear question to a 60-day negotiation while the HEU stays inside Iran, so the breakout clock moves from the battlefield to the talks table without resetting. Iranian media frames the signing as a wartime pause that “delays the final battle” and buys time to rebuild — negotiation as a means of managing the adversary, in the IRGC paper’s words. ISW reads Iran using renewed economic access through the 60-day window to rebuild the Axis of Resistance, Hezbollah first, with funding to follow the first unfreezing of Iranian assets — the breakout clock and the proxy rebuild running on the same window. What stays in the long-term allocation is the nightmare convergence — nuclear Iran + reinvigorated Russia + hedging China + exhausted America + isolated Israel arriving together around summer 2026 as oil buffers run dry; Murrin Article 3’s late-stage US-decline thesis sits on the “exhausted America” leg as backdrop conviction, monitored daily at the tip-of-iceberg layer.

Verdict

Murrin Article 2 cannot be ruled out and remains a live tail risk on a 6–12 month horizon. The apex probability sits below the threshold where another modelled scenario would change the prescription. Israeli unilateral action remains the most plausible trigger — and Israel’s 14 Jun strike on Beirut, timed to the eve of a signing, fired the Citrinowicz window a second time.

04 · The signed MoU

What the text says — the milestone, Article by Article. New · v6

The 14-point text Bloomberg published on 16 June and the version a senior US official read aloud on signing (17 Jun); the Article text below is verbatim from those primary publications. The read-aloud version follows the draft with the Iran-secured edits to Articles 1 (Lebanon) and 5 (Hormuz) and an addition to Article 8 — on-site down-blending of the enriched-uranium stockpile under IAEA supervision as the baseline method. The flag is our reading of what each Article concedes, defers, or leaves contested.

ArticleText (close paraphrase)Our reading
1 · Ceasefire Immediate and permanent end to the war on all fronts including Lebanon; no hostile actions; refrain from the threat or use of force; the final agreement confirms this. Signed edit: a Lebanese-sovereignty guarantee was added. Contested — Iran reads it as obliging an Israeli withdrawal; Israel is not a signatory and keeps striking.
2 · Sovereignty Both parties respect each other’s sovereignty and territorial integrity and refrain from interfering in internal affairs. Implicitly constrains US regime-change; the IRGC structure survives. Iran also leans on this to frame “managing” Hormuz as an internal affair.
3 · 60-day window Negotiate and reach a final agreement within a maximum of 60 days, extendable by mutual consent. The clock for every deferred core. “Extendable” means the pause can stretch if Iran judges its leverage growing.
4 · US obligations Immediate: lift the naval blockade, prevent interference against Iran, restore traffic within 30 days to pre-war capacity proportional to Iran’s pre-war volume. US withdraws forces from surrounding areas within 30 days after the final agreement. Asymmetry: the blockade lifts at once, but force withdrawal waits on the final agreement — US forces stay through the 60-day window plus 30 days. “Proportional to Iran’s pre-war traffic” hands Iran the reopening pace.
5 · Iran obligations (Hormuz) Immediate: resume merchant shipping in the Gulf / Sea of Oman within 30 days to pre-war volume, removing technical obstacles and neutralising mines. Signed edit: a joint Iranian-Omani administration and a 60-day toll bar were added; IRGC media says fees resume after. Iran’s win — the text does not bar Iran from “managing” the strait; toll-free is time-boxed.
6 · $300bn development US and regional partners build a rehabilitation and development plan financing at least $300bn; the implementation mechanism is set within 60 days. External Gulf money, not a US outlay (Trump: “no money changes hands”; Reuters: no US government funds). Gulf-funded, routing the financing into the Gulf-state track.
7 · Sanctions US commits to ending all sanctions — UNSC resolutions, IAEA Board resolutions, all unilateral US primary and secondary sanctions — on a schedule set in the final agreement. Exceeds the JCPOA’s scope in principle, but the schedule is deferred to the final agreement — a commitment on record, not yet executed.
8 · Nuclear Iran reiterates it will never produce nuclear weapons; the fate of enriched material and all nuclear-related issues, including Iran’s “nuclear needs,” is “adequately addressed” in the final agreement. A restatement of Iran’s standing position, not a new concession. The version read aloud on signing adds on-site down-blending of the stockpile under IAEA supervision as the baseline method — a step beyond the draft’s deferral, but gated to the final agreement and cutting against Iran’s simultaneous hardening of its HEU tunnels. The ~440kg core stays in Iran through the window.
9 · Status quo Pending the final agreement, Iran holds the status quo on its nuclear programme and the US imposes no new sanctions and does not strengthen its forces in the region. The strategic freeze: Iran’s programme stays intact through the 60 days while US leverage is capped. The most consequential clause for the deferral read.
10 · Oil waivers US Treasury issues waivers immediately after signing for Iranian crude, petrochemicals and derivatives, and related services (banking, insurance, transport). Immediate Iranian oil re-entry — the supply signal the market is pricing. A waiver, not removal (Art 7); revocable, and capped by mine-clearance and infrastructure (Art 5).
11 · Frozen assets Frozen and restricted Iranian funds released and made fully available in line with negotiation progress, directed by the Central Bank of Iran. Sequenced to negotiation progress — Iran’s leverage-extraction path. Vance conditions release on “verified steps” on the HEU; Vahidi insists the funds are not barred from military spending.
12 · Implementation A mechanism to oversee implementation and the commitment to the final agreement. Compliance architecture with no specified membership, voting or enforcement — to be designed in 60 days.
13 · Sequencing gate Nuclear negotiations begin only after Iran receives assurances on the implementation of Articles 4, 5, 10 and 11 (blockade, Hormuz, oil waivers, frozen assets); talks then address only the remaining articles. Iran’s verification gate, now in the text — it can delay the nuclear talks by contesting any of those four implementations.
14 · UNSC ratification The final agreement is approved through a binding UN Security Council resolution. China and Russia become structural guarantors — neither the US nor Israel can unilaterally exit the final deal without a UNSC crisis. Iran’s durability anchor.
05 · Stakeholder map

Actor positions through the Stalemate / Resolution lens.

The Roaring Lion A/B/C framework is the analytical reference: Stalemate ≈ persistent Bad B; Resolution ≈ Good A via JPOA 2.0 + Hormuz Council + Security Guarantee. One chip flips this iteration — the US, from “Off-ramp realised” (v5) to “MoU signed”; the Israel, Iran and GCC cells sharpen on the signed text. All other chips carry from v5.

United States
The MoU is signed. The US and Iran signed the MoU on 17 June, two days early, a US official reading out the verbatim text; the blockade lifts and oil sanctions waive on signing. The US version of the terms — dismantle the nuclear programme, end Axis support — is far harder than Iran’s, so the signed ceasefire still sits on an unreconciled core.
MoU
signed
Israel
Citrinowicz: intent intact, capacity to disrupt constrained. Israel bombed Beirut on 14 Jun as the deal neared signing — the inverted-trigger window firing again — and Trump openly warned Netanyahu off further Beirut strikes, a visible US–Israel fault line. A “complete end to the war in Lebanon” is an explicit Iranian red line, so Israel’s Lebanon campaign is a direct obstacle to the deal. The signed Clause 1 guarantees Lebanese sovereignty and territorial integrity, which Tehran is telling Hezbollah (via Emirati media) obliges a phased Israeli withdrawal over the 60-day window. Israel is not a party to the MoU and remains in Lebanon; Beirut says it has no official guarantee of a withdrawal and has not discussed one with US officials, so the clause is contested at signature. Regime change stays off the Resolution menu; Netanyahu’s stake in a US–Iran deal failing is unchanged.
Good A/C →
regime change
Iran
Fractured — and the hardline camp is winning the fracture. Per ISW, neither the Supreme Leader nor the IRGC had approved the MOU as of 12 Jun, and IRGC commander Vahidi’s maximalist camp now dominates regime decision-making — driving the red lines of Iranian “management” of the strait and a complete end to the Lebanon war; the 60-day final agreement still has to clear that camp. Iran’s primary Hormuz objective is recognised sovereignty over the strait, with the fee scheme secondary, and the MOU concedes that — reopening under Iranian arrangements. Signed Clause 5 writes the sovereignty claim into the text: First VP Aref states the strait “belongs to Iran” and its management “will remain Iran’s responsibility,” with vessels to pay service fees for safe navigation. Fees are barred for 60 days only; after that Iran reopens fee talks with the Gulf states and keeps the post-window lever.

hardliners up

Publicly pushes Resolution (ceasefire, lanes reopened, Xi/Putin Beijing meeting), but the pivot is partly performative — PRC kept sending missile components and fuel precursors during the war. Per Pape, the war already shifted Beijing’s read of US resolve; munitions depletion (FT, Global21) reinforces it.
Pushes Resolution

Aligned with China publicly; has formally offered to take Iran’s HEU (acknowledged by Araghchi, “not currently under discussion”). Accepting HEU under the Security Guarantee architecture would be the technical pathway. Preserves Iran ties privately (drones via Caspian).
HEU custodian role available

Signed Clause 5 requires Iran to negotiate the post-60-day fee regime with Oman and the other Gulf states, who ISW judges unlikely to acquiesce without Iranian coercion — the structural objection now sits inside the signed text, deferred rather than resolved. The Saudi–Pakistan airspace cross-promise still binds Israeli unilateral action.

The map — ceasefire signed

Stalemate stays modal because the US–Iran nuclear divide is wide and the hardliner faction blocks Good A. The ceasefire is a Stage-1 instrument: it ends the shooting, lifts the blockade and reopens Hormuz, while the nuclear core, the HEU stockpile and sanctions sequencing are pushed to a 60-day track. The Hormuz sovereignty question resolves in Iran’s favour — the strait reopens toll-free but under Iranian management, a reopening Iran controls and can reverse. The signing writes Iran’s wins into the text but settles none of the deferrals: the Hormuz toll runs free for exactly 60 days before fee talks the Gulf states are unlikely to concede without coercion, the Lebanese-sovereignty clause binds an Israel that is not a party, and the nuclear language leaves the existing HEU stockpile to the same 60-day track. The unresolved question is whether those ambiguities settle inside the window; if any one does not, the constraint it defers — the strait, the Lebanon front, or the fissile core — returns as binding at the 60-day mark rather than being retired by the signing.

v7 · Implementation underway · 23 Jun 2026
Architecture & all chips held · 60-day track convened · Hormuz two-sided · IAEA readmitted · Brent ~$79

The architecture holds — Stalemate (modal) / Resolution (improving) / apex in the long-term allocation. The signed MoU has moved into implementation. The first round of the 60-day track convened in Switzerland (Bürgenstock, 21–22 Jun) — Vance for the US, Araghchi for Iran, Qatari and Pakistani mediation — and concluded on 22 Jun with a joint statement reaffirming the 60-day roadmap and standing up technical working groups. No chip moves: the deal is executing under stress, with every deferred core still open.

What moved from v6: implementation is now observable rather than prospective, and it runs two ways. Hormuz: physical flow resumed at scale — Bloomberg tracks ~20m barrels over Fri–Sun, the fastest pace since the war began (US CENTCOM ~17m Sat; ~6.66 mbd, about two-fifths of pre-war), with Iran clearing its blockaded Chabahar cargoes — even as the IRGC Navy announced a re-closure of the strait on 20 Jun, conditioned on Israel halting operations against Hezbollah. CENTCOM’s rebuttal, “Iran does not control the Strait of Hormuz,” reads the closure as leverage rather than interdiction, but Iran has now exercised the re-closure lever once. Iran agreed to readmit IAEA inspectors, the first compliance step on the otherwise-deferred nuclear file, though the ~440kg HEU core stays inside Iran and is being hardened. Brent printed ~$79 (a post-war low) as barrels return. The carried open question is unchanged in kind, and is now being tested live in Iran’s favour.

Inputs (19–23 Jun intake): ISW Iran Updates 20 & 21 Jun (Gary via Eric) · Reuters “US and Iran conclude Switzerland talks” (22 Jun) · Bloomberg “Gulf oil floods through Hormuz” (22 Jun) · David Switzerland/MoU note + Xinhua Cairo R-4 readout (22 Jun) · Geopolitical Futures “Iran War Drains US Oil Reserves” (Gary via Eric, 19 Jun) · BCA “Hormuz Relief” / Gertken (21 Jun) · WSJ / SCMP 22–23 Jun Iran clippings (IAEA readmission, frozen-asset access, renewed Lebanon ceasefire)

Yellow marks what is new or changed in v7 — the first 60-day-track round in Switzerland, the two-sided Hormuz picture (verified flow against the 20 Jun re-closure announcement), the IAEA inspector readmission, and the Brent level refresh. The scenario architecture, all actor chips, the signed-MoU text, the Resolution mechanics, the Murrin due diligence and the Brent bands carry from v6 unmarked.

Bottom line · implementation underway, settlement still deferred

The scenario set stays bifurcated: Stalemate (modal) and Resolution (improving probability), with the apex tail held in the long-term allocation below the scenario-modelling threshold. The Stage-1 ceasefire ended the war on all fronts, lifted the blockade and reopened Hormuz, while the nuclear core is deferred to a 60-day negotiation that Tehran’s own media calls a pause to rebuild. That negotiation has now begun — a first round in Switzerland reaffirmed the 60-day roadmap — but Iran is front-loading its wins and slow-walking the nuclear file: banking sanctions relief and frozen-asset release first, gating nuclear talks behind delivery of the ceasefire, blockade-lift, Hormuz and asset clauses (the MoU’s own Clause 13), and reasserting the strait as leverage by announcing a re-closure tied to the Lebanon front. Oil flows have resumed at scale regardless, and Iran agreed to readmit IAEA inspectors. Murrin’s Article 2 nuclear scenario remains the analytical foundation under structural tail protection; the apex stays re-timed to the 60-day window. Full scenario stances, drivers, equity and Brent views are in section 03.

01 · Due Diligence on Murrin

Verdict unchanged — cannot rule it out.

Capabilities corroborated (Tier 1); interpretive layers held as hypotheses (Tier 2); the policy conclusion treated as advocacy (Tier 3). The full claim-by-claim verifiability table sits in the v1 tab.

Murrin’s capabilities case is sound and OSINT-sourced. Timeline sits at the aggressive end of a 6–12 month band that overlaps Roaring Lion’s own assessment. The preemptive-nuclear-use conclusion is his advocacy. The Gallant “440 Kilograms” piece restates the ~440kg 60% HEU stockpile that anchors the capabilities case, and ISW, citing five US-intelligence sources via CNN, reports Iran is physically hardening that stockpile — collapsing tunnels and mining storage entrances to defeat any seizure — so the fissile core is intact, deferred, and being entrenched. Iran agreed this week to readmit IAEA inspectors, a near-term access concession and the first forward step on the nuclear file; it is not down-blending. The inspectors return while the ~440kg core stays inside Iran and the tunnels are entrenched, so breakout capacity is deferred, not removed. “Cannot rule out the nuclear scenario” holds as the analytical foundation; the apex is handled via the long-term allocation rather than modelled scenarios.

02 · Scenario test

Live tail risk on a 6–12 month horizon — below the scenario-modelling threshold.

The conclusion is unchanged; the operational decision is to bear this tail in the long-term allocation rather than model it.

The apex first-use event is paradigm-break, so protection is structural rather than model-derived. As David noted, “in short, I probably don’t need to know what the damage would look like.” The deal does not lower the tail — it re-times it. The MOU defers the nuclear question to a 60-day negotiation while the HEU stays inside Iran, so the breakout clock moves from the battlefield to the talks table without resetting. Iranian media frames the signing as a wartime pause that “delays the final battle” and buys time to rebuild — negotiation as a means of managing the adversary, in the IRGC paper’s words. That table is now live, and Iran is using it that way: the first 60-day-track round met in Switzerland on 21–22 Jun, but Iran sent an economics-heavy delegation with no nuclear-committee members and made opening the nuclear file conditional on the prior delivery of sanctions waivers, frozen-asset release and the Lebanon ceasefire. The breakout clock and the proxy rebuild run on the same window, funded by relief Iran is banking up front. What stays in the long-term allocation is the nightmare convergence — nuclear Iran + reinvigorated Russia + hedging China + exhausted America + isolated Israel arriving together around summer 2026 as oil buffers run dry; Murrin Article 3’s late-stage US-decline thesis sits on the “exhausted America” leg as backdrop conviction, now quantified by a US Strategic Petroleum Reserve at a historically low ~340m barrels, monitored daily at the tip-of-iceberg layer.

Verdict

Murrin Article 2 cannot be ruled out and remains a live tail risk on a 6–12 month horizon. The apex probability sits below the threshold where another modelled scenario would change the prescription. Israeli unilateral action remains the most plausible trigger — and with the 19 Jun Israel–Hezbollah ceasefire already being violated on both sides, the Lebanon front is the live channel through which that trigger could fire.

04 · The signed MoU

What the text says — the milestone, Article by Article.

The 14-point text Bloomberg published on 16 June and the version a senior US official read aloud on signing (17 Jun); the Article text below is verbatim from those primary publications, carried from v6 as the reference for the 60-day track. The flag is our reading of what each Article concedes, defers, or leaves contested; the implementation box below tracks how the immediate obligations executed in the first week.

ArticleText (close paraphrase)Our reading
1 · Ceasefire Immediate and permanent end to the war on all fronts including Lebanon; no hostile actions; refrain from the threat or use of force; the final agreement confirms this. Signed edit: a Lebanese-sovereignty guarantee was added. Contested — Iran reads it as obliging an Israeli withdrawal; Israel is not a signatory and keeps striking.
2 · Sovereignty Both parties respect each other’s sovereignty and territorial integrity and refrain from interfering in internal affairs. Implicitly constrains US regime-change; the IRGC structure survives. Iran also leans on this to frame “managing” Hormuz as an internal affair.
3 · 60-day window Negotiate and reach a final agreement within a maximum of 60 days, extendable by mutual consent. The clock for every deferred core. “Extendable” means the pause can stretch if Iran judges its leverage growing.
4 · US obligations Immediate: lift the naval blockade, prevent interference against Iran, restore traffic within 30 days to pre-war capacity proportional to Iran’s pre-war volume. US withdraws forces from surrounding areas within 30 days after the final agreement. Asymmetry: the blockade lifts at once, but force withdrawal waits on the final agreement — US forces stay through the 60-day window plus 30 days. “Proportional to Iran’s pre-war traffic” hands Iran the reopening pace.
5 · Iran obligations (Hormuz) Immediate: resume merchant shipping in the Gulf / Sea of Oman within 30 days to pre-war volume, removing technical obstacles and neutralising mines. Signed edit: a joint Iranian-Omani administration and a 60-day toll bar were added; IRGC media says fees resume after. Iran’s win — the text does not bar Iran from “managing” the strait; toll-free is time-boxed.
6 · $300bn development US and regional partners build a rehabilitation and development plan financing at least $300bn; the implementation mechanism is set within 60 days. External Gulf money, not a US outlay (Trump: “no money changes hands”; Reuters: no US government funds). Gulf-funded, routing the financing into the Gulf-state track.
7 · Sanctions US commits to ending all sanctions — UNSC resolutions, IAEA Board resolutions, all unilateral US primary and secondary sanctions — on a schedule set in the final agreement. Exceeds the JCPOA’s scope in principle, but the schedule is deferred to the final agreement — a commitment on record, not yet executed.
8 · Nuclear Iran reiterates it will never produce nuclear weapons; the fate of enriched material and all nuclear-related issues, including Iran’s “nuclear needs,” is “adequately addressed” in the final agreement. A restatement of Iran’s standing position, not a new concession. The version read aloud on signing adds on-site down-blending of the stockpile under IAEA supervision as the baseline method — gated to the final agreement and cutting against Iran’s simultaneous hardening of its HEU tunnels. The ~440kg core stays in Iran through the window.
9 · Status quo Pending the final agreement, Iran holds the status quo on its nuclear programme and the US imposes no new sanctions and does not strengthen its forces in the region. The strategic freeze: Iran’s programme stays intact through the 60 days while US leverage is capped. The most consequential clause for the deferral read.
10 · Oil waivers US Treasury issues waivers immediately after signing for Iranian crude, petrochemicals and derivatives, and related services (banking, insurance, transport). Immediate Iranian oil re-entry — the supply signal the market is pricing. A waiver, not removal (Art 7); revocable, and capped by mine-clearance and infrastructure (Art 5).
11 · Frozen assets Frozen and restricted Iranian funds released and made fully available in line with negotiation progress, directed by the Central Bank of Iran. Sequenced to negotiation progress — Iran’s leverage-extraction path. Vance conditions release on “verified steps” on the HEU; Vahidi insists the funds are not barred from military spending.
12 · Implementation A mechanism to oversee implementation and the commitment to the final agreement. Compliance architecture with no specified membership, voting or enforcement — to be designed in 60 days.
13 · Sequencing gate Nuclear negotiations begin only after Iran receives assurances on the implementation of Articles 4, 5, 10 and 11 (blockade, Hormuz, oil waivers, frozen assets); talks then address only the remaining articles. Iran’s verification gate, now in the text — it can delay the nuclear talks by contesting any of those four implementations.
14 · UNSC ratification The final agreement is approved through a binding UN Security Council resolution. China and Russia become structural guarantors — neither the US nor Israel can unilaterally exit the final deal without a UNSC crisis. Iran’s durability anchor.
Implementation — week 1 · v7

The first week of execution runs two ways. Hormuz (Art 4/5): oil is flowing at the fastest pace since the war — Bloomberg ~20m barrels Fri–Sun, CENTCOM ~17m Sat, Chabahar cargoes clearing — yet the IRGC Navy announced a re-closure on 20 Jun conditioned on Israel halting Lebanon operations; CENTCOM rebuts that traffic still flows (“Iran does not control the strait”), and a one-day Kpler dip to 5 vessels (Sun) from 26 (Sat) shows the lever is real but, so far, unexercised in practice. Nuclear (Art 8/9): Iran agreed to readmit IAEA inspectors; the ~440kg HEU stays in Iran. Sequencing (Art 13): Iran is gating the nuclear file behind delivery of the ceasefire, blockade-lift, Hormuz, oil-waiver and frozen-asset clauses, and is banking ~$12bn of relief up front — a $500m “test purchase,” with ~$6bn held in Qatar — to cut US leverage. Lebanon (Art 1): now Iran’s declared top priority, read as obliging an Israeli withdrawal, with the 19 Jun Israel–Hezbollah ceasefire already violated on both sides.

05 · Stakeholder map

Actor positions through the Stalemate / Resolution lens.

The Roaring Lion A/B/C framework is the analytical reference: Stalemate ≈ persistent Bad B; Resolution ≈ Good A via JPOA 2.0 + Hormuz Council + Security Guarantee. No chip flips this iteration — the signed-MoU state carries from v6; the US, Israel, Iran and GCC cells sharpen on the first implementation week.

United States
The MoU is signed; implementation is under conditional pressure. The US version of the terms — dismantle the nuclear programme, end Axis support — is far harder than Iran’s, so the signed ceasefire still sits on an unreconciled core. Vance led the first 60-day-track round in Switzerland; Trump warned Iran “you won’t have a country” if it re-closes the strait, floated a US takeover of the waterway with a US-charged toll, and threatened to “hit Iran very hard again… only harder” if Hezbollah is not reined in. Conditional rhetoric, no new strike — the chip holds.
MoU
signed
Israel
Citrinowicz: intent intact, capacity to disrupt constrained. A “complete end to the war in Lebanon” is an explicit Iranian red line, so Israel’s Lebanon campaign is a direct obstacle to the deal; the signed Clause 1 guarantees Lebanese sovereignty, which Tehran tells Hezbollah obliges a phased Israeli withdrawal, while Israel is not a party and remains in southern Lebanon. The 19 Jun Israel–Hezbollah ceasefire is already failing: the IDF struck 300+ Hezbollah targets and killed 100+ fighters over 19–20 Jun in response to Hezbollah fire (147 rockets, 20 drones, 9 ATGMs), framed as enforcement; Netanyahu keeps the IDF in its security zone “as long as necessary.” The inverted-trigger window is flickering again on the Lebanon front — a notch hotter than the 14 Jun Beirut strike, but no unilateral strike on Iran proper. Regime change stays off the Resolution menu; Netanyahu’s stake in a US–Iran deal failing is unchanged.
Good A/C →
regime change
Iran
Fractured — and the hardline camp is winning the fracture. Iran’s primary Hormuz objective is recognised sovereignty over the strait; signed Clause 5 writes that claim into the text, with fees barred for 60 days only and reopened with the Gulf states after. This week Iran reasserted the strait as leverage — an IRGC-Navy re-closure announcement (20 Jun) tied to Israel halting Lebanon operations — and made the Lebanon ceasefire its declared top priority, reinterpreting the sovereignty clause to demand an Israeli withdrawal. The factional split is now overt: IRGC-aligned Fars attacks pro-talks voices and reports the Supreme Leader is “not fully” behind the MoU, and Tasnim says Araghchi had “no justification” to attend; the delegation slow-walks the nuclear file behind Clause 13. The 60-day final agreement still has to clear that camp.

Per Pape, the war already shifted Beijing’s read of US resolve; munitions depletion (FT, Global21) reinforces it.

Aligned with China publicly; has formally offered to take Iran’s HEU (acknowledged by Araghchi, “not currently under discussion”). Accepting HEU under the Security Guarantee architecture would be the technical pathway. Preserves Iran ties privately (drones via Caspian).

Mediation delivered the ceasefire — Pakistan’s PM announced the deal first, Qatar brokered a reported $12bn financial channel, and the UAE reopened a direct security line to Tehran. Seen in action again: the 21–22 Jun Bürgenstock round was quadrilateral, with Qatar and Pakistan mediating and issuing the joint statement on the 60-day roadmap. All GCC bar Oman reject permanent Iranian control of Hormuz; signed Clause 5 requires Iran to negotiate the post-60-day fee regime with Oman and the other Gulf states, who ISW judges unlikely to acquiesce without Iranian coercion — the objection sits inside the text, deferred. The Saudi–Pakistan airspace cross-promise still binds Israeli unilateral action.
Active mediators

Aligns with the diplomatic track; refused the US request to reopen the Strait, so it is constrained from leading any post-war architecture. A NATO contribution to the HEU Security Guarantee would be the test. Switzerland hosted the formal ceremony and the first implementation round; the post-war security architecture would sit with other parties.
Diplomatic backer

Lean on the US for Resolution; ~60% of energy via Hormuz. The US warned Japan of up-to-two-year Tomahawk delays as the Pentagon prioritises Middle East restocking — US overstretch landing on an Indo-Pacific ally, reinforcing the hedge. Dormant nuclear conversations re-open if the scenario shifts to apex.
Hedging

The 8–22 Jun: whipsaw, signing, and implementation

8–15 Jun
Ceasefire collapses then resolves onto de-escalation: Israeli strikes inside Iran (8 Jun) and US strikes on ~20 targets (9–10 Jun); Trump scraps a third strike-day and declares the deal “close,” then “complete” (15 Jun); blockade lifts, war ends on all fronts including Lebanon. Brent falls toward $84.
17 Jun
The US and Iran sign the MoU two days early; a senior US official reads the text aloud. Signed edits to Clause 1 (Lebanon) and Clause 5 (Hormuz: joint Iranian-Omani administration, fees barred for 60 days). The blockade lifts and oil sanctions waive on signing.
19 Jun
A separate Israel–Hezbollah ceasefire is announced — and is violated on both sides within the day (Hezbollah fires 147 rockets, 20 drones and 9 ATGMs, killing one IDF soldier; the IDF strikes back at scale).
20 Jun
Iran’s IRGC Navy announces a re-closure of the Strait of Hormuz, conditioned on Israel halting Lebanon operations; CENTCOM rebuts — “Iran does not control the strait… traffic continues to flow.” Implementation talks open in Switzerland with an economics-heavy Iranian delegation (Central Bank Governor Hemmati, NIOC chair); IAEA DG Grossi attends.
21 Jun
Quadrilateral talks at Bürgenstock (Vance–Araghchi, Qatar/Pakistan mediating). A wobble after Trump’s “harder” threat — Iran’s team reportedly withdraws “in protest” but, per Axios, never leaves the venue and keeps negotiating; 16 vessels transit Hormuz via the Omani route.
22 Jun
The first round concludes; a Qatar–Pakistan joint statement reaffirms the 60-day roadmap and stands up technical working groups; Iran reports “major progress.” Bloomberg tracks Hormuz flow at the fastest pace since the war. Brent falls to ~$79, a post-war low.
What it confirms: the off-ramp produced a signed ceasefire that is now executing, on an unreconciled core. What it does not change: the bifurcated architecture, the apex held in the long-term allocation, the Murrin DD, the actor chips. Watch-list: the signing trigger fired (v6); this week added a Lebanon-front exchange under a failing ceasefire and Iran’s first use of the Hormuz re-closure lever (assessed as leverage, flow continuing). The live dates are the 60-day track’s two clocks — Hormuz fee talks and the nuclear file — and the standing Israeli-strike trigger; the mediation-breakdown trigger has not fired, the 60-day roadmap reaffirmed.
The map — implementation underway

Stalemate stays modal because the US–Iran nuclear divide is wide and the hardliner faction blocks Good A. The ceasefire is a Stage-1 instrument: it ends the shooting, lifts the blockade and reopens Hormuz, while the nuclear core, the HEU stockpile and sanctions sequencing are pushed to a 60-day track. The first implementation week is the carried question’s first real test, and it is running in Iran’s favour: Iran banked its front-loaded wins, made the Lebanon ceasefire and the strait its leverage, and gated the nuclear file behind delivery of the relief clauses, while oil flowed regardless. The unresolved question is whether the deferred cores — the nuclear file, the Hormuz fee regime, the Lebanon front — settle inside the 60-day window or return as binding at its end; nothing this week resolved them, and the sequencing now runs on Iran’s order.

Scenario views across equities, Brent, rates, and rotation.

Views only. Brent bands and the scenario architecture are unchanged from v6; the market sits at a post-war low as barrels return, against a thin buffer and a live re-closure lever.
