The $300bn in the signed terms is external Gulf investment funded by sanctions relief — for instance a UAE-built power plant in Iran — not a US Treasury outlay, which reconciles the terms with Iran’s frozen-asset demand; the relief flows through GCC capital.

Resolution mechanics

The Resolution path follows the framework David specified: Mearsheimer’s “humble pie” off-ramp; a JPOA 2.0 nuclear architecture; the Ferguson Hormuz Council (Iran as Chair, a 45% / 30% / balance revenue split, transit fees at cost); HEU export under a Security Guarantee with Russia available as custodian; and a post-war Gulf Treaty Organisation with China and Russia as signatories. The realised Stage-1 MOU diverges from the Council mechanic on Hormuz: rather than a chaired revenue split with fees at cost, the strait reopens toll-free but under Iranian and Omani management — Iran’s sovereignty claim conceded operationally, the revenue-sharing design bypassed, with fees barred for 60 days then talks with the Gulf states. The deeper cores the framework was built to settle — HEU ~440kg, enrichment, sanctions sequencing — remain for the 60-day track. The first round (Switzerland, 21–22 Jun) stood up technical working groups on those cores but opened none of them: Iran gated the nuclear file behind delivery of the relief clauses.

v8 · First armed exchange, then stand-down · 30 Jun 2026
Architecture & bands held · US/Iran chips moved on the exchange · stand-down + 30 Jun Doha track (disputed) · market contained · Brent ~$73

The architecture holds — Stalemate (modal) / Resolution (improving) / apex in the long-term allocation. The signed MoU stayed intact on paper through its first kinetic test: between 25 and 28 June the US and Iran traded strikes for the first time since the 19 Jun signing. The deal framework held — both sides signalled restraint and the 60-day talks continue — but implementation turned violent, and two chips move on it.

What moved from v7: an Iranian IRGC drone struck the Singapore-flagged Ever Lovely in the Gulf of Oman (25 Jun); US CENTCOM struck Iranian missile, drone-storage and coastal-radar sites in response (26–27 Jun); and Iran fired drones and ballistic missiles at the US Fifth Fleet base in Bahrain and the Ali Al Salem airbase in Kuwait (28 Jun). The condition the v7 US chip rested on — “conditional rhetoric, no new strike” — is gone, so the US and Iran cells move. The market read the violation as contained: Brent sits back at the pre-war anchor (~$72.60 close, $73.36 intraday), WTI near $70, with equity and bond volatility below pre-war levels — a real kinetic re-escalation priced as a contained incident. By 29–30 Jun the exchange de-escalated: both sides agreed to stand down, and a Doha implementation track is set for 30 Jun — though its status is disputed, the US calling it talks Iran requested and Iran calling it MoU-implementation follow-up. The chips that moved on the exchange hold; the de-escalation confirms the framework-held read. The architecture, the verdict, the Brent bands and the apex placement are unchanged.

Bottom line · the MoU’s first kinetic test, contained — then a stand-down

The scenario set stays bifurcated: Stalemate (modal) and Resolution (improving probability), with the apex tail held in the long-term allocation below the scenario-modelling threshold. The Stage-1 ceasefire ended the war on all fronts, lifted the blockade and reopened Hormuz, while the nuclear core is deferred to a 60-day negotiation. That implementation has now turned kinetic without breaking the frame: a ship strike, US retaliatory strikes on Iranian military sites, and Iranian drone and missile strikes on US bases in Bahrain and Kuwait, all between 25 and 28 Jun — the first armed exchange since the signing. Oil returned to its pre-war anchor through it and volatility stayed below pre-war, so the market is treating the violation as a contained incident. Iran is using force to assert control of the strait, with Araghchi claiming Iran is “solely responsible” for Hormuz under the MoU, while the toll question and the IAEA-inspection dispute remain open. By 29–30 Jun the exchange gave way to a stand-down and a disputed 30 Jun Doha implementation track, where Iran will press the frozen-asset and sequencing clauses; the open Omani southern route is meanwhile eroding Iran’s strait leverage. 02 · Scenario test

The 25–28 Jun armed exchange is the test case for kinetic escalation short of first-use — a ship strike, US retaliation on Iranian military sites, and Iranian strikes on US bases in Bahrain and Kuwait, all conventional, with the apex below threshold throughout. It sits in Stalemate-stress, below the apex. What stays in the long-term allocation is the nightmare convergence — nuclear Iran + reinvigorated Russia + hedging China + exhausted America + isolated Israel arriving together around summer 2026 as oil buffers run dry; Murrin Article 3’s late-stage US-decline thesis sits on the “exhausted America” leg as backdrop conviction, with a US Strategic Petroleum Reserve at a historically low ~325.7m barrels by 29 Jun, the lowest since 1983, monitored daily at the tip-of-iceberg layer.

Verdict

Murrin Article 2 cannot be ruled out and remains a live tail risk on a 6–12 month horizon. The apex probability sits below the threshold where another modelled scenario would change the prescription. Israeli unilateral action remains the most plausible trigger — and the first armed US-Iran exchange under the MoU now shows the deal can turn kinetic through Hormuz directly; the strait and the Lebanon front are both live channels for an escalation that the long-term allocation is built to absorb.

Per the Eric/team binding decision (22 May): two scenarios modelled, plus the BAU baseline. The apex first-use scenario is held in the long-term allocation. Architecture unchanged in v8.

04 · The signed MoU

What the text says — the milestone, Article by Article.

The 14-point text Bloomberg published on 16 June and the version a senior US official read aloud on signing (17 Jun); the Article text below is verbatim from those primary publications, carried as the reference for the 60-day track. The flag is our reading of what each Article concedes, defers, or leaves contested; the implementation boxes below track how the obligations have executed.

ArticleText (close paraphrase)Our reading
1 · Ceasefire Immediate and permanent end to the war on all fronts including Lebanon; no hostile actions; refrain from the threat or use of force; the final agreement confirms this. Signed edit: a Lebanese-sovereignty guarantee was added. Contested — Iran reads it as obliging an Israeli withdrawal; Israel is not a signatory and keeps striking.
2 · Sovereignty Both parties respect each other’s sovereignty and territorial integrity and refrain from interfering in internal affairs. Implicitly constrains US regime-change; the IRGC structure survives. Iran also leans on this to frame “managing” Hormuz as an internal affair.
3 · 60-day window Negotiate and reach a final agreement within a maximum of 60 days, extendable by mutual consent. The clock for every deferred core. “Extendable” means the pause can stretch if Iran judges its leverage growing.
4 · US obligations Immediate: lift the naval blockade, prevent interference against Iran, restore traffic within 30 days to pre-war capacity proportional to Iran’s pre-war volume. US withdraws forces from surrounding areas within 30 days after the final agreement. Asymmetry: the blockade lifts at once, but force withdrawal waits on the final agreement — US forces stay through the 60-day window plus 30 days. “Proportional to Iran’s pre-war traffic” hands Iran the reopening pace.
5 · Iran obligations (Hormuz) Immediate: resume merchant shipping in the Gulf / Sea of Oman within 30 days to pre-war volume, removing technical obstacles and neutralising mines. Signed edit: a joint Iranian-Omani administration and a 60-day toll bar were added; IRGC media says fees resume after. Iran’s win — the text does not bar Iran from “managing” the strait; toll-free is time-boxed.
6 · $300bn development US and regional partners build a rehabilitation and development plan financing at least $300bn; the implementation mechanism is set within 60 days. External Gulf money, not a US outlay (Trump: “no money changes hands”; Reuters: no US government funds). Gulf-funded, routing the financing into the Gulf-state track.
7 · Sanctions US commits to ending all sanctions — UNSC resolutions, IAEA Board resolutions, all unilateral US primary and secondary sanctions — on a schedule set in the final agreement. Exceeds the JCPOA’s scope in principle, but the schedule is deferred to the final agreement — a commitment on record, not yet executed.
8 · Nuclear Iran reiterates it will never produce nuclear weapons; the fate of enriched material and all nuclear-related issues, including Iran’s “nuclear needs,” is “adequately addressed” in the final agreement. A restatement of Iran’s standing position, not a new concession. The version read aloud on signing adds on-site down-blending of the stockpile under IAEA supervision as the baseline method — gated to the final agreement and cutting against Iran’s simultaneous hardening of its HEU tunnels. The ~440kg core stays in Iran through the window.
9 · Status quo Pending the final agreement, Iran holds the status quo on its nuclear programme and the US imposes no new sanctions and does not strengthen its forces in the region. The strategic freeze: Iran’s programme stays intact through the 60 days while US leverage is capped. The most consequential clause for the deferral read.
10 · Oil waivers US Treasury issues waivers immediately after signing for Iranian crude, petrochemicals and derivatives, and related services (banking, insurance, transport). Immediate Iranian oil re-entry — the supply signal the market is pricing. A waiver, not removal (Art 7); revocable, and capped by mine-clearance and infrastructure (Art 5).
11 · Frozen assets Frozen and restricted Iranian funds released and made fully available in line with negotiation progress, directed by the Central Bank of Iran. Sequenced to negotiation progress — Iran’s leverage-extraction path. Vance conditions release on “verified steps” on the HEU; Vahidi insists the funds are not barred from military spending.
12 · Implementation A mechanism to oversee implementation and the commitment to the final agreement. Compliance architecture with no specified membership, voting or enforcement — to be designed in 60 days.
13 · Sequencing gate Nuclear negotiations begin only after Iran receives assurances on the implementation of Articles 4, 5, 10 and 11 (blockade, Hormuz, oil waivers, frozen assets); talks then address only the remaining articles. Iran’s verification gate, now in the text — it can delay the nuclear talks by contesting any of those four implementations.
14 · UNSC ratification The final agreement is approved through a binding UN Security Council resolution. China and Russia become structural guarantors — neither the US nor Israel can unilaterally exit the final deal without a UNSC crisis. Iran’s durability anchor.
Implementation — week 1

The first week of execution ran two ways. Hormuz (Art 4/5): oil flowed at the fastest pace since the war — Bloomberg ~20m barrels Fri–Sun, CENTCOM ~17m Sat, Chabahar cargoes clearing — yet the IRGC Navy announced a re-closure on 20 Jun conditioned on Israel halting Lebanon operations; CENTCOM rebutted that traffic still flows. Nuclear (Art 8/9): Iran agreed to readmit IAEA inspectors; the ~440kg HEU stays in Iran. Sequencing (Art 13): Iran gated the nuclear file behind delivery of the ceasefire, blockade-lift, Hormuz, oil-waiver and frozen-asset clauses, banking ~$12bn of relief up front. Lebanon (Art 1): now Iran’s declared top priority, with the 19 Jun Israel–Hezbollah ceasefire already violated on both sides.

Implementation — week 2 · v8

Week 2 turned kinetic. Hormuz (Art 4/5): after crossings surged to a post-war high (~73 on Wed, ~125 on the week per Lloyd’s), an Iranian drone struck the Ever Lovely (25 Jun); the IMO paused its ~600-ship evacuation and Friday traffic halved as Iran’s maritime authority warned that transit outside its designated routes is not covered by safe passage. US–Iran exchange: CENTCOM struck Iranian missile, drone-storage and radar sites in response (26–27 Jun); Iran hit US bases in Bahrain and Kuwait (28 Jun). Sovereignty (Art 5): Araghchi asserts Iran is “solely responsible” for the strait, while the Article text mandates dialogue with Oman and the Gulf littorals; Oman signals service fees and the US and GCC reject tolls — the post-60-day fee regime is being contested early. Nuclear (Art 8/9): the IAEA-inspection dispute is unresolved and Iran has signalled it could suspend talks.

05 · Stakeholder map

Actor positions through the Stalemate / Resolution lens.

The Roaring Lion A/B/C framework is the analytical reference: Stalemate ≈ persistent Bad B; Resolution ≈ Good A via JPOA 2.0 + Hormuz Council + Security Guarantee. The US and Iran cells move on the first armed exchange; the other chips hold, with the Israel cell sharpening on the new Lebanon framework.

United States
The MoU holds, but implementation turned kinetic. The US version of the terms — dismantle the nuclear programme, end Axis support — is far harder than Iran’s, so the signed ceasefire still sits on an unreconciled core. Vance led the first 60-day-track round in Switzerland; Trump warned Iran “you won’t have a country” if it re-closes the strait, floated a US takeover of the waterway with a US-charged toll, and threatened to “hit Iran very hard again” if Hezbollah is not reined in. That threat was carried out in kind: US CENTCOM struck Iranian missile, drone-storage and coastal-radar sites on 26–27 Jun in response to the Ever Lovely attack — “a powerful response” — and is providing “safe passage coordination” for shipping. The no-new-strike condition the v7 chip rested on is gone; Washington signalled no return to sustained war, so the off-ramp framing survives the exchange. By 29–30 Jun both sides agreed to stand down, and Trump says a US–Iran meeting runs in Doha on 30 Jun with Witkoff and Kushner attending — though he calls it “perhaps important, perhaps not” and keeps the stated goal at denuclearisation.
MoU holds ·
struck Iran
Israel
Citrinowicz: intent intact, capacity to disrupt constrained. A “complete end to the war in Lebanon” is an explicit Iranian red line, so Israel’s Lebanon campaign is a direct obstacle to the deal; the signed Clause 1 guarantees Lebanese sovereignty, which Tehran tells Hezbollah obliges a phased Israeli withdrawal, while Israel is not a party and remains in southern Lebanon. The 19 Jun Israel–Hezbollah ceasefire failed on both sides within the day, and the IDF kept striking. A trilateral Israel–Lebanon–US framework followed on 27 Jun: Israel keeps a security zone along the yellow line and withdraws from two pilot zones for Lebanese-army-led Hezbollah disarmament, while Hezbollah rejects the terms. This is a de-escalation step on the Lebanon front, and it does not move the spoiler read; there is no unilateral strike on Iran proper. Regime change stays off the Resolution menu; Netanyahu’s stake in a US–Iran deal failing is unchanged.
Good A/C →
regime change
Iran
Fractured — and the hardline camp is winning the fracture. Iran’s primary Hormuz objective is recognised sovereignty over the strait; signed Clause 5 writes that claim into the text, with fees barred for 60 days only and reopened with the Gulf states after. The factional split is overt, the delegation slow-walks the nuclear file behind Clause 13, and IRGC-aligned media reports the Supreme Leader is “not fully” behind the MoU. This week Iran moved from announcing the re-closure lever to using force: two ship strikes to push vessels onto its own approved traffic scheme, then drone and ballistic-missile strikes on US bases in Bahrain and Kuwait after the US retaliation, warning Gulf states against hosting US attacks. Araghchi claims Iran is “solely responsible” for the strait under Article 5. Ahead of the Doha track Iran doubles down — Gharibabadi says it will designate transit routes and act without Oman if needed — while framing the trip as implementation follow-up (frozen assets, Clause 13) rather than final-agreement talks. The leverage is checked, though: the open Omani southern route is drawing traffic away from Iran’s scheme.
Hardliners up ·
using force
China
Publicly pushes Resolution (ceasefire, lanes reopened, Xi/Putin Beijing meeting), but the pivot is partly performative — PRC kept sending missile components and fuel precursors during the war. Per Pape, the war already shifted Beijing’s read of US resolve; munitions depletion (FT, Global21) reinforces it.
Pushes Resolution
Russia
Aligned with China publicly; has formally offered to take Iran’s HEU (acknowledged by Araghchi, “not currently under discussion”). Accepting HEU under the Security Guarantee architecture would be the technical pathway. Preserves Iran ties privately (drones via Caspian).
HEU custodian role available
GCC / Pakistan
Mediation delivered the ceasefire — Pakistan’s PM announced the deal first, Qatar brokered a reported $12bn financial channel, and the UAE reopened a direct security line to Tehran; the 21–22 Jun Bürgenstock round was quadrilateral, with Qatar and Pakistan mediating. All GCC bar Oman reject permanent Iranian control of Hormuz; signed Clause 5 requires Iran to negotiate the post-60-day fee regime with Oman and the other Gulf states, who ISW judges unlikely to acquiesce without Iranian coercion. The US and GCC jointly rejected any Iranian tolls or strait control on 25 Jun; Iran’s 28 Jun strikes on Bahrain read as coercion of the Gulf hosts, and Oman has told European officials transiting ships may face service fees — the fee question is opening under pressure. By 30 Jun Oman, meeting Macron in Paris, reaffirmed freedom of navigation “without conditions,” resisting Iranian-imposed tolls, and the open Omani route keeps drawing traffic — capping Iran’s ability to dictate terms. The Saudi–Pakistan airspace cross-promise still binds Israeli unilateral action.
Active mediators
Europe
Aligns with the diplomatic track; refused the US request to reopen the Strait, so it is constrained from leading any post-war architecture. A NATO contribution to the HEU Security Guarantee would be the test. Switzerland hosted the formal ceremony and the first implementation round; the post-war security architecture would sit with other parties.
Diplomatic backer
Japan / Korea
Lean on the US for Resolution; ~60% of energy via Hormuz. The US warned Japan of up-to-two-year Tomahawk delays as the Pentagon prioritises Middle East restocking — US overstretch landing on an Indo-Pacific ally, reinforcing the hedge. Dormant nuclear conversations re-open if the scenario shifts to apex.
Hedging

The 8 Jun – 30 Jun: whipsaw, signing, and the first armed exchange, then stand-down

8–17 Jun
Ceasefire collapses then resolves onto de-escalation: Israeli and US strikes inside Iran (8–10 Jun); Trump declares the deal “complete” (15 Jun); the US and Iran sign the MoU two days early (17 Jun), with signed edits to Clause 1 (Lebanon) and Clause 5 (Hormuz: joint Iranian-Omani administration, fees barred for 60 days). The blockade lifts and oil sanctions waive on signing.
19–22 Jun
A separate Israel–Hezbollah ceasefire is announced and violated within the day; Iran’s IRGC Navy announces a re-closure of the strait tied to Lebanon, which CENTCOM rebuts as flow continues. The first 60-day-track round meets at Bürgenstock (Vance–Araghchi, Qatar/Pakistan mediating) and reaffirms the roadmap; Bloomberg tracks Hormuz flow at the fastest pace since the war; Brent falls to ~$79.
24–25 Jun
Oil falls to its pre-war anchor as barrels return — WTI below $70, Brent toward $73 — and the IMO and Oman stand up a safe-route mechanism to move ~600 stranded ships along the Omani coast. On 25 Jun an Iranian IRGC drone strikes the Singapore-flagged Ever Lovely in the Gulf of Oman; Iran demands vessels use its approved traffic scheme, and Trump calls the attack a “foolish violation” of the ceasefire — the first vessel strike under the MoU.
26 Jun
US CENTCOM strikes Iranian missile, drone-storage and coastal-radar sites in response. The IMO pauses its evacuation and Friday traffic halves. Oman tells European officials transiting ships may face fees, and the US and GCC reject any Iranian tolls or strait control.
27 Jun
Iran’s first drones reach US positions in Bahrain — one intercepted, one down in a remote area — and a second tanker is hit off Oman. Israel, Lebanon and the US reach a trilateral framework to end the Lebanon conflict — Israel keeps a security zone and withdraws from two pilot zones — which Hezbollah rejects.
28 Jun
Iran escalates to drone and ballistic-missile strikes on the US Fifth Fleet base in Bahrain and the Ali Al Salem airbase in Kuwait; Bahraini air defences intercept several, though one strike destroys a residential building. Araghchi asserts Iran is “solely responsible” for the strait under the MoU.
29 Jun
Both sides agree to stand down after the weekend strikes; Rubio briefs Congress on the tentative agreement. Trump swings from a Saturday threat to “militarily complete the job” to announcing a Doha meeting he says Iran requested.
30 Jun
A US–Iran track opens in Doha — Witkoff and Kushner attend — though Iran calls it MoU-implementation follow-up (frozen assets, Clause 13), not a negotiation. Gharibabadi reasserts Iranian control of Hormuz while the open Omani route erodes the leverage; the US SPR prints 325.7m barrels, the lowest since 1983.
What it confirms: the off-ramp produced a signed ceasefire that is executing, on an unreconciled core. What it does not change: the bifurcated architecture, the apex held in the long-term allocation, the Murrin DD, the Brent bands. Watch-list: a confirmed-strike escalation fired this week — US–Iran rather than the standing Israeli-strike trigger — with strikes both ways and Iranian fire on US bases in Bahrain and Kuwait. The mediation-breakdown trigger has not fired: the 60-day talks continue and both sides signalled restraint. The live channels are the contested Hormuz governance and toll regime, the IAEA dispute, and the Lebanon front. By 29–30 Jun the exchange de-escalated into a stand-down and a disputed Doha implementation track — the whipsaw’s next swing, which confirms the framework-held read without settling the cores.
The map — first kinetic test

Stalemate stays modal because the US–Iran nuclear divide is wide and the hardliner faction blocks Good A. The ceasefire is a Stage-1 instrument: it ends the shooting, lifts the blockade and reopens Hormuz, while the nuclear core, the HEU stockpile and sanctions sequencing are pushed to a 60-day track. The first armed exchange under the MoU is the sharpest test yet of the carried question, and the read so far is mixed: the deal turned kinetic but did not break, while the market priced the violation as a contained incident. The unresolved question is whether the deferred cores — the nuclear file, the Hormuz fee regime, the Lebanon front — settle inside the 60-day window or return as binding at its end; the exchange moved none of them toward settlement, and Iran is using force to set the terms of the strait. The 29–30 Jun stand-down and the Doha track are the de-escalation swing, and they leave the cores unsettled — Iran frames the talks as MoU-implementation follow-up, with final-agreement negotiation still gated behind Clause 13.

06 · Market & thematic implications

Scenario views across equities, Brent, rates, and rotation.

Views only. Brent bands and the scenario architecture are unchanged from v7; the market sits at the pre-war anchor and priced the armed exchange as a contained incident.

ScenarioEquitiesBrentRatesSector & thematic rotation
Stalemate
Modal		 Mid-teens downside; EPS holds until ~2027 demand destruction. Tight, elevated, drawing inventory. Per Carlyle, the move down is destocking-driven — US SPR near its operational floor, Cushing near the settlement floor — not a fundamentals rebalance, and fragile to any reopening slippage. By 29 Jun Brent is back at the pre-war anchor (~$72.60 close, $73.36 intraday) and the armed exchange added only a ~$1 risk bid, with equity and bond volatility below pre-war. The buffer against a re-closure is still thin — the SPR at ~325.7m barrels by 29 Jun, the lowest since 1983 — so the market’s own re-closure tail (a JPMorgan-cited ~$150 shock on a strait closure) sits on the other side of a pre-war print. Fiscal pressure lifts yields; energy tightening Jul–Sep adds to the curve. Iran-axis exposure screens unattractive on the retaliation vectors (UAE data centres, regional fintech, Gulf cable-dependent payments). China sleeve re-rates up on the Beijing read of US weakness; munitions depletion (FT, Global21) is the observable marker. Energy-paradigm shift favours rare earths, security and infrastructure; defensive on regional refining. A non-oil vector: Iran is extending the transit-control playbook to the Europe–Asia undersea cables — IRGC-linked demands for transit fees on the hyperscalers and veiled threats to the fibre, reaching toward SWIFT. Low feasibility, but it sharpens the cable-dependent-payments screen.

Improving		 Mid-single-digit upside. Declines toward $90–110 over months; market below the band — ~$84 on the ceasefire, ~$73 by 29 Jun at the pre-war anchor — pricing a clean reopening the substance does not yet support; trust rebuild takes quarters. Yields ease as the energy premium unwinds. Iran-axis exposure screens better selectively; the energy/renewables opportunity set persists on the structural energy-paradigm shift; the China sleeve recalibrates on a firmer Hormuz-access outcome.

Apex / tail: escalation toward $130–160+ Brent is held in the long-term allocation; first-use ($200+, SPX ~3,500) sits below the scenario-modelling threshold as structural tail protection. The deal re-times this tail to the 60-day nuclear window rather than removing it — the HEU stays in Iran and is being hardened. The re-closure scenario the market is now pricing as a ~$150 shock is the near-term, non-nuclear face of this escalation leg. The Taiwan-war and US-default extrapolations are held as backdrop only, outside the modelled set.

The $300bn in the signed terms is external Gulf investment funded by sanctions relief — for instance a UAE-built power plant in Iran — not a US Treasury outlay, which reconciles the terms with Iran’s frozen-asset demand; the relief flows through GCC capital.

Resolution mechanics

The Resolution path follows the framework David specified: Mearsheimer’s “humble pie” off-ramp; a JPOA 2.0 nuclear architecture; the Ferguson Hormuz Council (Iran as Chair, a 45% / 30% / balance revenue split, transit fees at cost); HEU export under a Security Guarantee with Russia available as custodian; and a post-war Gulf Treaty Organisation with China and Russia as signatories. The realised Stage-1 MOU diverges from the Council mechanic on Hormuz: rather than a chaired revenue split with fees at cost, the strait reopens toll-free but under Iranian and Omani management — Iran’s sovereignty claim conceded operationally, the revenue-sharing design bypassed, with fees barred for 60 days then talks with the Gulf states. The deeper cores the framework was built to settle — HEU ~440kg, enrichment, sanctions sequencing — remain for the 60-day track. The Hormuz governance the framework was built to settle is now being contested by force: Iran is asserting Article-5 “sole responsibility” and striking vessels that use the Omani route, Oman signals service fees, and the US and GCC reject tolls — the fee-regime negotiation the MoU deferred is opening under fire.

v9 · Second armed exchange, oil-waiver revoked · 8 Jul 2026
Architecture & bands held · US chip flipped · Hormuz threat level raised to severe · oil-export waiver revoked · Trump declares MoU “over” · Doha track failed

The architecture holds — Stalemate (modal) / Resolution (improving) / apex in the long-term allocation. A second, larger armed exchange has landed since the late-June test: between 6 and 8 July Iran missiled a Qatari gas carrier and a Saudi tanker, then droned a third vessel — the largest single-day volume of Hormuz attacks since the deal — and the US responded with fresh airstrikes on Iran plus revocation of the waiver that has let Iran sell oil internationally since the ceasefire. One chip moves on it.

What moved from v8: the US chip, from “MoU holds · struck Iran” to “Struck again · declares MoU ‘over.’” The waiver revocation is a structural rollback: it withdraws a core MoU economic term for the first time since signing, with Iran given until 17 July to wind down transactions under a license that had run through 21 August. JMIC raised the regional threat level from substantial to severe. Iran vows “decisive actions” in response; talks are separately paused for the late Supreme Leader Khamenei’s funeral (burial 9 Jul, Mashhad). The Doha track, disputed in status since 30 Jun, concluded with no sign of headway, and at the NATO summit in Ankara Trump went further than any prior statement this cycle: “I think it’s over. I don’t want to deal with them… they’re scum… a waste of time dealing with them.” Markets read the escalation as more serious than June’s: US indices fell (Dow -0.3%, S&P -0.5%, Nasdaq -1.2%), oil rose more than 3% intraday on the tanker attacks alone before the waiver news added a further leg, and European gas futures added roughly 7%; Asian markets were mixed (Hang Seng higher on financials, Topix and ASX lower on the crude jump). No settled Brent print has reached this desk yet — direction is up from the ~$73 pre-war anchor v8 carried. The architecture, the verdict, the Brent bands and the apex placement are unchanged; Trump’s remark is one leader’s statement, without a reciprocal declaration from Tehran, and the pattern through this file has repeatedly been hardline rhetoric that later gave way to continued contact.

Inputs (30 Jun–8 Jul intake): ISW Iran Updates 6–7 Jul (Gary via Eric) — Iran’s missile/drone attacks on non-compliant tankers, the UAE’s rejected IMO strait-management proposal, Gulf bypass-pipeline plans, the NATO Ankara meeting · Bloomberg, “US Strikes Iran and Blocks Oil Sales in New Threats to Ceasefire” and “Hormuz Sees Biggest Day of Attacks Since US-Iran Peace Deal” (8 Jul, via Eric) · Morning Report market-reaction data (8 Jul, LSP/XSZ) · Reuters, “Trump says interim accord with Iran to end war is ‘over’” (8 Jul, via Xie Shangzhao/PMST)

Yellow marks what is new or changed in v9 — the 6–8 Jul escalation (tanker attacks, US strikes, the oil-waiver revocation), the US chip flip, the raised Hormuz threat level, the sharper market reaction, and the new implementation-week box. The scenario architecture, all other actor chips, the signed-MoU text, the Resolution mechanics, the Murrin due diligence and the Brent bands carry from v8 unmarked.

Bottom line · a second, larger armed exchange, a failed talks track, and Trump's sharpest words yet

The scenario set stays bifurcated: Stalemate (modal) and Resolution (improving probability), with the apex tail held in the long-term allocation below the scenario-modelling threshold. Iran escalated its campaign to control Hormuz transit on 6–7 July, missiling a Qatari gas carrier and a Saudi tanker and droning a third vessel — the largest single-day volume of attacks since the MoU — prompting JMIC to raise the regional threat level from substantial to severe. The US answered on 8 July with fresh airstrikes on Iran and, separately, revoked the waiver that has let Iran sell oil internationally since the ceasefire, withdrawing a core economic term of the deal for the first time since signing. The Doha indirect track concluded with no headway, and at the NATO summit in Ankara Trump called the MoU “over,” saying he does not want to deal with Tehran — the sharpest US-side statement of the cycle. Markets read this as more serious than the late-June exchange: US equities fell, oil jumped, and Asian markets were mixed. Talks are paused for the late Supreme Leader Khamenei’s funeral, not a mediation breakdown, and no named watch-list trigger matches this event exactly, though it clears the bar as a new escalation in its own right. Murrin’s Article 2 nuclear scenario remains the analytical foundation under structural tail protection; the apex stays re-timed to the 60-day window. Full scenario stances, drivers, equity and Brent views are in section 03.

01 · Due Diligence on Murrin

Verdict unchanged — cannot rule it out.

Capabilities corroborated (Tier 1); interpretive layers held as hypotheses (Tier 2); the policy conclusion treated as advocacy (Tier 3). The full claim-by-claim verifiability table sits in the v1 tab.

Murrin’s capabilities case is sound and OSINT-sourced. Timeline sits at the aggressive end of a 6–12 month band that overlaps Roaring Lion’s own assessment. The preemptive-nuclear-use conclusion is his advocacy. The Gallant “440 Kilograms” piece restates the ~440kg 60% HEU stockpile that anchors the capabilities case, and ISW, citing five US-intelligence sources via CNN, reports Iran is physically hardening that stockpile — collapsing tunnels and mining storage entrances to defeat any seizure — so the fissile core is intact, deferred, and being entrenched. Iran agreed to readmit IAEA inspectors, the first forward step on the nuclear file; it is not down-blending. That step has stalled: the inspection access is unresolved, with Grossi expecting entry while Iran ties it to a final deal and sanctions relief, and Iran has signalled it could suspend the talks over the armed exchange. The ~440kg core stays inside Iran behind the entrenched tunnels. “Cannot rule out the nuclear scenario” holds as the analytical foundation; the apex is handled via the long-term allocation rather than modelled scenarios.

02 · Scenario test

Live tail risk on a 6–12 month horizon — below the scenario-modelling threshold.

The conclusion is unchanged; the operational decision is to bear this tail in the long-term allocation rather than model it.

The apex first-use event is paradigm-break, so protection is structural rather than model-derived. As David noted, “in short, I probably don’t need to know what the damage would look like.” The deal does not lower the tail — it re-times it. The MOU defers the nuclear question to a 60-day negotiation while the HEU stays inside Iran, so the breakout clock moves from the battlefield to the talks table without resetting. That table is now live, and Iran is using it to bank its front-loaded wins: the first 60-day-track round met in Switzerland on 21–22 Jun, but Iran made opening the nuclear file conditional on the prior delivery of sanctions waivers, frozen-asset release and the Lebanon ceasefire. The 25–28 Jun armed exchange is the test case for kinetic escalation short of first-use — a ship strike, US retaliation on Iranian military sites, and Iranian strikes on US bases in Bahrain and Kuwait, all conventional, with the apex below threshold throughout. It sits in Stalemate-stress, below the apex. The 6–8 Jul exchange is a second instance of the same pattern — Iranian tanker strikes, a US military and economic response — conventional throughout, with the apex unmoved. What stays in the long-term allocation is the nightmare convergence — nuclear Iran + reinvigorated Russia + hedging China + exhausted America + isolated Israel arriving together around summer 2026 as oil buffers run dry; Murrin Article 3’s late-stage US-decline thesis sits on the “exhausted America” leg as backdrop conviction, with a US Strategic Petroleum Reserve at a historically low ~325.7m barrels by 29 Jun, the lowest since 1983, monitored daily at the tip-of-iceberg layer.

Verdict

Murrin Article 2 cannot be ruled out and remains a live tail risk on a 6–12 month horizon. The apex probability sits below the threshold where another modelled scenario would change the prescription. Israeli unilateral action remains the most plausible trigger — and the first armed US-Iran exchange under the MoU showed the deal can turn kinetic through Hormuz directly; the strait and the Lebanon front are both live channels for an escalation that the long-term allocation is built to absorb. A second such exchange in early July confirms Hormuz as a recurring, non-Israeli escalation channel. It does not change which trigger is most plausible.

Per the Eric/team binding decision (22 May): two scenarios modelled, plus the BAU baseline. The apex first-use scenario is held in the long-term allocation. Architecture unchanged in v9.

04 · The signed MoU

What the text says — the milestone, Article by Article.

The 14-point text Bloomberg published on 16 June and the version a senior US official read aloud on signing (17 Jun); the Article text below is verbatim from those primary publications, carried as the reference for the 60-day track. The flag is our reading of what each Article concedes, defers, or leaves contested; the implementation boxes below track how the obligations have executed.

ArticleText (close paraphrase)Our reading
1 · Ceasefire Immediate and permanent end to the war on all fronts including Lebanon; no hostile actions; refrain from the threat or use of force; the final agreement confirms this. Signed edit: a Lebanese-sovereignty guarantee was added. Contested — Iran reads it as obliging an Israeli withdrawal; Israel is not a signatory and keeps striking.
2 · Sovereignty Both parties respect each other’s sovereignty and territorial integrity and refrain from interfering in internal affairs. Implicitly constrains US regime-change; the IRGC structure survives. Iran also leans on this to frame “managing” Hormuz as an internal affair.
3 · 60-day window Negotiate and reach a final agreement within a maximum of 60 days, extendable by mutual consent. The clock for every deferred core. “Extendable” means the pause can stretch if Iran judges its leverage growing.
4 · US obligations Immediate: lift the naval blockade, prevent interference against Iran, restore traffic within 30 days to pre-war capacity proportional to Iran’s pre-war volume. US withdraws forces from surrounding areas within 30 days after the final agreement. Asymmetry: the blockade lifts at once, but force withdrawal waits on the final agreement — US forces stay through the 60-day window plus 30 days. “Proportional to Iran’s pre-war traffic” hands Iran the reopening pace.
5 · Iran obligations (Hormuz) Immediate: resume merchant shipping in the Gulf / Sea of Oman within 30 days to pre-war volume, removing technical obstacles and neutralising mines. Signed edit: a joint Iranian-Omani administration and a 60-day toll bar were added; IRGC media says fees resume after. Iran’s win — the text does not bar Iran from “managing” the strait; toll-free is time-boxed.
6 · $300bn development US and regional partners build a rehabilitation and development plan financing at least $300bn; the implementation mechanism is set within 60 days. External Gulf money, not a US outlay (Trump: “no money changes hands”; Reuters: no US government funds). Gulf-funded, routing the financing into the Gulf-state track.
7 · Sanctions US commits to ending all sanctions — UNSC resolutions, IAEA Board resolutions, all unilateral US primary and secondary sanctions — on a schedule set in the final agreement. Exceeds the JCPOA’s scope in principle, but the schedule is deferred to the final agreement — a commitment on record, not yet executed.
8 · Nuclear Iran reiterates it will never produce nuclear weapons; the fate of enriched material and all nuclear-related issues, including Iran’s “nuclear needs,” is “adequately addressed” in the final agreement. A restatement of Iran’s standing position, not a new concession. The version read aloud on signing adds on-site down-blending of the stockpile under IAEA supervision as the baseline method — gated to the final agreement and cutting against Iran’s simultaneous hardening of its HEU tunnels. The ~440kg core stays in Iran through the window.
9 · Status quo Pending the final agreement, Iran holds the status quo on its nuclear programme and the US imposes no new sanctions and does not strengthen its forces in the region. The strategic freeze: Iran’s programme stays intact through the 60 days while US leverage is capped. The most consequential clause for the deferral read.
10 · Oil waivers US Treasury issues waivers immediately after signing for Iranian crude, petrochemicals and derivatives, and related services (banking, insurance, transport). Signed edit tested: the waiver was revoked on 8 Jul, seven weeks after signing, in response to the 6–7 Jul tanker attacks — the first rollback of a core MoU economic term. Revocable, as flagged; the risk is now realised rather than theoretical.
11 · Frozen assets Frozen and restricted Iranian funds released and made fully available in line with negotiation progress, directed by the Central Bank of Iran. Sequenced to negotiation progress — Iran’s leverage-extraction path. Vance conditions release on “verified steps” on the HEU; Vahidi insists the funds are not barred from military spending.
12 · Implementation A mechanism to oversee implementation and the commitment to the final agreement. Compliance architecture with no specified membership, voting or enforcement — to be designed in 60 days.
13 · Sequencing gate Nuclear negotiations begin only after Iran receives assurances on the implementation of Articles 4, 5, 10 and 11 (blockade, Hormuz, oil waivers, frozen assets); talks then address only the remaining articles. Iran’s verification gate, now in the text — it can delay the nuclear talks by contesting any of those four implementations. The Article 10 waiver revocation gives Iran fresh grounds to invoke this gate.
14 · UNSC ratification The final agreement is approved through a binding UN Security Council resolution. China and Russia become structural guarantors — neither the US nor Israel can unilaterally exit the final deal without a UNSC crisis. Iran’s durability anchor.
Implementation — week 1

The first week of execution ran two ways. Hormuz (Art 4/5): oil flowed at the fastest pace since the war — Bloomberg ~20m barrels Fri–Sun, CENTCOM ~17m Sat, Chabahar cargoes clearing — yet the IRGC Navy announced a re-closure on 20 Jun conditioned on Israel halting Lebanon operations; CENTCOM rebutted that traffic still flows. Nuclear (Art 8/9): Iran agreed to readmit IAEA inspectors; the ~440kg HEU stays in Iran. Sequencing (Art 13): Iran gated the nuclear file behind delivery of the ceasefire, blockade-lift, Hormuz, oil-waiver and frozen-asset clauses, banking ~$12bn of relief up front. Lebanon (Art 1): now Iran’s declared top priority, with the 19 Jun Israel–Hezbollah ceasefire already violated on both sides.

Implementation — week 2

Week 2 turned kinetic. Hormuz (Art 4/5): after crossings surged to a post-war high (~73 on Wed, ~125 on the week per Lloyd’s), an Iranian drone struck the Ever Lovely (25 Jun); the IMO paused its ~600-ship evacuation and Friday traffic halved as Iran’s maritime authority warned that transit outside its designated routes is not covered by safe passage. US–Iran exchange: CENTCOM struck Iranian missile, drone-storage and radar sites in response (26–27 Jun); Iran hit US bases in Bahrain and Kuwait (28 Jun). Sovereignty (Art 5): Araghchi asserts Iran is “solely responsible” for the strait, while the Article text mandates dialogue with Oman and the Gulf littorals; Oman signals service fees and the US and GCC reject tolls — the post-60-day fee regime is being contested early. Nuclear (Art 8/9): the IAEA-inspection dispute is unresolved and Iran has signalled it could suspend talks.

Implementation — week 3 · v9

Hormuz (Art 4/5): Iran missiled a Qatari gas carrier and a Saudi tanker on 6 Jul for using non-Iranian-approved transit routes, then droned a third vessel on 7 Jul — the largest single-day attack volume since signing; JMIC raised the threat level from substantial to severe. Iran separately rejected a UAE-brokered IMO proposal to manage the strait, reasserting that transit must follow Iranian arrangements, while Saudi Arabia, Kuwait and the UAE advance pipeline plans to bypass Hormuz entirely and NATO foreign ministers took up the issue with Gulf counterparts in Ankara. Oil waivers (Art 10): the US revoked the waiver permitting Iranian oil sales on 8 Jul, giving Iran until 17 Jul to wind down transactions — the first rollback of a core MoU economic term since signing. Nuclear (Art 8/9): unchanged this window. 60-day window (Art 3): the indirect Doha track concluded with no headway, and Trump told reporters in Ankara the MoU is “over” and he does not want to deal with Tehran — the sharpest test yet of whether the window holds, though it is his statement alone, with no reciprocal declaration from Iran. Sequencing (Art 13): talks are paused for the late Supreme Leader Khamenei’s funeral (burial 9 Jul, Mashhad).

05 · Stakeholder map

Actor positions through the Stalemate / Resolution lens.

The Roaring Lion A/B/C framework is the analytical reference: Stalemate ≈ persistent Bad B; Resolution ≈ Good A via JPOA 2.0 + Hormuz Council + Security Guarantee. The US cell moves again on a second armed exchange and the oil-waiver revocation; the other chips hold, with the Iran and GCC cells sharpening on the 6–8 Jul detail.

United States
Struck again, and this time pulled an economic lever. The US version of the terms — dismantle the nuclear programme, end Axis support — is far harder than Iran’s, so the signed ceasefire still sits on an unreconciled core. Vance led the first 60-day-track round in Switzerland; Trump warned Iran “you won’t have a country” if it re-closes the strait, floated a US takeover of the waterway with a US-charged toll, and threatened to “hit Iran very hard again” if Hezbollah is not reined in. That threat was carried out once already in late June; US CENTCOM struck Iranian missile, drone-storage and coastal-radar sites on 26–27 Jun in response to the Ever Lovely attack, and both sides stood down by 29–30 Jun. A second exchange landed on 6–8 Jul: after Iran’s worst single day of tanker attacks since the MoU, the US struck Iran again and, separately, revoked the waiver permitting Iranian oil sales, giving Iran until 17 July to wind down transactions — the first withdrawal of a core economic term of the deal since signing. A US official framed continued deal benefits as conditional on Iranian “good behaviour”; a second official maintained negotiators remain in good faith. That restraint did not last the day: the Doha indirect track concluded with no headway, and at the NATO summit in Ankara Trump said “I think it’s over. I don’t want to deal with them,” calling Iran’s leadership “scum” and “sick people” and the process “a waste of time.” It is the sharpest US-side statement of the cycle, and the first delivered by Trump personally. This file’s pattern to date has been hardline rhetoric that later gave way to continued contact.
Struck again ·
declares MoU over
Israel
Citrinowicz: intent intact, capacity to disrupt constrained. A “complete end to the war in Lebanon” is an explicit Iranian red line, so Israel’s Lebanon campaign is a direct obstacle to the deal; the signed Clause 1 guarantees Lebanese sovereignty, which Tehran tells Hezbollah obliges a phased Israeli withdrawal, while Israel is not a party and remains in southern Lebanon. The 19 Jun Israel–Hezbollah ceasefire failed on both sides within the day, and the IDF kept striking. A trilateral Israel–Lebanon–US framework followed on 27 Jun: Israel keeps a security zone along the yellow line and withdraws from two pilot zones for Lebanese-army-led Hezbollah disarmament, while Hezbollah rejects the terms. This is a de-escalation step on the Lebanon front, and it does not move the spoiler read; there is no unilateral strike on Iran proper. Regime change stays off the Resolution menu; Netanyahu’s stake in a US–Iran deal failing is unchanged.
Good A/C →
regime change
Iran
Fractured — and the hardline camp is winning the fracture. Iran’s primary Hormuz objective is recognised sovereignty over the strait; signed Clause 5 writes that claim into the text, with fees barred for 60 days only and reopened with the Gulf states after. The factional split is overt, the delegation slow-walks the nuclear file behind Clause 13, and IRGC-aligned media reports the Supreme Leader is “not fully” behind the MoU. In late June Iran moved from announcing the re-closure lever to using force — two ship strikes, then drone and ballistic-missile strikes on US bases in Bahrain and Kuwait, warning Gulf states against hosting US attacks — while framing the Doha follow-up as implementation talks, not final-agreement negotiation. On 6–7 Jul Iran escalated again, missiling two tankers and droning a third for using non-Iranian-approved routes — its largest single-day attack volume since the MoU — and rejected a UAE-brokered IMO proposal to manage the strait, insisting transit follow Iranian arrangements. It vows “decisive actions” against the 8 Jul US strikes and waiver revocation. The leverage stays checked: Gulf states are advancing pipeline plans to bypass Hormuz entirely, and the open Omani route continues to draw traffic away from Iran’s scheme.
Hardliners up ·
using force
China
Publicly pushes Resolution (ceasefire, lanes reopened, Xi/Putin Beijing meeting), but the pivot is partly performative — PRC kept sending missile components and fuel precursors during the war. Per Pape, the war already shifted Beijing’s read of US resolve; munitions depletion (FT, Global21) reinforces it.
Pushes Resolution
Russia
Aligned with China publicly; has formally offered to take Iran’s HEU (acknowledged by Araghchi, “not currently under discussion”). Accepting HEU under the Security Guarantee architecture would be the technical pathway. Preserves Iran ties privately (drones via Caspian).
HEU custodian role available
GCC / Pakistan
Mediation delivered the ceasefire — Pakistan’s PM announced the deal first, Qatar brokered a reported $12bn financial channel, and the UAE reopened a direct security line to Tehran; the 21–22 Jun Bürgenstock round was quadrilateral, with Qatar and Pakistan mediating. All GCC bar Oman reject permanent Iranian control of Hormuz; signed Clause 5 requires Iran to negotiate the post-60-day fee regime with Oman and the other Gulf states, who ISW judges unlikely to acquiesce without Iranian coercion. The US and GCC jointly rejected any Iranian tolls or strait control on 25 Jun; Iran’s 28 Jun strikes on Bahrain read as coercion of the Gulf hosts. By 30 Jun Oman, meeting Macron in Paris, reaffirmed freedom of navigation “without conditions,” and the open Omani route keeps drawing traffic. The UAE has now taken the dispute multilateral, proposing an IMO plan for managing the strait that Iran rejected — the first attempt to institutionalise strait governance outside direct US-Iran or Iran-Oman channels. Saudi Arabia, Kuwait and the UAE are separately advancing pipeline plans to bypass Hormuz altogether, and NATO foreign ministers met Bahraini, Kuwaiti, Qatari and Emirati counterparts on the strait issue in Ankara on 7 Jul. The Saudi–Pakistan airspace cross-promise still binds Israeli unilateral action.
Active mediators
Europe
Aligns with the diplomatic track; refused the US request to reopen the Strait, so it is constrained from leading any post-war architecture. A NATO contribution to the HEU Security Guarantee would be the test. Switzerland hosted the formal ceremony and the first implementation round; the post-war security architecture would sit with other parties.
Diplomatic backer
Japan / Korea
Lean on the US for Resolution; ~60% of energy via Hormuz. The US warned Japan of up-to-two-year Tomahawk delays as the Pentagon prioritises Middle East restocking — US overstretch landing on an Indo-Pacific ally, reinforcing the hedge. Dormant nuclear conversations re-open if the scenario shifts to apex.
Hedging

The 8 Jun – 8 Jul: whipsaw, signing, first armed exchange, and a second, larger one

8–17 Jun
Ceasefire collapses then resolves onto de-escalation: Israeli and US strikes inside Iran (8–10 Jun); Trump declares the deal “complete” (15 Jun); the US and Iran sign the MoU two days early (17 Jun), with signed edits to Clause 1 (Lebanon) and Clause 5 (Hormuz: joint Iranian-Omani administration, fees barred for 60 days). The blockade lifts and oil sanctions waive on signing.
19–22 Jun
A separate Israel–Hezbollah ceasefire is announced and violated within the day; Iran’s IRGC Navy announces a re-closure of the strait tied to Lebanon, which CENTCOM rebuts as flow continues. The first 60-day-track round meets at Bürgenstock (Vance–Araghchi, Qatar/Pakistan mediating) and reaffirms the roadmap; Bloomberg tracks Hormuz flow at the fastest pace since the war; Brent falls to ~$79.
24–30 Jun
Oil falls to its pre-war anchor (~$73). An Iranian drone strikes the Ever Lovely (25 Jun); US CENTCOM strikes Iranian missile, drone-storage and radar sites in response (26–27 Jun); Iran fires drones and ballistic missiles at US bases in Bahrain and Kuwait (28 Jun) — the first armed exchange under the MoU. Both sides stand down by 29–30 Jun; a disputed Doha implementation track opens on 30 Jun.
6 Jul
Iran missiles a Qatari gas carrier (Al Rekayyat) and a Saudi-flagged tanker in the strait for using non-Iranian-approved transit routes.
7 Jul
Iran drones a third, unspecified tanker — the largest single-day volume of Hormuz attacks since the MoU; JMIC raises the regional threat level from substantial to severe. Iran rejects a UAE-brokered IMO proposal to manage the strait; NATO foreign ministers meet Gulf counterparts on the strait issue in Ankara.
8 Jul
The US launches fresh airstrikes on Iran and revokes the waiver permitting Iranian oil sales, giving Iran until 17 July to wind down transactions; Iran vows “decisive actions.” The Doha indirect track concludes with no headway, and at the NATO summit in Ankara Trump says “I think it’s over. I don’t want to deal with them,” calling Iran’s leadership “scum” and “sick people.” Markets read the day as more serious than June’s — US equities fall, oil jumps, Asian markets are mixed. Talks are separately paused for Khamenei’s funeral (burial 9 Jul, Mashhad).
What it confirms: the off-ramp produced a signed ceasefire that is executing, on an unreconciled core. What it does not change: the bifurcated architecture, the apex held in the long-term allocation, the Murrin DD, the Brent bands. Watch-list: a second confirmed-strike escalation fired this run, again US-Iran; the standing Israeli-strike trigger has not fired. This time it is paired with the oil-waiver revocation — a first rollback of a core MoU term — and with the closest approach yet to the mediation-breakdown trigger: the Doha track failed and Trump called the deal “over.” The trigger is not called fired outright, since Iran has not reciprocated and no joint declaration exists; it is logged as the sharpest test of that trigger to date. The pause in talks also traces to Khamenei’s funeral. The live channels are unchanged: contested Hormuz governance and the toll regime, the IAEA dispute, and the Lebanon front.
The map — a second kinetic test

Stalemate stays modal because the US–Iran nuclear divide is wide and the hardliner faction blocks Good A. The ceasefire is a Stage-1 instrument: it ends the shooting, lifts the blockade and reopens Hormuz, while the nuclear core, the HEU stockpile and sanctions sequencing are pushed to a 60-day track. The first armed exchange under the MoU showed the deal could turn kinetic without breaking; the market priced that violation as contained. A second, larger exchange on 6–8 Jul confirms the pattern: Hormuz keeps producing kinetic tests, and this time the US answered with an economic rollback as well. The same day, the Doha track failed and Trump called the MoU “over” — the sharpest rhetorical break of the cycle. The unresolved question is unchanged: whether the deferred cores settle inside the 60-day window or return as binding at its end. This exchange, like the last one, moves none of them toward settlement; whether Trump’s statement does is the open question the next input will test.

06 · Market & thematic implications

Scenario views across equities, Brent, rates, and rotation.

Views only. Brent bands and the scenario architecture are unchanged from v8; the 6–8 Jul escalation pushed oil back up from the pre-war anchor and drew a sharper equity reaction than June’s exchange.

ScenarioEquitiesBrentRatesSector & thematic rotation
Stalemate
Modal		 Mid-teens downside; EPS holds until ~2027 demand destruction. US indices fell on the 8 Jul news (Dow -0.3%, S&P -0.5%, Nasdaq -1.2%, chipmakers leading); Asian markets were mixed (Hang Seng higher on financials, Topix and ASX lower on the crude jump). Tight, elevated, drawing inventory. Per Carlyle, the move down through late June was destocking-driven — US SPR near its operational floor, Cushing near the settlement floor — not a fundamentals rebalance, and fragile to any reopening slippage. That fragility showed on 6–8 Jul: oil rose more than 3% intraday on the tanker attacks alone (European gas futures +7%), with a further leg on the waiver-revocation news — no settled print has reached this desk yet, but the direction is a genuine re-tightening away from the ~$73 pre-war anchor. The SPR buffer is still thin at ~325.7m barrels, the lowest since 1983, against a JPMorgan-cited ~$150 re-closure shock. Fiscal pressure lifts yields; energy tightening Jul–Sep adds to the curve. Iran-axis exposure screens unattractive on the retaliation vectors (UAE data centres, regional fintech, Gulf cable-dependent payments). Energy-paradigm shift favours rare earths, security and infrastructure; defensive on regional refining. Low feasibility, but it sharpens the cable-dependent-payments screen.

Improving		 Mid-single-digit upside. Declines toward $90–110 over months; market below the band — ~$84 on the ceasefire, ~$73 by 29 Jun at the pre-war anchor — pushed back up by the 6–8 Jul escalation, direction confirmed though no settled print yet — pricing a clean reopening the substance does not yet support; trust rebuild takes quarters. Yields ease as the energy premium unwinds. Iran-axis exposure screens better selectively; the energy/renewables opportunity set persists on the structural energy-paradigm shift; the China sleeve recalibrates on a firmer Hormuz-access outcome.

Apex / tail: escalation toward $130–160+ Brent is held in the long-term allocation; first-use ($200+, SPX ~3,500) sits below the scenario-modelling threshold as structural tail protection. The deal re-times this tail to the 60-day nuclear window rather than removing it — the HEU stays in Iran and is being hardened. The re-closure scenario the market is now pricing as a ~$150 shock is the near-term, non-nuclear face of this escalation leg. The Taiwan-war and US-default extrapolations are held as backdrop only, outside the modelled set.

The $300bn in the signed terms is external Gulf investment funded by sanctions relief — for instance a UAE-built power plant in Iran — not a US Treasury outlay, which reconciles the terms with Iran’s frozen-asset demand; the relief flows through GCC capital.

Resolution mechanics

The Resolution path follows the framework David specified: Mearsheimer’s “humble pie” off-ramp; a JPOA 2.0 nuclear architecture; the Ferguson Hormuz Council (Iran as Chair, a 45% / 30% / balance revenue split, transit fees at cost); HEU export under a Security Guarantee with Russia available as custodian; and a post-war Gulf Treaty Organisation with China and Russia as signatories. The realised Stage-1 MOU diverges from the Council mechanic on Hormuz: rather than a chaired revenue split with fees at cost, the strait reopens toll-free but under Iranian and Omani management — Iran’s sovereignty claim conceded operationally, the revenue-sharing design bypassed, with fees barred for 60 days then talks with the Gulf states. The deeper cores the framework was built to settle — HEU ~440kg, enrichment, sanctions sequencing — remain for the 60-day track. The Hormuz governance the framework was built to settle is being contested by force: Iran asserts Article-5 “sole responsibility” and strikes vessels that use the Omani route, Oman signals service fees, and the US and GCC reject tolls — the fee-regime negotiation the MoU deferred is opening under fire. Iran’s rejection of the UAE’s IMO strait-management proposal fits the same logic: control of the mechanism matters more to Iran than the toll income itself, which is why a multilateral body is a harder concession than a bilateral one with Oman. That logic held even against Oman itself: Muscat proposed a two-corridor split on 11 Jul — an unrestricted southern route in Omani waters, a northern route in Iranian waters requiring prior Iranian approval but no tolls — and Iran rejected it on 12 Jul after Foreign Minister Araghchi could not secure Tehran’s sign-off. The joint Iranian-Omani administration the signed Clause 5 specifies is still, on paper, the operative framework; it has not yet produced an implementable arrangement.

v10 · Third strike wave reaches central Iran, Iran hits five Gulf states · 13 Jul 2026
Architecture & bands held · US chip flipped · IRGC chain-of-command confirmed · Oman corridor proposal rejected · talks continued anyway · Hormuz-closure claim disputed, unconfirmed

The architecture holds — Stalemate (modal) / Resolution (improving) / apex in the long-term allocation. The exchange that opened this file’s second armed round kept widening: a second US strike wave hit 90 targets on 8 Jul, Iran extended retaliation from two Gulf states to three (9 Jul) then five (12 Jul), and a third, much larger wave — roughly 140 targets on 11 Jul alone, CENTCOM says over 300 across three nights — reached central Iran for the first time, beyond the coastal target set of the prior two rounds. Oman brokered a two-corridor Hormuz compromise on 11 Jul; Iran rejected it the next day. One chip moves on it.

What moved from v9:

  • US chip flips: “Struck again · declares MoU ‘over’” → “Talks resumed · struck deeper.” On 10 Jul Trump said the US agreed to resume talks with Iran at Iran’s request — the first test of the open question v9 flagged, whether hardline rhetoric would give way to continued contact — while still maintaining the ceasefire is over. Iranian and Omani officials met in Muscat the next day.
  • Third strike wave reaches central Iran. CENTCOM’s 11 Jul wave — its largest yet — expanded into Kerman, Lorestan, Markazi and Kohgiluyeh and Boyer Ahmad provinces, beyond the coastal sites hit in the first two rounds.
  • Chain of command ordered the strikes. ISW assesses top IRGC officers close to the supreme leader approved the strait attacks, contradicting Iranian negotiators’ account of an “errant faction.” Araghchi, Ghalibaf and Pezeshkian kept negotiating in parallel regardless. Supreme Leader Military Adviser Maj. Gen. Mohsen Rezaei called the strait “more important than dozens of atomic bombs” (12 Jul).
  • Barclays flags a lower Brent range. $65–80, below this file’s $90–110 Resolution-quick floor; puts a return to all-out conflict at 20% and rates the MoU “on life support but may survive.” No settled print has reached this desk for the 9–12 Jul window.
  • Hormuz-closure claim, unconfirmed. A 13 Jul report has Iran claiming it closed the strait outright, disputed by the US. It postdates the newest primary source on hand (ISW, 12 Jul) and is carried as Iran’s contested claim, not established fact.

The architecture, the verdict, the Brent bands and the apex placement are unchanged.

Bottom line · escalation keeps widening even as the diplomatic channel stays open

The scenario set stays bifurcated: Stalemate (modal) and Resolution (improving probability), with the apex tail held in the long-term allocation below the scenario-modelling threshold. The exchange that opened on 6 Jul kept escalating through 12 Jul: a second US strike wave (90 targets, 8 Jul) hit rail and bridge infrastructure Iran uses to move Russian- and Chinese-sourced military material; Iran widened retaliation from two Gulf states to three (Kuwait, Bahrain, Qatar, 9 Jul) then five (adding Jordan and Oman, 12 Jul); and a third, much larger wave — roughly 140 targets on 11 Jul, over 300 across three nights per CENTCOM — reached central Iran, beyond the coastal target sets of the first two rounds. Oman brokered a two-corridor Hormuz compromise on 11 Jul that Iran rejected the next day. Set against the widening strikes, the diplomatic channel did not close: Trump said on 10 Jul the US agreed to resume talks at Iran’s request even while calling the ceasefire over, and Iranian-Omani officials met in Muscat on 11 Jul. ISW assesses Iran’s own negotiators are working in parallel with, not overridden by, the IRGC officers who ordered the strait attacks. The mediation-breakdown watch-list trigger recedes rather than firing outright — talks continued through the window even as the kinetic tempo rose. The Israeli-strike trigger has still not fired; this remains a US-Iran exchange. Murrin’s Article 2 nuclear scenario remains the analytical foundation under structural tail protection; the apex stays re-timed to the 60-day window. Full scenario stances, drivers, equity and Brent views are in section 03.

01 · Due Diligence on Murrin

Verdict unchanged — cannot rule it out.

Capabilities corroborated (Tier 1); interpretive layers held as hypotheses (Tier 2); the policy conclusion treated as advocacy (Tier 3). The full claim-by-claim verifiability table sits in the v1 tab.

Murrin’s capabilities case is sound and OSINT-sourced. Timeline sits at the aggressive end of a 6–12 month band that overlaps Roaring Lion’s own assessment. The preemptive-nuclear-use conclusion is his advocacy. The Gallant “440 Kilograms” piece restates the ~440kg 60% HEU stockpile that anchors the capabilities case, and ISW, citing five US-intelligence sources via CNN, reports Iran is physically hardening that stockpile — collapsing tunnels and mining storage entrances to defeat any seizure — so the fissile core is intact, deferred, and being entrenched. Iran agreed to readmit IAEA inspectors, the first forward step on the nuclear file; it is not down-blending. That step has stalled: the inspection access is unresolved, with Grossi expecting entry while Iran ties it to a final deal and sanctions relief, and Iran has signalled it could suspend the talks over the armed exchange. The ~440kg core stays inside Iran behind the entrenched tunnels. “Cannot rule out the nuclear scenario” holds as the analytical foundation; the apex is handled via the long-term allocation rather than modelled scenarios.

02 · Scenario test

Live tail risk on a 6–12 month horizon — below the scenario-modelling threshold.

The conclusion is unchanged; the operational decision is to bear this tail in the long-term allocation rather than model it.

The apex first-use event is paradigm-break, so protection is structural rather than model-derived. As David noted, “in short, I probably don’t need to know what the damage would look like.” The deal does not lower the tail — it re-times it. The MOU defers the nuclear question to a 60-day negotiation while the HEU stays inside Iran, so the breakout clock moves from the battlefield to the talks table without resetting. That table is now live, and Iran is using it to bank its front-loaded wins: the first 60-day-track round met in Switzerland on 21–22 Jun, but Iran made opening the nuclear file conditional on the prior delivery of sanctions waivers, frozen-asset release and the Lebanon ceasefire. The 25–28 Jun armed exchange is the test case for kinetic escalation short of first-use — a ship strike, US retaliation on Iranian military sites, and Iranian strikes on US bases in Bahrain and Kuwait, all conventional, with the apex below threshold throughout. It sits in Stalemate-stress, below the apex. The 6–8 Jul exchange is a second instance of the same pattern — Iranian tanker strikes, a US military and economic response — conventional throughout, with the apex unmoved. The 8–12 Jul continuation is a third instance, larger in scale (strikes now reach central Iran, retaliation now reaches five Gulf states) but the same kind of event — conventional strikes over strait control, not a step toward first-use. Two senior Iranian sources told Reuters that Iran will not discuss its nuclear program unless the US recognises Iranian control of the strait, and US officials told the Wall Street Journal the US will not conclude a nuclear deal unless Iran relinquishes its enriched material — both sides now state the linkage explicitly, tightening the coupling between the Hormuz dispute and the 60-day nuclear track without changing what is being modelled. What stays in the long-term allocation is the nightmare convergence — nuclear Iran + reinvigorated Russia + hedging China + exhausted America + isolated Israel arriving together around summer 2026 as oil buffers run dry; Murrin Article 3’s late-stage US-decline thesis sits on the “exhausted America” leg as backdrop conviction, with a US Strategic Petroleum Reserve at a historically low ~325.7m barrels by 29 Jun, the lowest since 1983, monitored daily at the tip-of-iceberg layer.

Verdict

Murrin Article 2 cannot be ruled out and remains a live tail risk on a 6–12 month horizon. The apex probability sits below the threshold where another modelled scenario would change the prescription. Israeli unilateral action remains the most plausible trigger — and the first armed US-Iran exchange under the MoU showed the deal can turn kinetic through Hormuz directly; the strait and the Lebanon front are both live channels for an escalation that the long-term allocation is built to absorb. A second such exchange in early July confirmed Hormuz as a recurring, non-Israeli escalation channel. A third, larger round over 8–12 July reinforces the same read: eleven days of near-continuous US-Iran strikes have not displaced Israeli unilateral action as the most plausible apex trigger, nor drawn Israel directly into the exchange.

03 · The scenario set

Stalemate / Resolution / BAU — stance, drivers, and market views.

Per the Eric/team binding decision (22 May): two scenarios modelled, plus the BAU baseline. The apex first-use scenario is held in the long-term allocation. Architecture unchanged in v10.

ScenarioKey driversEquitiesBrent
Stalemate
Modal
Eric/team · persistent Bad B		 Sustained economic pressure; US–Iran nuclear divide wide; hardliners block Good A; energy tightens Jul–Sep; fiscal pressure lifts yields; S&P EPS holds until demand destruction (~2027). The ceasefire pauses the shooting, but the nuclear divide is deferred and the IRGC/Vahidi camp dominates Tehran’s red lines. Iran has re-armed Hormuz as leverage and gated the nuclear file behind the relief clauses. It has used force to assert that control three times now — an 8–12 Jul continuation reaching central Iran and five Gulf states, the widest and deepest round yet, with talks nonetheless continuing in parallel (Muscat, 11 Jul). Mid-teens downside. Tight, elevated, drawing inventory. Barclays reads the immediate escalation as adding roughly 6% to oil on the day, with a $65–80 range expected over coming months on OPEC supply and Chinese reserve dynamics — below this file’s $90–110 Resolution-quick floor, flagged rather than adopted; no settled print has reached this desk for the 9–12 Jul window.
Resolution
Improving
Eric/team + David · Good A		 Mearsheimer “humble pie”; JPOA 2.0 framework; Ferguson Hormuz Council; HEU export under Security Guarantee; post-war Gulf Treaty Organisation. Stage 1 signed; the first 60-day-track round convened (Switzerland, 21–22 Jun) and reaffirmed the roadmap, with IAEA inspectors readmitted; the durable settlement stays gated on that track. The oil-waiver revocation is a setback to the Resolution path’s economic-normalisation leg specifically, even as talks continue on other fronts; Iran’s rejection of a UAE-brokered multilateral proposal for the strait reaffirms it wants control negotiated bilaterally with Oman. That preference held only up to a point: Iran rejected Oman’s own two-corridor proposal on 12 Jul after failing to secure Tehran’s approval, even as the Muscat channel itself stayed open. The tier holds “improving” on that basis — the diplomatic track survived a Trump statement calling the deal over and a record strike tempo — not on any concession by Iran. Mid-single-digit upside. Declines toward $90–110 over months; market below the band — ~$84 on the ceasefire, ~$73 by 29 Jun at the pre-war anchor, pushed back up by the 6–8 Jul escalation — and again by the 8–12 Jul continuation; direction confirmed, no settled print yet — pricing a clean reopening the substance does not yet support; trust rebuild takes quarters.
