Apex / nightmare convergence — held in the long-term allocation, below the scenario-modelling threshold. Paradigm-break severity (SPX ~3,500, Brent $200+); handled through structural tail protection, outside the modelled set.

04 · The signed MoU

What the text says — the milestone, Article by Article.

The 14-point text Bloomberg published on 16 June and the version a senior US official read aloud on signing (17 Jun); the Article text below is verbatim from those primary publications, carried as the reference for the 60-day track. The flag is our reading of what each Article concedes, defers, or leaves contested; the implementation boxes below track how the obligations have executed.

ArticleText (close paraphrase)Our reading
1 · Ceasefire Immediate and permanent end to the war on all fronts including Lebanon; no hostile actions; refrain from the threat or use of force; the final agreement confirms this. Signed edit: a Lebanese-sovereignty guarantee was added. Contested — Iran reads it as obliging an Israeli withdrawal; Israel is not a signatory and keeps striking.
2 · Sovereignty Both parties respect each other’s sovereignty and territorial integrity and refrain from interfering in internal affairs. Implicitly constrains US regime-change; the IRGC structure survives. Iran also leans on this to frame “managing” Hormuz as an internal affair.
3 · 60-day window Negotiate and reach a final agreement within a maximum of 60 days, extendable by mutual consent. The clock for every deferred core. “Extendable” means the pause can stretch if Iran judges its leverage growing.
4 · US obligations Immediate: lift the naval blockade, prevent interference against Iran, restore traffic within 30 days to pre-war capacity proportional to Iran’s pre-war volume. US withdraws forces from surrounding areas within 30 days after the final agreement. Asymmetry: the blockade lifts at once, but force withdrawal waits on the final agreement — US forces stay through the 60-day window plus 30 days. “Proportional to Iran’s pre-war traffic” hands Iran the reopening pace.
5 · Iran obligations (Hormuz) Immediate: resume merchant shipping in the Gulf / Sea of Oman within 30 days to pre-war volume, removing technical obstacles and neutralising mines. Signed edit: a joint Iranian-Omani administration and a 60-day toll bar were added; IRGC media says fees resume after. Iran’s win — the text does not bar Iran from “managing” the strait; toll-free is time-boxed. Tested and not yet resolved: Oman’s own two-corridor proposal under this framework was rejected by Iran on 12 Jul, the co-administrator failing to secure agreement on the joint arrangement the clause specifies.
6 · $300bn development US and regional partners build a rehabilitation and development plan financing at least $300bn; the implementation mechanism is set within 60 days. External Gulf money, not a US outlay (Trump: “no money changes hands”; Reuters: no US government funds). Gulf-funded, routing the financing into the Gulf-state track.
7 · Sanctions US commits to ending all sanctions — UNSC resolutions, IAEA Board resolutions, all unilateral US primary and secondary sanctions — on a schedule set in the final agreement. Exceeds the JCPOA’s scope in principle, but the schedule is deferred to the final agreement — a commitment on record, not yet executed.
8 · Nuclear Iran reiterates it will never produce nuclear weapons; the fate of enriched material and all nuclear-related issues, including Iran’s “nuclear needs,” is “adequately addressed” in the final agreement. A restatement of Iran’s standing position, not a new concession. The version read aloud on signing adds on-site down-blending of the stockpile under IAEA supervision as the baseline method — gated to the final agreement and cutting against Iran’s simultaneous hardening of its HEU tunnels. The ~440kg core stays in Iran through the window. US officials told the Wall Street Journal (10 Jul) the US will not conclude a nuclear agreement unless Iran relinquishes its enriched material; Iran has given no indication it will.
9 · Status quo Pending the final agreement, Iran holds the status quo on its nuclear programme and the US imposes no new sanctions and does not strengthen its forces in the region. The strategic freeze: Iran’s programme stays intact through the 60 days while US leverage is capped. The most consequential clause for the deferral read.
10 · Oil waivers US Treasury issues waivers immediately after signing for Iranian crude, petrochemicals and derivatives, and related services (banking, insurance, transport). Signed edit tested: the waiver was revoked on 8 Jul, seven weeks after signing, in response to the 6–7 Jul tanker attacks — the first rollback of a core MoU economic term. Revocable, as flagged; the risk is now realised rather than theoretical.
11 · Frozen assets Frozen and restricted Iranian funds released and made fully available in line with negotiation progress, directed by the Central Bank of Iran. Sequenced to negotiation progress — Iran’s leverage-extraction path. Vance conditions release on “verified steps” on the HEU; Vahidi insists the funds are not barred from military spending.
12 · Implementation A mechanism to oversee implementation and the commitment to the final agreement. Compliance architecture with no specified membership, voting or enforcement — to be designed in 60 days.
13 · Sequencing gate Nuclear negotiations begin only after Iran receives assurances on the implementation of Articles 4, 5, 10 and 11 (blockade, Hormuz, oil waivers, frozen assets); talks then address only the remaining articles. Iran’s verification gate, now in the text — it can delay the nuclear talks by contesting any of those four implementations. The Article 10 waiver revocation gives Iran fresh grounds to invoke this gate. It has not invoked it to halt contact outright: the Muscat talks (11 Jul) and Trump’s resume-talks statement (10 Jul) both landed inside this window.
14 · UNSC ratification The final agreement is approved through a binding UN Security Council resolution. China and Russia become structural guarantors — neither the US nor Israel can unilaterally exit the final deal without a UNSC crisis. Iran’s durability anchor.
Implementation — week 1

The first week of execution ran two ways. Hormuz (Art 4/5): oil flowed at the fastest pace since the war — Bloomberg ~20m barrels Fri–Sun, CENTCOM ~17m Sat, Chabahar cargoes clearing — yet the IRGC Navy announced a re-closure on 20 Jun conditioned on Israel halting Lebanon operations; CENTCOM rebutted that traffic still flows. Nuclear (Art 8/9): Iran agreed to readmit IAEA inspectors; the ~440kg HEU stays in Iran. Sequencing (Art 13): Iran gated the nuclear file behind delivery of the ceasefire, blockade-lift, Hormuz, oil-waiver and frozen-asset clauses, banking ~$12bn of relief up front. Lebanon (Art 1): now Iran’s declared top priority, with the 19 Jun Israel–Hezbollah ceasefire already violated on both sides.

Implementation — week 2

Week 2 turned kinetic. Hormuz (Art 4/5): after crossings surged to a post-war high (~73 on Wed, ~125 on the week per Lloyd’s), an Iranian drone struck the Ever Lovely (25 Jun); the IMO paused its ~600-ship evacuation and Friday traffic halved as Iran’s maritime authority warned that transit outside its designated routes is not covered by safe passage. US–Iran exchange: CENTCOM struck Iranian missile, drone-storage and radar sites in response (26–27 Jun); Iran hit US bases in Bahrain and Kuwait (28 Jun). Sovereignty (Art 5): Araghchi asserts Iran is “solely responsible” for the strait, while the Article text mandates dialogue with Oman and the Gulf littorals; Oman signals service fees and the US and GCC reject tolls — the post-60-day fee regime is being contested early. Nuclear (Art 8/9): the IAEA-inspection dispute is unresolved and Iran has signalled it could suspend talks.

Implementation — week 3

Hormuz (Art 4/5): Iran missiled a Qatari gas carrier and a Saudi tanker on 6 Jul for using non-Iranian-approved transit routes, then droned a third vessel on 7 Jul — the largest single-day attack volume since signing; JMIC raised the threat level from substantial to severe. Iran separately rejected a UAE-brokered IMO proposal to manage the strait, reasserting that transit must follow Iranian arrangements, while Saudi Arabia, Kuwait and the UAE advance pipeline plans to bypass Hormuz entirely and NATO foreign ministers took up the issue with Gulf counterparts in Ankara. Oil waivers (Art 10): the US revoked the waiver permitting Iranian oil sales on 8 Jul, giving Iran until 17 Jul to wind down transactions — the first rollback of a core MoU economic term since signing. Nuclear (Art 8/9): unchanged this window. 60-day window (Art 3): the indirect Doha track concluded with no headway, and Trump told reporters in Ankara the MoU is “over” and he does not want to deal with Tehran — the sharpest test yet of whether the window holds, though it is his statement alone, with no reciprocal declaration from Iran. Sequencing (Art 13): talks are paused for the late Supreme Leader Khamenei’s funeral (burial 9 Jul, Mashhad).

Implementation — week 4 · v10

Hormuz (Art 4/5): the US struck 90 targets on 8 Jul, including a maritime traffic-control tower at Chabahar and rail/bridge infrastructure (Golestan Province) used to move Russian- and Chinese-sourced military material; Iran retaliated against Kuwait, Bahrain and Qatar on 9 Jul. Iranian and Omani officials met in Muscat on 11 Jul, where Oman proposed a two-corridor arrangement; Iran rejected it on 12 Jul, unable to secure Tehran’s approval. The same day CENTCOM struck ~140 targets (300+ over three nights), reaching central Iranian provinces for the first time, and Iran retaliated against five Gulf states — Bahrain, Kuwait, Jordan, Qatar and Oman. A 13 Jul press report has Iran claiming it has closed the strait outright, with the US disputing this; unconfirmed by a primary source as of this tab. Nuclear (Art 8/9): both sides now state the Hormuz-nuclear linkage explicitly — Iran won’t discuss its programme without recognised strait control; the US won’t conclude a deal without Iran relinquishing its enriched material. 60-day window (Art 3): Trump said 10 Jul the US agreed to resume talks at Iran’s request, even while maintaining the ceasefire is over; the Muscat meeting the next day is the first concrete sign of that. Sequencing (Art 13): not invoked to halt contact this window.

05 · Stakeholder map

Actor positions through the Stalemate / Resolution lens.

The Roaring Lion A/B/C framework is the analytical reference: Stalemate ≈ persistent Bad B; Resolution ≈ Good A via JPOA 2.0 + Hormuz Council + Security Guarantee. The US cell moves again on the resumed-talks statement and the third, deeper strike wave; the Iran cell sharpens on the chain-of-command finding and the Oman-corridor rejection; the GCC cell widens on Jordan and Oman now also being struck.

United States
The US version of the terms — dismantle the nuclear programme, end Axis support — is far harder than Iran’s, so the signed ceasefire still sits on an unreconciled core. Vance led the first 60-day-track round in Switzerland; Trump warned Iran “you won’t have a country” if it re-closes the strait, floated a US takeover of the waterway with a US-charged toll, and threatened to “hit Iran very hard again” if Hezbollah is not reined in. That threat was carried out once already in late June; US CENTCOM struck Iranian missile, drone-storage and coastal-radar sites on 26–27 Jun in response to the Ever Lovely attack, and both sides stood down by 29–30 Jun. A second exchange landed on 6–8 Jul: after Iran’s worst single day of tanker attacks since the MoU, the US struck Iran again and, separately, revoked the waiver permitting Iranian oil sales, giving Iran until 17 July to wind down transactions — the first withdrawal of a core economic term of the deal since signing. A US official framed continued deal benefits as conditional on Iranian “good behaviour”; a second official maintained negotiators remain in good faith. That restraint did not last the day: the Doha indirect track concluded with no headway, and at the NATO summit in Ankara Trump said “I think it’s over. I don’t want to deal with them,” calling Iran’s leadership “scum” and “sick people” and the process “a waste of time.” It is the sharpest US-side statement of the cycle, and the first delivered by Trump personally. This file’s pattern to date has been hardline rhetoric that later gave way to continued contact. On 10 Jul that pattern repeated: Trump said the US agreed to resume talks at Iran’s request, even while repeating that the ceasefire is over. The US struck again the next day, and harder — roughly 140 targets on 11 Jul (300+ over three nights, CENTCOM says), expanding geographically into central Iran for the first time rather than the coastal sites hit in the first two rounds. Israel Hayom reports an internal administration split behind the strike decisions: Witkoff, Kushner and Vance favouring continued negotiation against Hegseth, Bessent, Rubio and Ratcliffe; Trump has been weighing continued talks, a partial sanctions squeeze, or a full naval blockade (assessed as achievable in roughly two weeks) as alternative tracks. Talking and striking are running concurrently: the resume-talks statement and the largest strike wave of the war landed a day apart.
Talks resumed ·
struck deeper
Israel
Citrinowicz: intent intact, capacity to disrupt constrained. A “complete end to the war in Lebanon” is an explicit Iranian red line, so Israel’s Lebanon campaign is a direct obstacle to the deal; the signed Clause 1 guarantees Lebanese sovereignty, which Tehran tells Hezbollah obliges a phased Israeli withdrawal, while Israel is not a party and remains in southern Lebanon. The 19 Jun Israel–Hezbollah ceasefire failed on both sides within the day, and the IDF kept striking. A trilateral Israel–Lebanon–US framework followed on 27 Jun: Israel keeps a security zone along the yellow line and withdraws from two pilot zones for Lebanese-army-led Hezbollah disarmament, while Hezbollah rejects the terms. This is a de-escalation step on the Lebanon front, and it does not move the spoiler read; there is no unilateral strike on Iran proper. Regime change stays off the Resolution menu; Netanyahu’s stake in a US–Iran deal failing is unchanged. Israeli sources quoted by Nadav Eyal (Between US) frame the 6–9 Jul round as vindication — “we told you so” — and expect not a full resumption of the war but a prolonged “war between the wars”: periodic US-Iran blows, attempts to maintain some understanding, repeating for months. The same sources say Israel does not want the MoU to collapse outright, for now, partly given the Netanyahu government’s own stake in the war not being read as a failure ahead of elections called for 27 Oct. This reads consistently with the widening 8–12 Jul round: still no direct Israeli strike on Iran.
Good A/C →
regime change
Iran
Fractured — and the hardline camp is winning the fracture. Iran’s primary Hormuz objective is recognised sovereignty over the strait; signed Clause 5 writes that claim into the text, with fees barred for 60 days only and reopened with the Gulf states after. The factional split is overt, the delegation slow-walks the nuclear file behind Clause 13, and IRGC-aligned media reports the Supreme Leader is “not fully” behind the MoU. In late June Iran moved from announcing the re-closure lever to using force — two ship strikes, then drone and ballistic-missile strikes on US bases in Bahrain and Kuwait, warning Gulf states against hosting US attacks — while framing the Doha follow-up as implementation talks, not final-agreement negotiation. On 6–7 Jul Iran escalated again, missiling two tankers and droning a third for using non-Iranian-approved routes — its largest single-day attack volume since the MoU — and rejected a UAE-brokered IMO proposal to manage the strait, insisting transit follow Iranian arrangements. It vows “decisive actions” against the 8 Jul US strikes and waiver revocation. The leverage stays checked: Gulf states are advancing pipeline plans to bypass Hormuz entirely, and the open Omani route continues to draw traffic away from Iran’s scheme. Those “decisive actions” were the 9 Jul strikes on Kuwait, Bahrain and Qatar. ISW assesses the strait attacks were a chain-of-command decision — top IRGC officers close to the supreme leader approved the use of force — contradicting Iranian negotiators’ account of an “errant faction.” Araghchi, Ghalibaf and Pezeshkian kept working the diplomatic track regardless, including the 11 Jul Muscat meeting with Oman. Two senior Iranian sources told Reuters that relinquishing strait control would amount to “surrender,” and Iran would not discuss its nuclear programme unless the US recognises full Iranian management of the strait. On 12 Jul Iran rejected Oman’s two-corridor proposal and retaliated against five Gulf states in total — Bahrain, Kuwait, Jordan, Qatar and Oman. Maj. Gen. Mohsen Rezaei, the Supreme Leader’s military adviser, said the strait is “more important than dozens of atomic bombs.” A 13 Jul press report has Iran claiming the strait is now closed outright, disputed by the US — not yet corroborated by a primary source.
Hardliners up ·
using force
China
Publicly pushes Resolution (ceasefire, lanes reopened, Xi/Putin Beijing meeting), but the pivot is partly performative — PRC kept sending missile components and fuel precursors during the war. Per Pape, the war already shifted Beijing’s read of US resolve; munitions depletion (FT, Global21) reinforces it.
Pushes Resolution
Russia
Aligned with China publicly; has formally offered to take Iran’s HEU (acknowledged by Araghchi, “not currently under discussion”). Accepting HEU under the Security Guarantee architecture would be the technical pathway. Preserves Iran ties privately (drones via Caspian).
HEU custodian role available
GCC / Pakistan
Mediation delivered the ceasefire — Pakistan’s PM announced the deal first, Qatar brokered a reported $12bn financial channel, and the UAE reopened a direct security line to Tehran; the 21–22 Jun Bürgenstock round was quadrilateral, with Qatar and Pakistan mediating. All GCC bar Oman reject permanent Iranian control of Hormuz; signed Clause 5 requires Iran to negotiate the post-60-day fee regime with Oman and the other Gulf states, who ISW judges unlikely to acquiesce without Iranian coercion. The US and GCC jointly rejected any Iranian tolls or strait control on 25 Jun; Iran’s 28 Jun strikes on Bahrain read as coercion of the Gulf hosts. By 30 Jun Oman, meeting Macron in Paris, reaffirmed freedom of navigation “without conditions,” and the open Omani route keeps drawing traffic. The UAE has now taken the dispute multilateral, proposing an IMO plan for managing the strait that Iran rejected — the first attempt to institutionalise strait governance outside direct US-Iran or Iran-Oman channels. Saudi Arabia, Kuwait and the UAE are separately advancing pipeline plans to bypass Hormuz altogether, and NATO foreign ministers met Bahraini, Kuwaiti, Qatari and Emirati counterparts on the strait issue in Ankara on 7 Jul. Oman moved from reaffirming free navigation to actively brokering a compromise — the 11 Jul two-corridor proposal — and was rebuffed the next day, the clearest sign yet that even the friendliest Gulf channel cannot extract a concession Iran isn’t offering. The bloc’s exposure widened directly: Jordan and Oman joined Bahrain, Kuwait and Qatar as targets of Iranian retaliation on 12 Jul, the first time the retaliation footprint has reached beyond the immediate Gulf littoral. The Saudi–Pakistan airspace cross-promise still binds Israeli unilateral action.
Active mediators
Europe
Aligns with the diplomatic track; refused the US request to reopen the Strait, so it is constrained from leading any post-war architecture. A NATO contribution to the HEU Security Guarantee would be the test. Switzerland hosted the formal ceremony and the first implementation round; the post-war security architecture would sit with other parties.
Diplomatic backer
Japan / Korea
Lean on the US for Resolution; ~60% of energy via Hormuz. The US warned Japan of up-to-two-year Tomahawk delays as the Pentagon prioritises Middle East restocking — US overstretch landing on an Indo-Pacific ally, reinforcing the hedge. Dormant nuclear conversations re-open if the scenario shifts to apex.
Hedging

The 8 Jun – 12 Jul: whipsaw, signing, and three armed exchanges of rising scale

8–17 Jun
Ceasefire collapses then resolves onto de-escalation: Israeli and US strikes inside Iran (8–10 Jun); Trump declares the deal “complete” (15 Jun); the US and Iran sign the MoU two days early (17 Jun), with signed edits to Clause 1 (Lebanon) and Clause 5 (Hormuz: joint Iranian-Omani administration, fees barred for 60 days). The blockade lifts and oil sanctions waive on signing.
19–22 Jun
A separate Israel–Hezbollah ceasefire is announced and violated within the day; Iran’s IRGC Navy announces a re-closure of the strait tied to Lebanon, which CENTCOM rebuts as flow continues. The first 60-day-track round meets at Bürgenstock (Vance–Araghchi, Qatar/Pakistan mediating) and reaffirms the roadmap; Bloomberg tracks Hormuz flow at the fastest pace since the war; Brent falls to ~$79.
24–30 Jun
Oil falls to its pre-war anchor (~$73). An Iranian drone strikes the Ever Lovely (25 Jun); US CENTCOM strikes Iranian missile, drone-storage and radar sites in response (26–27 Jun); Iran fires drones and ballistic missiles at US bases in Bahrain and Kuwait (28 Jun) — the first armed exchange under the MoU. Both sides stand down by 29–30 Jun; a disputed Doha implementation track opens on 30 Jun.
6–8 Jul
Iran missiles a Qatari gas carrier and a Saudi-flagged tanker (6 Jul), then drones a third tanker (7 Jul) — the largest single-day Hormuz attack volume since the MoU; JMIC raises the threat level to severe. Iran rejects a UAE-brokered IMO strait-management proposal. The US launches fresh airstrikes and revokes the oil-sale waiver (8 Jul), giving Iran until 17 Jul to wind down transactions; the Doha track fails and Trump calls the MoU “over” at the NATO Ankara summit. Talks pause for Khamenei’s funeral (burial 9 Jul, Mashhad).
9 Jul
Iran retaliates against Kuwait, Bahrain and Qatar, widening from the two-state pattern of the first exchange.
10 Jul
Trump says the US agreed to resume talks with Iran at Iran’s request, while maintaining the ceasefire is over. US officials tell the Wall Street Journal no nuclear deal is possible unless Iran relinquishes its enriched material; Iranian sources tell Reuters ceding strait control would be “surrender.”
11 Jul
Iranian and Omani officials meet in Muscat; Oman proposes a two-corridor Hormuz arrangement. The IRGC Navy strikes the Cyprus-flagged GFS Galaxy. CENTCOM strikes roughly 140 Iranian military targets — 300+ over three nights, per CENTCOM — expanding for the first time into central Iran (Kerman, Lorestan, Markazi, Kohgiluyeh and Boyer Ahmad).
12 Jul
Iran rejects the Omani corridor proposal, unable to secure Tehran’s approval. Iran retaliates with missiles and drones against US facilities in five Gulf states — Bahrain, Kuwait, Jordan, Qatar and Oman. Supreme Leader Military Adviser Maj. Gen. Mohsen Rezaei calls the strait “more important than dozens of atomic bombs.”
13 Jul
Press reports (unconfirmed by a primary source as of this tab) have Iran claiming it has closed the strait outright; the US says it remains open.
What it confirms: the off-ramp produced a signed ceasefire that is executing, on an unreconciled core, and the diplomatic channel has not closed even as strikes widen. What it does not change: the bifurcated architecture, the apex held in the long-term allocation, the Murrin DD, the Brent bands. Watch-list: a third confirmed-strike escalation in this run, again US-Iran; the Israeli-strike trigger has still not fired. The mediation-breakdown trigger, the closest it came to firing in v9, recedes rather than firing outright this window — talks resumed (Trump, 10 Jul) and a mediation channel produced an actual proposal (Muscat, 11 Jul), even though Iran rejected it and the strikes kept widening. The live channels are unchanged: contested Hormuz governance and the toll regime, the IAEA dispute, and the Lebanon front. The Hormuz-closure claim reported 13 Jul sits outside this window’s confirmed record and is flagged, not adopted.
The map — a third kinetic test, wider but not deeper in kind

Stalemate stays modal because the US–Iran nuclear divide is wide and the hardliner faction blocks Good A. The ceasefire is a Stage-1 instrument: it ends the shooting, lifts the blockade and reopens Hormuz, while the nuclear core, the HEU stockpile and sanctions sequencing are pushed to a 60-day track. The first armed exchange under the MoU showed the deal could turn kinetic without breaking; the market priced that violation as contained. A second, larger exchange on 6–8 Jul confirmed the pattern: Hormuz keeps producing kinetic tests, and this time the US answered with an economic rollback as well. An 8–12 Jul continuation is a third round of the same pattern, the widest yet: strikes reaching central Iran, retaliation reaching five Gulf states, an Omani compromise attempted and rejected. In scale it moves the picture; in kind it stays the same conventional Hormuz-control contest the first two rounds were. The open question v9 flagged has a partial answer: Trump’s “over” gave way to a resume-talks statement within 48 hours, and to an actual Omani-brokered proposal within 72. The unresolved question is unchanged: whether the deferred cores settle inside the 60-day window or return as binding at its end. Iran’s rejection of Oman’s specific proposal moves neither toward settlement. Talks continuing anyway, through a fourth week of near-continuous strikes, is the one new data point on that question.

06 · Market & thematic implications

Scenario views across equities, Brent, rates, and rotation.

Views only. Brent bands and the scenario architecture are unchanged from v9; the 8–12 Jul continuation pushed oil further from the pre-war anchor, per Barclays, though no settled print has reached this desk.

ScenarioEquitiesBrentRatesSector & thematic rotation
Stalemate
Modal		 Mid-teens downside; EPS holds until ~2027 demand destruction. US indices fell on the 8 Jul news (Dow -0.3%, S&P -0.5%, Nasdaq -1.2%, chipmakers leading); Asian markets were mixed (Hang Seng higher on financials, Topix and ASX lower on the crude jump). Tight, elevated, drawing inventory. Per Carlyle, the move down through late June was destocking-driven — US SPR near its operational floor, Cushing near the settlement floor — not a fundamentals rebalance, and fragile to any reopening slippage. That fragility showed on 6–8 Jul: oil rose more than 3% intraday on the tanker attacks alone (European gas futures +7%), with a further leg on the waiver-revocation news — no settled print has reached this desk yet, but the direction is a genuine re-tightening away from the ~$73 pre-war anchor. Barclays (read this window) puts the immediate 6–8 Jul move at roughly +6% and expects a $65–80 range over coming months — below this file’s $90–110 Resolution-quick floor, on OPEC supply increases, a UAE/Saudi price contest, and Chinese reserve-release discretion — a watch item against this file's own band, pending a settled print. The 8–12 Jul continuation adds further upward pressure per the same source; still no settled print for this desk. The SPR buffer is still thin at ~325.7m barrels, the lowest since 1983, against a JPMorgan-cited ~$150 re-closure shock. Fiscal pressure lifts yields; energy tightening Jul–Sep adds to the curve. Iran-axis exposure screens unattractive on the retaliation vectors (UAE data centres, regional fintech, Gulf cable-dependent payments) — now including Jordan and Oman as directly-struck hosts. China sleeve re-rates up on the Beijing read of US weakness; munitions depletion (FT, Global21) is the observable marker. Energy-paradigm shift favours rare earths, security and infrastructure; defensive on regional refining. A non-oil vector: Iran is extending the transit-control playbook to the Europe–Asia undersea cables — IRGC-linked demands for transit fees on the hyperscalers and veiled threats to the fibre, reaching toward SWIFT. Low feasibility, but it sharpens the cable-dependent-payments screen.

Improving		 Mid-single-digit upside. Declines toward $90–110 over months; market below the band — ~$84 on the ceasefire, ~$73 by 29 Jun at the pre-war anchor — pushed back up by the 6–8 Jul escalation, direction confirmed though no settled print yet — and again by the 8–12 Jul continuation; Barclays’ $65–80 forward call sits below this band, flagged for reconciliation once a settled print lands — pricing a clean reopening the substance does not yet support; trust rebuild takes quarters. Iran-axis exposure screens better selectively; the energy/renewables opportunity set persists on the structural energy-paradigm shift; the China sleeve recalibrates on a firmer Hormuz-access outcome.

Apex / tail: escalation toward $130–160+ Brent is held in the long-term allocation; first-use ($200+, SPX ~3,500) sits below the scenario-modelling threshold as structural tail protection. The deal re-times this tail to the 60-day nuclear window rather than removing it — the HEU stays in Iran and is being hardened. The re-closure scenario the market is now pricing as a ~$150 shock is the near-term, non-nuclear face of this escalation leg. The Taiwan-war and US-default extrapolations are held as backdrop only, outside the modelled set.

The $300bn in the signed terms is external Gulf investment funded by sanctions relief — for instance a UAE-built power plant in Iran — not a US Treasury outlay, which reconciles the terms with Iran’s frozen-asset demand; the relief flows through GCC capital.

Resolution mechanics

The Resolution path follows the framework David specified: Mearsheimer’s “humble pie” off-ramp; a JPOA 2.0 nuclear architecture; the Ferguson Hormuz Council (Iran as Chair, a 45% / 30% / balance revenue split, transit fees at cost); HEU export under a Security Guarantee with Russia available as custodian; and a post-war Gulf Treaty Organisation with China and Russia as signatories. The realised Stage-1 MOU diverges from the Council mechanic on Hormuz: rather than a chaired revenue split with fees at cost, the strait reopens toll-free but under Iranian and Omani management — Iran’s sovereignty claim conceded operationally, the revenue-sharing design bypassed, with fees barred for 60 days then talks with the Gulf states. The deeper cores the framework was built to settle — HEU ~440kg, enrichment, sanctions sequencing — remain for the 60-day track. The Hormuz governance the framework was built to settle is being contested by force: Iran asserts Article-5 “sole responsibility” and strikes vessels that use the Omani route, Oman signals service fees, and the US and GCC reject tolls — the fee-regime negotiation the MoU deferred is opening under fire. Iran’s rejection of the UAE’s IMO strait-management proposal fits the same logic: control of the mechanism matters more to Iran than the toll income itself, which is why a multilateral body is a harder concession than a bilateral one with Oman. Oman’s own two-corridor proposal tested that logic directly on 11–12 Jul and was rejected on the same grounds: Iran held out against its own co-administrator under the signed Clause 5 framework, the same resistance it showed the UAE’s multilateral alternative.

v11 · Blockade reinstated, strikes reach inland, diplomatic channel goes quiet · 16 Jul 2026
Architecture & Brent bands held · US chip flipped · Iran chip flipped · mediation-breakdown watch-list fires · strikes reach ~200km inland for the first time

The architecture holds — Stalemate (modal) / Resolution (improving) / apex in the long-term allocation. The exchange that opened 8 Jul did not close: on 14 Jul the US reinstated a blockade targeting Iranian-flagged vessels and Iranian cargo, briefly proposed then dropped a 20% Hormuz transit fee in favour of pledged Gulf-state investment deals, and kept striking — by 15 Jul those strikes reached roughly 200km inland for the first time, beyond the coastal target sets of every prior round. Iran kept striking vessels on the Omani route through 13–14 Jul and on 16 Jul declared the strait an “inviolable red line,” warning it would strike infrastructure across the Gulf region if the US carries out its own threat against Iranian infrastructure. Two chips move on it.

What moved from v10:

  • US chip flips: “Talks resumed · struck deeper” → “Blockade back · strikes reach inland.” The naval blockade returned 14 Jul, targeted at Iranian-flagged shipping and Iranian cargo rather than a full closure; the 20% fee lasted about a day before Trump replaced it with pledged Gulf investment; strikes continued through a third consecutive night and, by 15 Jul, reached roughly 200km inland for the first time.
  • Iran chip flips: “Hardliners up · using force” → “Hardliners up · vows Gulf-wide retaliation.” Iran struck three vessels on the Omani route 13 Jul (one death, eight wounded), retaliated against Kuwait, Bahrain and Jordan 14 Jul with a possible first use of a cluster-munition warhead (unverified), and on 16 Jul named the strait an “inviolable red line,” warning it would hit infrastructure across the Gulf region if the US strikes Iranian infrastructure.
  • Mediation-breakdown watch-list fires. ISW’s own 13 Jul assessment states the ceasefire “has effectively ended”; the Iran–Oman weekend talks that followed the 11 Jul Muscat meeting produced no progress, and diplomatic channels went quiet through the weekend — a reversal of v10’s read that talks were continuing regardless of the strikes.
  • A Yemen dimension opens. US-backed Saudi strikes hit Houthi-controlled Sanaa airport 13 Jul; the Houthis warned commercial airlines off Saudi airspace 14 Jul — a second-front risk running alongside the direct US-Iran exchange.
  • Iran's deterrence is working without full-scale force. Shipping companies and tanker captains are reportedly avoiding the Omani route despite US escorts — individual strikes are enough to redirect traffic, advancing Iran's control objective short of closing the strait outright.
  • New sanctions layer on top of the blockade. The US Treasury added over 50 individuals, entities and vessels to sanctions on 14–15 Jul, targeting the Shamkhani family's oil-smuggling network and IRGC weapons procurement — a second Article 7 reversal alongside Article 4, both explicitly framed by the US as economic pressure.
  • Lebanon front advances, the one de-escalatory data point. Israeli and Lebanese officials reportedly agreed the structure of a Rome-brokered “pilot zones” plan (14–15 Jul) for a phased Lebanese-army deployment and Israeli withdrawal — the first forward movement since the 27 Jun framework, though scope and timeline stay unresolved.

The architecture, the verdict, the Brent bands and the apex placement are unchanged.

Inputs (13–16 Jul intake): Bloomberg (Gary via Eric) — the 14 Jul blockade reinstatement and 20% fee proposal, and the 14 Jul fee withdrawal in favour of Gulf investment deals · ISW Iran Updates, 13–15 Jul (Gary via Eric / LSP, full 15 Jul report read direct) — the ceasefire-has-effectively-ended assessment, the three-vessel strait attacks and cluster-munition claim, the Kuwait/Bahrain/Jordan retaliation, the Sanaa airport strike and Houthi airspace warning, the first inland strikes (Iranshahr, Saravan) with casualty figures, the Erbil drone incident, the new Treasury sanctions tranche, and the Rome Lebanon pilot-zones progress · Ian Bremmer / GZERO (Gary via Eric) — the stalled Iran-Oman talks, the traffic-collapse and Brent read, and the Bosphorus/Malacca-style long-run Hormuz-governance framing · Reuters (16 Jul) — Trump's own wording on the blockade's scope (Iranian-flagged vessels and cargo only) and Iran's fresh “red line” warning on Gulf-wide retaliation

Yellow marks what is new or changed in v11 — the blockade reinstatement and its targeted scope, the fee proposal and withdrawal, the inland strikes, the Kuwait/Bahrain/Jordan retaliation and cluster-munition claim, the Yemen dimension, the stalled diplomatic channel, and Iran's Gulf-wide red-line warning. The scenario architecture, the other actor chips, the signed-MoU text, the Resolution mechanics, the Murrin due diligence and the Brent bands carry from v10 unmarked.

Bottom line · blockade back, diplomatic channel stalls

The scenario set stays bifurcated: Stalemate (modal) and Resolution (improving probability), with the apex tail held in the long-term allocation below the scenario-modelling threshold. The US reinstated a naval blockade targeting Iranian-flagged shipping and Iranian cargo on 14 Jul, briefly floated then dropped a 20% Hormuz transit fee, and kept striking through a third consecutive night; by 15 Jul those strikes reached roughly 200km inland for the first time. Iran struck three more vessels on the Omani route (13 Jul, one death), retaliated against Kuwait, Bahrain and Jordan (14 Jul) with a possible first use of a cluster munition, and on 16 Jul declared the strait an “inviolable red line,” warning it would strike Gulf-wide infrastructure if the US hits Iranian infrastructure. ISW's own assessment is that the ceasefire has effectively ended, and the Iran-Oman diplomatic channel that produced the 11 Jul Muscat proposal went quiet over the following weekend — the mediation-breakdown watch-list trigger fires this round, a reversal of v10's read that talks were continuing regardless of the strikes. A US-backed Saudi strike on Houthi-controlled Sanaa airport and a Houthi airspace warning open a Yemen dimension running alongside the direct US-Iran exchange. The Israeli-strike trigger has still not fired; this remains a US-Iran exchange. Murrin’s Article 2 nuclear scenario remains the analytical foundation under structural tail protection; the apex stays re-timed to the 60-day window. Full scenario stances, drivers, equity and Brent views are in section 03.

01 · Due Diligence on Murrin

Verdict unchanged — cannot rule it out.

Capabilities corroborated (Tier 1); interpretive layers held as hypotheses (Tier 2); the policy conclusion treated as advocacy (Tier 3). The full claim-by-claim verifiability table sits in the v1 tab.

Murrin’s capabilities case is sound and OSINT-sourced. Timeline sits at the aggressive end of a 6–12 month band that overlaps Roaring Lion’s own assessment. The preemptive-nuclear-use conclusion is his advocacy. The Gallant “440 Kilograms” piece restates the ~440kg 60% HEU stockpile that anchors the capabilities case, and ISW, citing five US-intelligence sources via CNN, reports Iran is physically hardening that stockpile — collapsing tunnels and mining storage entrances to defeat any seizure — so the fissile core is intact, deferred, and being entrenched. Iran agreed to readmit IAEA inspectors, the first forward step on the nuclear file; it is not down-blending. That step has stalled: the inspection access is unresolved, with Grossi expecting entry while Iran ties it to a final deal and sanctions relief, and Iran has signalled it could suspend the talks over the armed exchange. The ~440kg core stays inside Iran behind the entrenched tunnels. “Cannot rule out the nuclear scenario” holds as the analytical foundation; the apex is handled via the long-term allocation rather than modelled scenarios.

02 · Scenario test

Live tail risk on a 6–12 month horizon — below the scenario-modelling threshold.

The conclusion is unchanged; the operational decision is to bear this tail in the long-term allocation rather than model it.

The apex first-use event is paradigm-break, so protection is structural rather than model-derived. As David noted, “in short, I probably don’t need to know what the damage would look like.” The deal does not lower the tail — it re-times it. The MOU defers the nuclear question to a 60-day negotiation while the HEU stays inside Iran, so the breakout clock moves from the battlefield to the talks table without resetting. That table is now live, and Iran is using it to bank its front-loaded wins: the first 60-day-track round met in Switzerland on 21–22 Jun, but Iran made opening the nuclear file conditional on the prior delivery of sanctions waivers, frozen-asset release and the Lebanon ceasefire. The 25–28 Jun armed exchange is the test case for kinetic escalation short of first-use — a ship strike, US retaliation on Iranian military sites, and Iranian strikes on US bases in Bahrain and Kuwait, all conventional, with the apex below threshold throughout. It sits in Stalemate-stress, below the apex. The 6–8 Jul exchange is a second instance of the same pattern — Iranian tanker strikes, a US military and economic response — conventional throughout, with the apex unmoved. The 8–12 Jul continuation is a third instance, larger in scale but the same kind of event — conventional strikes over strait control, not a step toward first-use. The 13–16 Jul window continues the same pattern at a fourth iteration: a reinstated blockade, inland strikes reaching Sistan and Baluchistan for the first time, and Iran's Gulf-wide red-line warning all raise the conventional stakes without touching the nuclear file directly — the ~440kg core is not mentioned in any of this window's exchanges, and the apex stays where it was. What stays in the long-term allocation is the nightmare convergence — nuclear Iran + reinvigorated Russia + hedging China + exhausted America + isolated Israel arriving together around summer 2026 as oil buffers run dry; Murrin Article 3’s late-stage US-decline thesis sits on the “exhausted America” leg as backdrop conviction, with a US Strategic Petroleum Reserve at a historically low ~325.7m barrels by 29 Jun, the lowest since 1983, monitored daily at the tip-of-iceberg layer.

Verdict

Murrin Article 2 cannot be ruled out and remains a live tail risk on a 6–12 month horizon. The apex probability sits below the threshold where another modelled scenario would change the prescription. Israeli unilateral action remains the most plausible trigger — and the first armed US-Iran exchange under the MoU showed the deal can turn kinetic through Hormuz directly; the strait and the Lebanon front are both live channels for an escalation that the long-term allocation is built to absorb. A second such exchange in early July confirmed Hormuz as a recurring, non-Israeli escalation channel; a third, larger round over 8–12 July reinforced the same read. A fourth round — the blockade's return, inland strikes, and Iran's Gulf-wide threat — extends the pattern further without changing it: eleven days become fifteen of near-continuous US-Iran friction, still without displacing Israeli unilateral action as the most plausible apex trigger, and still without drawing Israel directly into the exchange.
