04 · The signed MoU

What the text says — the milestone, Article by Article.

The 14-point text Bloomberg published on 16 June and the version a senior US official read aloud on signing (17 Jun); the Article text below is verbatim from those primary publications, carried as the reference for the 60-day track. The flag is our reading of what each Article concedes, defers, or leaves contested; the implementation boxes below track how the obligations have executed.

ArticleText (close paraphrase)Our reading
1 · Ceasefire Immediate and permanent end to the war on all fronts including Lebanon; no hostile actions; refrain from the threat or use of force; the final agreement confirms this. Signed edit: a Lebanese-sovereignty guarantee was added. Contested — Iran reads it as obliging an Israeli withdrawal; Israel is not a signatory and keeps striking.
2 · Sovereignty Both parties respect each other’s sovereignty and territorial integrity and refrain from interfering in internal affairs. Implicitly constrains US regime-change; the IRGC structure survives. Iran also leans on this to frame “managing” Hormuz as an internal affair.
3 · 60-day window Negotiate and reach a final agreement within a maximum of 60 days, extendable by mutual consent. The clock for every deferred core. “Extendable” means the pause can stretch if Iran judges its leverage growing.
4 · US obligations Immediate: lift the naval blockade, prevent interference against Iran, restore traffic within 30 days to pre-war capacity proportional to Iran’s pre-war volume. US withdraws forces from surrounding areas within 30 days after the final agreement. Asymmetry: the blockade lifts at once, but force withdrawal waits on the final agreement — US forces stay through the 60-day window plus 30 days. “Proportional to Iran’s pre-war traffic” hands Iran the reopening pace. Signed term tested and reversed: the US reinstated the blockade on 14 Jul, targeted at Iranian-flagged vessels and Iranian cargo rather than a full closure of the strait (Trump's own wording, via Reuters). A briefly floated 20% transit fee on other cargo was dropped the same week.
5 · Iran obligations (Hormuz) Immediate: resume merchant shipping in the Gulf / Sea of Oman within 30 days to pre-war volume, removing technical obstacles and neutralising mines. Signed edit: a joint Iranian-Omani administration and a 60-day toll bar were added; IRGC media says fees resume after. Iran’s win — the text does not bar Iran from “managing” the strait; toll-free is time-boxed. Tested and not yet resolved: Oman’s own two-corridor proposal under this framework was rejected by Iran on 12 Jul, the co-administrator failing to secure agreement on the joint arrangement the clause specifies. Iran struck three further vessels on the alternative Omani route on 13 Jul (one death, eight wounded); shipping companies and tanker captains are reportedly avoiding that route despite US escorts.
6 · $300bn development US and regional partners build a rehabilitation and development plan financing at least $300bn; the implementation mechanism is set within 60 days. External Gulf money, not a US outlay (Trump: “no money changes hands”; Reuters: no US government funds). Gulf-funded, routing the financing into the Gulf-state track.
7 · Sanctions US commits to ending all sanctions — UNSC resolutions, IAEA Board resolutions, all unilateral US primary and secondary sanctions — on a schedule set in the final agreement. Exceeds the JCPOA’s scope in principle, but the schedule is deferred to the final agreement — a commitment on record, not yet executed. Signed term tested in the other direction: the US Treasury added over 50 individuals, entities and vessels to sanctions on 14–15 Jul, targeting the Shamkhani family's oil-smuggling network and IRGC weapons procurement — new sanctions rather than the wind-down the Article commits to, explicitly framed by the US as pressure alongside the blockade.
8 · Nuclear Iran reiterates it will never produce nuclear weapons; the fate of enriched material and all nuclear-related issues, including Iran’s “nuclear needs,” is “adequately addressed” in the final agreement. A restatement of Iran’s standing position, not a new concession. The version read aloud on signing adds on-site down-blending of the stockpile under IAEA supervision as the baseline method — gated to the final agreement and cutting against Iran’s simultaneous hardening of its HEU tunnels. The ~440kg core stays in Iran through the window. US officials told the Wall Street Journal (10 Jul) the US will not conclude a nuclear agreement unless Iran relinquishes its enriched material; Iran has given no indication it will.
9 · Status quo Pending the final agreement, Iran holds the status quo on its nuclear programme and the US imposes no new sanctions and does not strengthen its forces in the region. The strategic freeze: Iran’s programme stays intact through the 60 days while US leverage is capped. The most consequential clause for the deferral read.
10 · Oil waivers US Treasury issues waivers immediately after signing for Iranian crude, petrochemicals and derivatives, and related services (banking, insurance, transport). Signed edit tested: the waiver was revoked on 8 Jul, seven weeks after signing, in response to the 6–7 Jul tanker attacks — the first rollback of a core MoU economic term. Revocable, as flagged; the risk is now realised rather than theoretical.
11 · Frozen assets Frozen and restricted Iranian funds released and made fully available in line with negotiation progress, directed by the Central Bank of Iran. Sequenced to negotiation progress — Iran’s leverage-extraction path. Vance conditions release on “verified steps” on the HEU; Vahidi insists the funds are not barred from military spending.
12 · Implementation A mechanism to oversee implementation and the commitment to the final agreement. Compliance architecture with no specified membership, voting or enforcement — to be designed in 60 days.
13 · Sequencing gate Nuclear negotiations begin only after Iran receives assurances on the implementation of Articles 4, 5, 10 and 11 (blockade, Hormuz, oil waivers, frozen assets); talks then address only the remaining articles. Iran’s verification gate, now in the text — it can delay the nuclear talks by contesting any of those four implementations. The Article 10 waiver revocation gives Iran fresh grounds to invoke this gate. It has not invoked it to halt contact outright: the Muscat talks (11 Jul) and Trump’s resume-talks statement (10 Jul) both landed inside the prior window. The blockade's 14 Jul return — a direct reversal of Article 4 — gives Iran a second, and stronger, ground to invoke the gate; whether it does so is unresolved, but the diplomatic channel's silence over the weekend of 12–13 Jul is consistent with it.
14 · UNSC ratification The final agreement is approved through a binding UN Security Council resolution. China and Russia become structural guarantors — neither the US nor Israel can unilaterally exit the final deal without a UNSC crisis. Iran’s durability anchor.
Implementation — week 1

The first week of execution ran two ways. Hormuz (Art 4/5): oil flowed at the fastest pace since the war — Bloomberg ~20m barrels Fri–Sun, CENTCOM ~17m Sat, Chabahar cargoes clearing — yet the IRGC Navy announced a re-closure on 20 Jun conditioned on Israel halting Lebanon operations; CENTCOM rebutted that traffic still flows. Nuclear (Art 8/9): Iran agreed to readmit IAEA inspectors; the ~440kg HEU stays in Iran. Sequencing (Art 13): Iran gated the nuclear file behind delivery of the ceasefire, blockade-lift, Hormuz, oil-waiver and frozen-asset clauses, banking ~$12bn of relief up front. Lebanon (Art 1): now Iran’s declared top priority, with the 19 Jun Israel–Hezbollah ceasefire already violated on both sides.

Implementation — week 2

Week 2 turned kinetic. Hormuz (Art 4/5): after crossings surged to a post-war high (~73 on Wed, ~125 on the week per Lloyd’s), an Iranian drone struck the Ever Lovely (25 Jun); the IMO paused its ~600-ship evacuation and Friday traffic halved as Iran’s maritime authority warned that transit outside its designated routes is not covered by safe passage. US–Iran exchange: CENTCOM struck Iranian missile, drone-storage and radar sites in response (26–27 Jun); Iran hit US bases in Bahrain and Kuwait (28 Jun). Sovereignty (Art 5): Araghchi asserts Iran is “solely responsible” for the strait, while the Article text mandates dialogue with Oman and the Gulf littorals; Oman signals service fees and the US and GCC reject tolls — the post-60-day fee regime is being contested early. Nuclear (Art 8/9): the IAEA-inspection dispute is unresolved and Iran has signalled it could suspend talks.

Implementation — week 3

Hormuz (Art 4/5): Iran missiled a Qatari gas carrier and a Saudi tanker on 6 Jul for using non-Iranian-approved transit routes, then droned a third vessel on 7 Jul — the largest single-day attack volume since signing; JMIC raised the threat level from substantial to severe. Iran separately rejected a UAE-brokered IMO proposal to manage the strait, reasserting that transit must follow Iranian arrangements, while Saudi Arabia, Kuwait and the UAE advance pipeline plans to bypass Hormuz entirely and NATO foreign ministers took up the issue with Gulf counterparts in Ankara. Oil waivers (Art 10): the US revoked the waiver permitting Iranian oil sales on 8 Jul, giving Iran until 17 Jul to wind down transactions — the first rollback of a core MoU economic term since signing. Nuclear (Art 8/9): unchanged this window. 60-day window (Art 3): the indirect Doha track concluded with no headway, and Trump told reporters in Ankara the MoU is “over” and he does not want to deal with Tehran — the sharpest test yet of whether the window holds, though it is his statement alone, with no reciprocal declaration from Iran. Sequencing (Art 13): talks are paused for the late Supreme Leader Khamenei’s funeral (burial 9 Jul, Mashhad).

Implementation — week 4

Hormuz (Art 4/5): the US struck 90 targets on 8 Jul, including a maritime traffic-control tower at Chabahar and rail/bridge infrastructure (Golestan Province) used to move Russian- and Chinese-sourced military material; Iran retaliated against Kuwait, Bahrain and Qatar on 9 Jul. Iranian and Omani officials met in Muscat on 11 Jul, where Oman proposed a two-corridor arrangement; Iran rejected it on 12 Jul, unable to secure Tehran’s approval. The same day CENTCOM struck ~140 targets (300+ over three nights), reaching central Iranian provinces for the first time, and Iran retaliated against five Gulf states — Bahrain, Kuwait, Jordan, Qatar and Oman. Nuclear (Art 8/9): both sides now state the Hormuz-nuclear linkage explicitly — Iran won’t discuss its programme without recognised strait control; the US won’t conclude a deal without Iran relinquishing its enriched material. 60-day window (Art 3): Trump said 10 Jul the US agreed to resume talks at Iran’s request, even while maintaining the ceasefire is over; the Muscat meeting the next day is the first concrete sign of that. Sequencing (Art 13): not invoked to halt contact this window.

Implementation — week 5 · v11

Blockade (Art 4): the US reinstated the naval blockade on 14 Jul, targeted at Iranian-flagged vessels and Iranian cargo rather than a full closure (Trump's own wording, via Reuters). A 20% transit fee on other cargo, proposed 13 Jul, was withdrawn 14 Jul in favour of pledged Gulf-state investment. Hormuz (Art 5): Iran struck three vessels on the Omani route 13 Jul (two Emirati tankers conducting shuttle runs, one death, eight wounded); shipping companies and tanker captains are reportedly avoiding that route despite US escorts. Strikes: a third consecutive night of US strikes was confirmed 14 Jul; by 15 Jul strikes reached roughly 200km inland for the first time — an Artesh barracks in Iranshahr (7 killed, 13 injured, per Iranian regime media) and a second IRGC site in Saravan, both in Sistan and Baluchistan. Iran retaliated against Kuwait, Bahrain and Jordan on 14–15 Jul, with a possible first use of a cluster-munition warhead against Bahrain (ISW unable to verify); Artesh separately claims eight waves of drone strikes against a US base in Jordan, and Iraqi media reports a further six-drone attack on Erbil airport and the US consulate there, attribution unclear. Sanctions (Art 7): the US Treasury added over 50 individuals, entities and vessels to sanctions on 14 Jul, targeting the Shamkhani family's Iranian oil-export and sanctions-evasion network, with a further tranche on 15 Jul for IRGC weapons procurement — new sanctions layered on top of the Article 7 commitment to end them, explicitly framed by the US as economic pressure alongside the blockade. Yemen: US-backed Saudi strikes hit Houthi-controlled Sanaa airport 13 Jul; the Houthis warned commercial airlines off Saudi airspace 14 Jul. Lebanon (Art 1): Israeli and Lebanese officials reportedly agreed the structure of a Rome-brokered “pilot zones” plan (14–15 Jul) for a phased Lebanese Armed Forces deployment and Israeli withdrawal from two zones under the 27 Jun Trilateral Framework — a rare forward step on this front, though timeline, scope and whether the LAF can search private property for Hezbollah weapons remain unresolved. Sequencing (Art 13) / 60-day window (Art 3): the Iran-Oman channel that produced the 11 Jul Muscat proposal went quiet over the following weekend, with no reported progress; on 16 Jul Iran called the strait an “inviolable red line” and warned it would strike infrastructure across the Gulf region if the US carries out its own threat against Iranian infrastructure.

05 · Stakeholder map

Actor positions through the Stalemate / Resolution lens.

The Roaring Lion A/B/C framework is the analytical reference: Stalemate ≈ persistent Bad B; Resolution ≈ Good A via JPOA 2.0 + Hormuz Council + Security Guarantee. The US and Iran cells both move — the US on the blockade's return and the inland strikes, Iran on the Gulf-wide red-line warning; the GCC cell widens further on the Yemen/Houthi dimension.

United States
The US version of the terms — dismantle the nuclear programme, end Axis support — is far harder than Iran’s, so the signed ceasefire still sits on an unreconciled core. Vance led the first 60-day-track round in Switzerland; Trump warned Iran “you won’t have a country” if it re-closes the strait, floated a US takeover of the waterway with a US-charged toll, and threatened to “hit Iran very hard again” if Hezbollah is not reined in. That threat was carried out once already in late June; US CENTCOM struck Iranian missile, drone-storage and coastal-radar sites on 26–27 Jun in response to the Ever Lovely attack, and both sides stood down by 29–30 Jun. A second exchange landed on 6–8 Jul: after Iran’s worst single day of tanker attacks since the MoU, the US struck Iran again and, separately, revoked the waiver permitting Iranian oil sales, giving Iran until 17 July to wind down transactions — the first withdrawal of a core economic term of the deal since signing. The Doha indirect track concluded with no headway, and at the NATO summit in Ankara Trump said “I think it’s over. I don’t want to deal with them,” calling Iran’s leadership “scum” and “sick people” and the process “a waste of time” — the sharpest US-side statement of the cycle. This file’s pattern to date has been hardline rhetoric that later gave way to continued contact, and on 10 Jul that pattern repeated: Trump said the US agreed to resume talks at Iran’s request, even while repeating that the ceasefire is over. The US struck again the next day, and harder — roughly 140 targets on 11 Jul (300+ over three nights, CENTCOM says), expanding geographically into central Iran for the first time. Israel Hayom reports an internal administration split behind the strike decisions: Witkoff, Kushner and Vance favouring continued negotiation against Hegseth, Bessent, Rubio and Ratcliffe; Trump has been weighing continued talks, a partial sanctions squeeze, or a full naval blockade (assessed as achievable in roughly two weeks) as alternative tracks. On 13–14 Jul, Trump chose the blockade track: the US reinstated a blockade on Iranian-flagged shipping and Iranian cargo effective 14 Jul, briefly proposed a 20% transit fee on other cargo, and dropped it a day later for pledged Gulf-state investment deals instead. Strikes continued through a third consecutive night and, by 15 Jul, reached roughly 200km inland for the first time — an Artesh barracks in Iranshahr and a second IRGC site in Saravan — beyond the coastal target sets of every prior round. Separately, a US-backed Saudi strike hit Houthi-controlled Sanaa airport 13 Jul, and the Houthis warned airlines off Saudi airspace the next day. The blockade is the clearest test yet of whether the hardline-rhetoric-then-contact pattern repeats a third time: the diplomatic channel went quiet over the following weekend, with no reported progress reported as of 16 Jul.
Blockade back ·
strikes reach inland
Israel
Citrinowicz: intent intact, capacity to disrupt constrained. A “complete end to the war in Lebanon” is an explicit Iranian red line, so Israel’s Lebanon campaign is a direct obstacle to the deal; the signed Clause 1 guarantees Lebanese sovereignty, which Tehran tells Hezbollah obliges a phased Israeli withdrawal, while Israel is not a party and remains in southern Lebanon. The 19 Jun Israel–Hezbollah ceasefire failed on both sides within the day, and the IDF kept striking. A trilateral Israel–Lebanon–US framework followed on 27 Jun: Israel keeps a security zone along the yellow line and withdraws from two pilot zones for Lebanese-army-led Hezbollah disarmament, while Hezbollah rejects the terms. This is a de-escalation step on the Lebanon front, and it does not move the spoiler read; there is no unilateral strike on Iran proper. Regime change stays off the Resolution menu; Netanyahu’s stake in a US–Iran deal failing is unchanged. Israeli sources quoted by Nadav Eyal (Between US) frame the 6–9 Jul round as vindication — “we told you so” — and expect not a full resumption of the war but a prolonged “war between the wars”: periodic US-Iran blows, attempts to maintain some understanding, repeating for months. The same sources say Israel does not want the MoU to collapse outright, for now, partly given the Netanyahu government’s own stake in the war not being read as a failure ahead of elections called for 27 Oct. This reads consistently with the 13–16 Jul round: still no direct Israeli strike on Iran, even as the US-Iran exchange itself widens further. The Lebanon front itself moved forward for once: Israeli and Lebanese officials reportedly agreed the structure of a Rome-brokered “pilot zones” plan (14–15 Jul) under the 27 Jun framework, with a phased Lebanese-army deployment and Israeli withdrawal from two zones. Timeline, territorial scope and whether the Lebanese army can search private property for Hezbollah weapons remain unresolved, but it is the first forward movement on this front since the framework was signed.
Good A/C →
regime change
Iran
Fractured — and the hardline camp is winning the fracture. Iran’s primary Hormuz objective is recognised sovereignty over the strait; signed Clause 5 writes that claim into the text, with fees barred for 60 days only and reopened with the Gulf states after. The factional split is overt, the delegation slow-walks the nuclear file behind Clause 13, and IRGC-aligned media reports the Supreme Leader is “not fully” behind the MoU. In late June Iran moved from announcing the re-closure lever to using force — two ship strikes, then drone and ballistic-missile strikes on US bases in Bahrain and Kuwait, warning Gulf states against hosting US attacks — while framing the Doha follow-up as implementation talks, not final-agreement negotiation. On 6–7 Jul Iran escalated again, missiling two tankers and droning a third for using non-Iranian-approved routes — its largest single-day attack volume since the MoU — and rejected a UAE-brokered IMO proposal to manage the strait, insisting transit follow Iranian arrangements. ISW assesses the strait attacks were a chain-of-command decision — top IRGC officers close to the supreme leader approved the use of force — contradicting Iranian negotiators’ account of an “errant faction.” Araghchi, Ghalibaf and Pezeshkian kept working the diplomatic track regardless, including the 11 Jul Muscat meeting with Oman. Two senior Iranian sources told Reuters that relinquishing strait control would amount to “surrender,” and Iran would not discuss its nuclear programme unless the US recognises full Iranian management of the strait. On 12 Jul Iran rejected Oman’s two-corridor proposal and retaliated against five Gulf states in total — Bahrain, Kuwait, Jordan, Qatar and Oman. Maj. Gen. Mohsen Rezaei, the Supreme Leader’s military adviser, said the strait is “more important than dozens of atomic bombs.” Iran struck three further vessels on the Omani route on 13 Jul — two Emirati tankers conducting shuttle runs, killing one crew member and wounding eight — then retaliated against Kuwait, Bahrain and Jordan on 14 Jul, with a possible first use of a cluster-munition warhead against Bahrain that ISW could not verify. The diplomatic channel Iran kept open through the chain-of-command strikes went quiet over the weekend of 12–13 Jul: no reported progress from the Muscat track. On 16 Jul Iran named the strait an “inviolable red line” and warned it would strike infrastructure across the Gulf region if the US carries out its own threat against Iranian infrastructure — a broadening of Iran’s retaliation threat beyond the US bases and vessels it has targeted to date.
Hardliners up ·
vows Gulf-wide
retaliation
China
Publicly pushes Resolution (ceasefire, lanes reopened, Xi/Putin Beijing meeting), but the pivot is partly performative — PRC kept sending missile components and fuel precursors during the war. Per Pape, the war already shifted Beijing’s read of US resolve; munitions depletion (FT, Global21) reinforces it.
Pushes Resolution
Russia
Aligned with China publicly; has formally offered to take Iran’s HEU (acknowledged by Araghchi, “not currently under discussion”). Accepting HEU under the Security Guarantee architecture would be the technical pathway. Preserves Iran ties privately (drones via Caspian).
HEU custodian role available
GCC / Pakistan
Mediation delivered the ceasefire — Pakistan’s PM announced the deal first, Qatar brokered a reported $12bn financial channel, and the UAE reopened a direct security line to Tehran; the 21–22 Jun Bürgenstock round was quadrilateral, with Qatar and Pakistan mediating. All GCC bar Oman reject permanent Iranian control of Hormuz; signed Clause 5 requires Iran to negotiate the post-60-day fee regime with Oman and the other Gulf states, who ISW judges unlikely to acquiesce without Iranian coercion. The US and GCC jointly rejected any Iranian tolls or strait control on 25 Jun; Iran’s 28 Jun strikes on Bahrain read as coercion of the Gulf hosts. By 30 Jun Oman, meeting Macron in Paris, reaffirmed freedom of navigation “without conditions,” and the open Omani route keeps drawing traffic. The UAE has now taken the dispute multilateral, proposing an IMO plan for managing the strait that Iran rejected — the first attempt to institutionalise strait governance outside direct US-Iran or Iran-Oman channels. Saudi Arabia, Kuwait and the UAE are separately advancing pipeline plans to bypass Hormuz altogether, and NATO foreign ministers met Bahraini, Kuwaiti, Qatari and Emirati counterparts on the strait issue in Ankara on 7 Jul. Oman moved from reaffirming free navigation to actively brokering a compromise — the 11 Jul two-corridor proposal — and was rebuffed the next day, the clearest sign yet that even the friendliest Gulf channel cannot extract a concession Iran isn’t offering. The bloc’s exposure widened directly on 12 Jul: Jordan and Oman joined Bahrain, Kuwait and Qatar as targets of Iranian retaliation, the first time the retaliation footprint reached beyond the immediate Gulf littoral. The 14 Jul retaliation against Kuwait, Bahrain and Jordan repeats that widened footprint, and a separate US-backed Saudi strike on Houthi-controlled Sanaa airport (13 Jul) drew a Houthi warning to commercial airlines off Saudi airspace the next day — a Yemen dimension distinct from the direct US-Iran exchange, raising the bloc’s exposure without yet constituting a second front. Iran’s 16 Jul warning of Gulf-wide infrastructure retaliation extends the same coercive logic to the entire bloc, beyond the states already struck. The Saudi–Pakistan airspace cross-promise still binds Israeli unilateral action.
Active mediators
Europe
Aligns with the diplomatic track; refused the US request to reopen the Strait, so it is constrained from leading any post-war architecture. A NATO contribution to the HEU Security Guarantee would be the test. Switzerland hosted the formal ceremony and the first implementation round; the post-war security architecture would sit with other parties.
Diplomatic backer
Japan / Korea
Lean on the US for Resolution; ~60% of energy via Hormuz. The US warned Japan of up-to-two-year Tomahawk delays as the Pentagon prioritises Middle East restocking — US overstretch landing on an Indo-Pacific ally, reinforcing the hedge. Dormant nuclear conversations re-open if the scenario shifts to apex.
Hedging

The 8 Jun – 16 Jul: whipsaw, signing, and a blockade that returns

8–17 Jun
Ceasefire collapses then resolves onto de-escalation: Israeli and US strikes inside Iran (8–10 Jun); Trump declares the deal “complete” (15 Jun); the US and Iran sign the MoU two days early (17 Jun), with signed edits to Clause 1 (Lebanon) and Clause 5 (Hormuz: joint Iranian-Omani administration, fees barred for 60 days). The blockade lifts and oil sanctions waive on signing.
19–22 Jun
A separate Israel–Hezbollah ceasefire is announced and violated within the day; Iran’s IRGC Navy announces a re-closure of the strait tied to Lebanon, which CENTCOM rebuts as flow continues. The first 60-day-track round meets at Bürgenstock (Vance–Araghchi, Qatar/Pakistan mediating) and reaffirms the roadmap; Bloomberg tracks Hormuz flow at the fastest pace since the war; Brent falls to ~$79.
24–30 Jun
Oil falls to its pre-war anchor (~$73). An Iranian drone strikes the Ever Lovely (25 Jun); US CENTCOM strikes Iranian missile, drone-storage and radar sites in response (26–27 Jun); Iran fires drones and ballistic missiles at US bases in Bahrain and Kuwait (28 Jun) — the first armed exchange under the MoU. Both sides stand down by 29–30 Jun; a disputed Doha implementation track opens on 30 Jun.
6–8 Jul
Iran missiles a Qatari gas carrier and a Saudi-flagged tanker (6 Jul), then drones a third tanker (7 Jul) — the largest single-day Hormuz attack volume since the MoU; JMIC raises the threat level to severe. Iran rejects a UAE-brokered IMO strait-management proposal. The US launches fresh airstrikes and revokes the oil-sale waiver (8 Jul), giving Iran until 17 Jul to wind down transactions; the Doha track fails and Trump calls the MoU “over” at the NATO Ankara summit. Talks pause for Khamenei’s funeral (burial 9 Jul, Mashhad).
9–12 Jul
Iran retaliates against Kuwait, Bahrain and Qatar (9 Jul), widening from the two-state pattern of the first exchange. Trump says the US agreed to resume talks at Iran’s request while maintaining the ceasefire is over (10 Jul). Iranian and Omani officials meet in Muscat and Oman proposes a two-corridor arrangement; the IRGC Navy strikes the Cyprus-flagged GFS Galaxy; CENTCOM strikes roughly 140 targets, reaching central Iran for the first time (11 Jul). Iran rejects the Omani proposal and retaliates against five Gulf states — Bahrain, Kuwait, Jordan, Qatar and Oman (12 Jul).
13 Jul
Iran strikes three vessels on the Omani route — two Emirati tankers conducting shuttle runs, killing one crew member and wounding eight. CENTCOM conducts another strike wave. Trump proposes a 20% Hormuz transit fee and announces the naval blockade will resume 14 Jul; Iran says the deal has “entered a crisis phase.” A US-backed Saudi strike hits Houthi-controlled Sanaa airport in Yemen. Brent jumps roughly 10% intraday to ~$83.60.
14 Jul
The blockade formally resumes, targeted at Iranian-flagged vessels and Iranian cargo. Trump drops the 20% fee the same day, replacing it with pledged Gulf-state investment deals. Iran retaliates against US bases in Kuwait, Bahrain and Jordan, with a possible first use of a cluster-munition warhead against Bahrain (ISW unable to verify). The Houthis warn commercial airlines off Saudi airspace.
15 Jul
US strikes reach roughly 200km inland for the first time — an Artesh barracks in Iranshahr (7 killed, 13 injured, per Iranian regime media) and a second IRGC site in Saravan, both in Sistan and Baluchistan province. Artesh claims eight waves of drone strikes against a US base in Jordan; separately, six drones targeted Erbil airport and the US consulate in Iraqi Kurdistan, attribution unclear between Iran and an Iranian-backed Iraqi militia. The US Treasury sanctions over 50 entities and vessels tied to the Shamkhani family's Iranian oil-smuggling network, explicitly framed by the US as economic pressure alongside the blockade. Shipping companies and tanker captains are reported avoiding the Omani route despite US escorts.
16 Jul
Iran declares the strait an “inviolable red line,” warning it will strike infrastructure across the Gulf region if the US carries out its threat against Iranian infrastructure. Per Bremmer, the Iran-Oman weekend talks produced no meaningful progress and diplomatic channels have gone quiet.
What it confirms: the reinstated blockade and the inland strikes show the off-ramp's terms are reversible under pressure, and the diplomatic channel that produced the Muscat proposal has gone quiet rather than producing a follow-through. What it does not change: the bifurcated architecture, the apex held in the long-term allocation, the Murrin DD, the Brent bands. Watch-list: the mediation-breakdown trigger fires this window — ISW's own assessment is that the ceasefire has effectively ended, and the diplomatic channel that produced the 11 Jul Muscat proposal has gone quiet with no reported progress since. This is a reversal of v10's read, where the trigger receded because talks had resumed and produced an actual proposal. The Israeli-strike trigger has still not fired; this remains a US-Iran exchange, plus a Yemen dimension (Saudi-Houthi) that is not yet a second front. The live channels are unchanged: contested Hormuz governance and the toll regime, the IAEA dispute, and the Lebanon front. Iran's 16 Jul warning raises the ceiling on a Gulf-wide infrastructure exchange if the US strikes Iranian infrastructure, but the warning itself is not yet an event.
The map — the blockade returns, the diplomatic channel stalls

Stalemate stays modal because the US–Iran nuclear divide is wide and the hardliner faction blocks Good A. The ceasefire is a Stage-1 instrument: it ends the shooting, lifts the blockade and reopens Hormuz, while the nuclear core, the HEU stockpile and sanctions sequencing are pushed to a 60-day track. The first armed exchange under the MoU showed the deal could turn kinetic without breaking; the market priced that violation as contained. A second, larger exchange on 6–8 Jul confirmed the pattern; an 8–12 Jul continuation was the widest round yet — strikes reaching central Iran, retaliation reaching five Gulf states, an Omani compromise attempted and rejected. The 13–16 Jul window goes further than scale: for the first time since signing, a core term of the deal itself reversed — the naval blockade Article 4 lifted on signing came back on 14 Jul. Strikes reached roughly 200km inland, beyond every prior round's coastal target set. Iran's own escalation kept pace: three more vessels struck, a possible first cluster-munition use, and on 16 Jul a formal warning to strike Gulf-wide infrastructure. The open question v10 flagged — whether the deferred cores settle inside the 60-day window or return as binding at its end — has a harder answer this window: the diplomatic channel that produced the 11 Jul Muscat proposal went quiet, and the blockade's return is the clearest sign yet that Article 4 is not holding as a settled term. Whether the file's established pattern — hardline rhetoric giving way to renewed contact — repeats a third time is the question carried forward; it has not yet been retested since the 10 Jul resume-talks statement.

06 · Market & thematic implications

Scenario views across equities, Brent, rates, and rotation.

Views only. Brent bands and the scenario architecture are unchanged from v10; the 13–16 Jul window pushed oil further from the pre-war anchor, per Bremmer, though no settled print has independently reached this desk.

ScenarioEquitiesBrentRatesSector & thematic rotation
Stalemate
Modal		 Mid-teens downside; EPS holds until ~2027 demand destruction. US indices fell on the 8 Jul news (Dow -0.3%, S&P -0.5%, Nasdaq -1.2%, chipmakers leading); Asian markets were mixed (Hang Seng higher on financials, Topix and ASX lower on the crude jump). Tight, elevated, drawing inventory. Per Carlyle, the move down through late June was destocking-driven — US SPR near its operational floor, Cushing near the settlement floor — not a fundamentals rebalance, and fragile to any reopening slippage. That fragility showed on 6–8 Jul: oil rose more than 3% intraday on the tanker attacks alone (European gas futures +7%), with a further leg on the waiver-revocation news. Barclays (read for v10) puts the immediate 6–8 Jul move at roughly +6% and expects a $65–80 range over coming months — below this file’s $90–110 Resolution-quick floor — a watch item against this file's own band, pending a settled print. The 14 Jul blockade reinstatement moved the market further: per Bremmer, Brent jumped roughly 10% intraday on the blockade/fee announcement (13–14 Jul) to ~$83.60, and is back above $80 and rising as of 16 Jul, with strait traffic below 15% of pre-war levels — down from ~25% a week earlier. Still no settled print independently reached by this desk. The SPR buffer is still thin at ~325.7m barrels, the lowest since 1983, against a JPMorgan-cited ~$150 re-closure shock. Fiscal pressure lifts yields; energy tightening Jul–Sep adds to the curve. Iran-axis exposure screens unattractive on the retaliation vectors (UAE data centres, regional fintech, Gulf cable-dependent payments) — now including Jordan and Oman as directly-struck hosts. A Yemen/Houthi vector adds Saudi airspace and Red Sea-adjacent shipping to the screen, flagged ahead of any realised disruption given the 13–14 Jul Sanaa-airport exchange. China sleeve re-rates up on the Beijing read of US weakness; munitions depletion (FT, Global21) is the observable marker. Energy-paradigm shift favours rare earths, security and infrastructure; defensive on regional refining. A non-oil vector: Iran is extending the transit-control playbook to the Europe–Asia undersea cables — IRGC-linked demands for transit fees on the hyperscalers and veiled threats to the fibre, reaching toward SWIFT. Low feasibility, but it sharpens the cable-dependent-payments screen.
Resolution
Improving		 Mid-single-digit upside. Declines toward $90–110 over months; market below the band — ~$84 on the ceasefire, ~$73 by 29 Jun at the pre-war anchor — pushed back up by the 6–8 Jul and 8–12 Jul escalations, direction confirmed though no settled print yet — and further by the blockade's 14 Jul return: per Bremmer, Brent is above $80 and rising as of 16 Jul, with traffic collapsed to below 15% of pre-war levels — a wider gap from this band than any prior window, still unconfirmed by a settled print reaching this desk — pricing a clean reopening the substance does not yet support; trust rebuild takes quarters. Yields ease as the energy premium unwinds. Iran-axis exposure screens better selectively; the energy/renewables opportunity set persists on the structural energy-paradigm shift; the China sleeve recalibrates on a firmer Hormuz-access outcome.

Apex / tail: escalation toward $130–160+ Brent is held in the long-term allocation; first-use ($200+, SPX ~3,500) sits below the scenario-modelling threshold as structural tail protection. The deal re-times this tail to the 60-day nuclear window rather than removing it — the HEU stays in Iran and is being hardened. The re-closure scenario the market is now pricing as a ~$150 shock is the near-term, non-nuclear face of this escalation leg. The Taiwan-war and US-default extrapolations are held as backdrop only, outside the modelled set.

The $300bn in the signed terms is external Gulf investment funded by sanctions relief — for instance a UAE-built power plant in Iran — not a US Treasury outlay, which reconciles the terms with Iran’s frozen-asset demand; the relief flows through GCC capital.

Resolution mechanics

The Resolution path follows the framework David specified: Mearsheimer’s “humble pie” off-ramp; a JPOA 2.0 nuclear architecture; the Ferguson Hormuz Council (Iran as Chair, a 45% / 30% / balance revenue split, transit fees at cost); HEU export under a Security Guarantee with Russia available as custodian; and a post-war Gulf Treaty Organisation with China and Russia as signatories. The realised Stage-1 MOU diverges from the Council mechanic on Hormuz: rather than a chaired revenue split with fees at cost, the strait reopens toll-free but under Iranian and Omani management — Iran’s sovereignty claim conceded operationally, the revenue-sharing design bypassed, with fees barred for 60 days then talks with the Gulf states. The deeper cores the framework was built to settle — HEU ~440kg, enrichment, sanctions sequencing — remain for the 60-day track. The Hormuz governance the framework was built to settle is being contested by force: Iran asserts Article-5 “sole responsibility” and strikes vessels that use the Omani route, Oman signals service fees, and the US and GCC reject tolls — the fee-regime negotiation the MoU deferred is opening under fire. Iran’s rejection of the UAE’s IMO strait-management proposal fits the same logic: control of the mechanism matters more to Iran than the toll income itself, which is why a multilateral body is a harder concession than a bilateral one with Oman. Oman’s own two-corridor proposal tested that logic directly on 11–12 Jul and was rejected on the same grounds: Iran held out against its own co-administrator under the signed Clause 5 framework, the same resistance it showed the UAE’s multilateral alternative. The blockade's 14 Jul return adds Article 4 to the list of contested terms, alongside Article 5. Bremmer's independent read frames the eventual off-ramp in similar terms to the Ferguson Council: an institutionalised, divided Iranian management of the strait, funded by a Gulf-state collective, on the model of Turkey's role in the Bosphorus and Dardanelles or Malaysia and Singapore's in the Malacca Strait — a variant of the mechanism the signed MoU deferred rather than a departure from it. Neither Washington nor Tehran is yet ready to accept that compromise on his read; both this file's mechanics and his independent framing point to the same open question, unresolved this window.

v12 · First US deaths, Tehran suspends the MoU, Murrin calls Chapter Two · 20 Jul 2026
Architecture, verdict & Brent bands held · US chip flipped · Iran chip flipped · Resolution tier marked Suspended · iran-formal-response watch-list fires · Murrin WW3 116 read into the DD

The architecture holds — Stalemate (modal) / Resolution (track suspended) / apex in the long-term allocation. The 17–19 Jul window crossed two thresholds. An Iranian missile attack on Muwaffaq Salti Airbase in Jordan killed two US service members on 17 Jul, and a third died in Iraqi Kurdistan on 18 Jul disposing of ordnance from a downed Iranian drone — the first US deaths of the conflict, taking the US toll to 17 since the war began on 28 Feb. On 18 Jul Tehran formally suspended the MoU: Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei declared Trump's signature “invalid” and Deputy FM Gharibabadi announced Iran is “suspending” its commitments, after the Deputy FM had called the deal “completely dismantled” on state TV on 14 Jul. The question v11 carried forward — whether hardline rhetoric would again give way to renewed contact — resolved toward breakdown this round: Ghalibaf was still defending the talks track on television on 16 Jul, and the suspension landed two days later. Murrin's new piece, WW3 116 (17 Jul), declares Chapter Two of the war and puts oil above $150 within weeks. Two chips move and the Resolution tier is marked down.

What moved from v11:

  • US chip flips: “Blockade back · strikes reach inland” → “First troop deaths · strikes touch the nuclear file.” Three US service members killed 17–18 Jul; a sixth, seventh and eighth consecutive strike night interdicting Iran's internal logistics feeding Bandar Abbas; blockade enforcement turned kinetic (three vessels redirected, one disabled, the M/T Wen Yao boarded); and Iran's AEOI says a 19 Jul strike hit the Darkhovin nuclear plant. Three US officials called the recent strikes “shaping operations” (Reuters); more F-16s, F-35s and refuellers are moving to the region.
  • Iran chip flips: “Hardliners up · vows Gulf-wide retaliation” → “MoU suspended · Gulf-wide retaliation executed.” The 16 Jul red-line warning became execution: retaliation reached seven countries — adding Saudi Arabia (first in about three months; sirens at Yanbu), Syria (al Tanf) and southern Jordan (Aqaba) — and Kuwaiti power, desalination and oil facilities were struck three days running, with material damage per Kuwait Petroleum Corporation. The IRGC Navy is enforcing a permission regime: “not a single drop of oil, gas, or chemical fertilizer” passes without its coordination.
  • iran-formal-response fires; mediation-breakdown deepens. The 18 Jul suspension declarations are the formal Iranian position landing — a termination declaration is the slug's closest fit. ISW's negotiations section reads “nothing significant to report,” and mediator Oman is itself now a target of Iranian strikes and public hostility. The Israeli-strike trigger has still not fired: Israel is intercepting drones and Defence Minister Katz warns it will re-engage if attacked, without a confirmed Israeli strike.
  • Resolution tier marked Suspended. The 60-day track's formal suspension, a dead diplomatic channel and a targeted mediator take the tier off “improving.” The scenario stays in the modelled set per the Eric/team architecture; the open question carried forward is whether a successor venue convenes and whether the regime split — Rezaei declaring diplomacy over against Ghalibaf and a Nour News op-ed still arguing for talks — resolves toward a reopened channel.
  • Murrin called the deal's failure mechanism on signing day. His 17 Jun read of Clause 5 — the article setting Iran's Hormuz obligations, which the signed text placed under joint Iranian-Omani management — was that it conceded effective strait control to Iran, and that is what ISW now assesses broke the MoU; the HEU-survives and Hormuz-as-substitute-deterrent calls from May have held as well, and all three are already working assumptions in this file. He fails as consistently on timing: “looms large” in WW3 116 is his third dated breakout call and none has landed, and the Israel-as-trigger thesis running through four of his pieces has not fired once. Neither the verdict nor the bands move on the piece.
  • A second-chokepoint vector is logged at Bab el-Mandeb. Reuters (16 Jul, single root source): Iran instructed the Houthis to prepare to close the strait if the US strikes Iranian power plants; Houthi-Saudi cross-border attacks have resumed for the first time since 2022. Saudi Arabia now moves ~4mn bpd (~60% of pre-war crude exports) via Yanbu, so a dual closure traps the bypass — Capital Economics prices it at +$15–20/bbl and near its adverse global-recession scenario.
  • First settled Brent prints since v11 — bands hold. $82.96 close on 16 Jul (desk series) and ~$88 settle on 17 Jul per Bloomberg (+4.6%, the biggest weekly advance since April), with the front month in backwardation; SPR updated to 316.5m barrels (10 Jul, lowest since April 1983). Both prints sit below the $90–110 Resolution-quick floor.
  • The de-escalatory increment: Lebanon pilot zones move to implementation. The Lebanese Armed Forces began deploying into the first zones on 16–17 Jul under the Rome-brokered plan — the one front advancing in the other direction.

The scenario architecture, the verdict on Murrin Article 2, the Brent bands and the apex placement are unchanged.

Inputs (17–20 Jul intake): ISW Iran Update Special Reports, 17 / 18 / 19 Jul (Gary via Eric / LSP, full PDFs read) — the troop deaths, the suspension declarations, the Bandar Abbas logistics interdiction, the seven-country retaliation set, the Darkhovin strike, the SNSC-committee disclosure · Murrin WW3 116, “The Battle for the Strait is On” (17 Jul, Gary via Eric) · Bloomberg terminal (Eric) — the third-troop-death report, the ~$88 settle, and Ghalibaf's 16 Jul defence of the talks track · Vali Nasr FT op-ed and Citrinowicz X post (Gary via Eric) — the structural-breakdown and escalation-management reads · Capital Economics Houthi-implications note (16 Jul, LSP) — the Bab el-Mandeb / Yanbu arithmetic · 13D WILTW (16 Jul, LSP) — the 14 Jul Deputy-FM statement and the 10 Jul War Powers notification · Vanda CIO risk report (18 Jul, LSP) — the equity right-tail read · internal desk products (16–17 Jul) for the settled prints and SPR series

Yellow marks what is new or changed in v12 — the first US troop deaths, Tehran's formal suspension, the seven-country retaliation footprint and the Kuwait infrastructure strikes, the Darkhovin strike, the Murrin WW3 116 read, the Bab el-Mandeb vector, the settled Brent prints and the Resolution tier markdown. The scenario architecture, the other actor chips, the signed-MoU text, the Murrin Article 2 due-diligence tiers and the Brent bands carry from v11 unmarked.

Bottom line · first US deaths, the MoU formally suspended

The scenario set stays bifurcated: Stalemate (modal) and Resolution (track suspended), with the apex tail held in the long-term allocation below the scenario-modelling threshold. Three US service members were killed on 17–18 Jul — two in an Iranian missile attack on Muwaffaq Salti Airbase in Jordan, a third in Iraqi Kurdistan — the first US deaths of the conflict. Tehran formally suspended the MoU on 18 Jul: Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei called Trump's signature “invalid” and the Deputy FM announced suspension of commitments, resolving v11's open question toward breakdown. Iranian retaliation reached seven countries, adding Saudi Arabia, Syria and southern Jordan, and struck Kuwaiti power, desalination and oil facilities three days running; the US ran its sixth, seventh and eighth consecutive strike nights, severing the logistics feeding Bandar Abbas, and a 19 Jul strike hit the Darkhovin nuclear plant per Iran's AEOI — the first kinetic touch on the nuclear file this cycle (IAEA: very early construction, no nuclear material at the last inspection). Reuters reports Iran instructed the Houthis to prepare to close Bab el-Mandeb, putting the ~4mn bpd Yanbu bypass at risk. Brent settled at $82.96 on 16 Jul and ~$88 on 17 Jul — the first settled prints since v11, still below the $90–110 floor. The Israeli-strike trigger has still not fired; Israel is intercepting drones and warning it will re-engage if attacked. Murrin’s Article 2 nuclear scenario remains the analytical foundation under structural tail protection; WW3 116 restates the breakout case a third time with no new evidence behind it. Full scenario stances, drivers, equity and Brent views are in section 03.

01 · Due Diligence on Murrin

Verdict unchanged — cannot rule it out. WW3 116 adds no new evidence.

Capabilities corroborated (Tier 1); interpretive layers held as hypotheses (Tier 2); the policy conclusion treated as advocacy (Tier 3). The full claim-by-claim verifiability table sits in the v1 tab.

Murrin’s capabilities case is sound and OSINT-sourced. Timeline sits at the aggressive end of a 6–12 month band that overlaps Roaring Lion’s own assessment. The preemptive-nuclear-use conclusion is his advocacy. The Gallant “440 Kilograms” piece restates the ~440kg 60% HEU stockpile that anchors the capabilities case, and ISW, citing five US-intelligence sources via CNN, reports Iran is physically hardening that stockpile — collapsing tunnels and mining storage entrances to defeat any seizure — so the fissile core is intact, deferred, and being entrenched. Iran agreed to readmit IAEA inspectors, the first forward step on the nuclear file; it is not down-blending. That step has stalled: the inspection access is unresolved, with Grossi expecting entry while Iran ties it to a final deal and sanctions relief, and Iran has signalled it could suspend the talks over the armed exchange. The ~440kg core stays inside Iran behind the entrenched tunnels. “Cannot rule out the nuclear scenario” holds as the analytical foundation; the apex is handled via the long-term allocation rather than modelled scenarios.

WW3 116, “The Battle for the Strait is On, so Prepare for Escalation” (17 Jul), is the tracked author's read of the current exchange. Murrin declares “Chapter Two” of the war — an existential contest for the strait and the Red Sea sea lanes that in his framing ends only with a victor and a vanquished. It is commentary rather than reporting: twenty-four distinct claims with no primary text of his own behind them, and the two that carry real weight are corroborated from outside the piece. Four of his headline claims have nothing on this desk that tests them either way.

ClaimTierChecked againstOur read
The MoU. An appeasement dwarfing Chamberlain's, and in full form it would have destroyed US maritime hegemony at a stroke. Interpretive The signed 14-point text is on file. ISW (18 Jul) attributes the collapse to divergent readings of Clause 5 — Iran reading it as affirming Iranian authority over the strait, Washington expecting pre-war traffic conditions. Corroborated at the level of mechanism: Clause 5 is where the deal failed, the leg he identified on signing day from the published clauses. The Chamberlain scaling and the hegemony conclusion are his register and carry no checkable content.
Breakout. The IRGC used the pause to repair and replenish while moving closer to a weapon day by day. Advocacy The IAEA puts the stockpile at 441kg of 60% HEU as of June 2025; ISW, citing US intelligence via CNN, reports it being physically hardened in tunnels. Nothing on the desk tests imminence. Retention and hardening are corroborated and stand independently of him. The imminence claim is his third dated one and carries no enrichment figure, no breakout estimate and no date, so it cannot be tested on its own terms. Timing stays at the aggressive end of the 6–12 month band.
Total war. An existential contest for the shipping lanes, resolved only with a victor and a vanquished; America should commit to defeating the IRGC with overwhelming firepower. Advocacy A prescription. No source is offered and nothing on the desk bears on it. Reported as his conviction and kept in quotation. The escalation direction is consistent with the 6–19 Jul record. The grand-strategy prescription carries no falsifiable content.
Trump's resolve. Hardened: the midterm gas-price motive is dead on poor ratings, and he reads Iran as having acted in bad faith. Interpretive The “scum” remark is on the record from the Ankara NATO summit and already carried here. Tehran suspended the MoU on 18 Jul. No polling has reached the desk. The hardening direction is consistent with the observable sequence. The popularity leg is asserted without a pollster and is not carried. His own oil call runs against it — a print above $150 would restore the gas-price motive he argues is dead.
Assassination plot. A reported IRGC plot against Trump, passed via Israeli intelligence, corroborated by the Secret Service switching him back to the older Air Force One, and possibly the decisive factor driving him to destroy the IRGC. Interpretive A single unnamed Israeli-intelligence channel, hedged by Murrin himself. The aircraft switch is asserted without source and is unverified here, including the donor state of the aircraft he names. Uncorroborated on this desk. That the switch evidences the plot is his inference rather than anyone's report. The escalation-driver conclusion rests on that unverified chain plus a psychological read of Trump.
China and Russia. Iran fights on with their approval, confirmed by supplies flowing into its replenishment; US strikes on supply-route bridges acknowledge it. Interpretive ISW (17–18 Jul) confirms strikes on bridges, roads, a tunnel and telecoms around Bandar Abbas, a senior US official (WSJ, 17 Jul) describing the aim as disrupting supply routes to the city — routes ISW describes as Iran's own interior-to-coast logistics. ISW separately assesses the PRC and Russia are likely supporting Iranian targeting, citing a PRC reconnaissance satellite, Russian geolocation of US warships and aircraft, and Russian drone tactics. Both component facts are corroborated; joining them is his. What ISW documents is targeting intelligence, and no source on the desk reports Chinese or Russian materiel entering Iran. Held as a hypothesis.
Interceptor stocks. Every month of war consumes US holdings of PAC-3, THAAD and SM-3. Corroborated CSIS (Cancian and Park, 30 May 2026): PAC-3 MSE runs at roughly 650 a year with half going to allies, against a US decade-average buy of 225; the 3,203 Patriots requested for FY2027 first deliver May 2029. THAAD runs at 96 a year against an 857-interceptor request first delivering mid-2029. Both sit in the slowest three-plus-year replacement tier. Corroborated for two of his three named systems, from a defence-budget source rather than a strategic commentator. SM-3 is excluded: CSIS records less use than ground-launched systems and attributes the lag to small past orders and 36–39 month lead times. The monthly-burn framing is not how CSIS measures it, and the piece predates three further escalation rounds.
Who gains. Depletion benefits Moscow, leaving Ukraine exposed as PAC-3 stocks fall, and benefits Beijing more still by raising first-strike confidence before US stocks recover. Interpretive CSIS documents the allocation friction — THAAD deliveries re-sequenced away from allies, Japan's Tomahawks possibly delayed, Ukraine Patriots pushed back in the queue — and states the depleted inventories created a window of vulnerability in the Western Pacific. It contests the conclusion: demonstrated US combat experience against a PLA with none may preserve deterrence until inventories are restored. It says nothing about Russia benefiting. The queue and the Western Pacific window are corroborated in the source's own words, stronger support than he offers himself. The further step — that Moscow and Beijing are prolonging the conflict for this reason — has no support on the desk.
Mobile launchers. The hard target, rather than the underground missile cities; the fix is compressing the kill chain with persistent armed ISR striking within minutes. Advocacy Self-labelled house conjecture. The only adjacent datum is one unelaborated Reuters line (three US officials, 16 Jul) describing recent strikes as “shaping operations.” Every missile-related strike ISW reports in the window is a fixed site — the Lar launch site, the Yazd Missile Base, Konarak airbase. No in-window evidence of the shift in either direction. One detail runs the other way: the 116 downed telecom towers and the strike on the Radio and Communications Regulatory Authority degrade Iran's control of its own Shahed drones, which is fixed-infrastructure suppression.
Order of battle. 135 MQ-9s, 18 RQ-4s, roughly 50 MQ-4C Tritons and an operational RQ-180 for that mission, with the binding constraint numerical — “not dozens but hundreds” of platforms. Interpretive No independent check on the desk. Every figure is asserted without source, and no coverage modelling sits behind the hundreds. Not carried, consistent with the numeric record in his work — 300kg and 460kg for the same HEU stock inside one article, “Fatyie Ii” for the Fattah-2, a moving SPR figure. The RQ-180 entry is the exception and is credited: he hedges it as classified and unconfirmed. The mass-over-sophistication constraint is sound in kind; the figure is illustrative.
The Ukraine model. The 250km strike-drone campaign that largely isolated Crimea by land transfers to Hormuz — drones from the Omani Peninsula interdicting Iran's coastal batteries. Advocacy The Crimea-isolation premise is stated flatly and unsourced. Nothing on the desk tests the 250km radius or the Omani basing proposition. Prescription. ISW's own Hormuz mechanic cuts against the payoff — a single successful attack, or the persistent threat of one, deters transits — so suppressing launchers need not restore traffic. Logged, not carried.
Bab el-Mandeb. Yemeni sources report the Houthis preparing to close it with al-Shabaab, holding closure in reserve as their own asset; the strait carries 10–12% of seaborne trade, and simultaneous disruption with Hormuz would exceed any modern maritime crisis. Interpretive A closure vector is independently reported (Reuters, 16 Jul), reaching this desk inside the Capital Economics note rather than read directly, and on a different causal account: Iran instructing the Houthis conditional on US strikes against Iranian power plants. ISW's 17 Jul edition carries no Yemen, Houthi, Bab el-Mandeb or al-Shabaab content at all. Bremmer and Barclays both attribute Houthi behaviour to a payments dispute with Riyadh. The trade share is asserted without source; Capital Economics instead quantifies the consequence at roughly 4mn bpd rerouted via Yanbu and +$15–20/bbl on a dual closure. The vector is live and the Sanaa-airport leg is already on this file. His account of agency — a Houthi-owned asset held in reserve, largely independent of Tehran — is not the account our sources give. The al-Shabaab coordination and drone-transfer legs are uncorroborated rather than contradicted, resting on unnamed sourcing alone. The consequence arithmetic is usable; the trade share is not carried.
Oil above $150. A surge in the weeks ahead as risk premia rise, with significant pressure on government bond markets. Advocacy $150 sits inside the $130–160+ escalation band this file already holds. Brent settled around $88 on 17 Jul. Barclays' $65–80 range is the genuine out-of-band divergence. The bond call names no market, tenor, level or horizon. The level is inside our own range. He carries $150 as a base expectation within weeks; this file holds it as a tail. No supply-outage volume or trigger level is given and the horizon has not elapsed, so the call is logged unscored. His section heading asserting oil is set to rocket higher overstates his own hedged text.

Track record — prior calls against the 20 May to 20 Jul record

The capability, structure and chokepoint calls have held, and three of them are now working assumptions in this file. Timing and agency are where he fails, consistently and across every piece. The rows below are not independent trials — the Israel-trigger thesis recurs in four pieces and is entered once, and several draw on the same escalation sequence — so this reads as a map of where he is reliable rather than a hit rate. Unfalsifiable framing and advocacy carrying no forecast content are excluded, as is a 2024 Lebanon ground-campaign piece re-stamped 29 May 2026 and circulated as current with its body text never revised.

02 · Scenario test

Live tail risk on a 6–12 month horizon — below the scenario-modelling threshold.

The conclusion is unchanged; the operational decision is to bear this tail in the long-term allocation rather than model it.

The apex first-use event is paradigm-break, so protection is structural rather than model-derived. As David noted, “in short, I probably don’t need to know what the damage would look like.” The deal does not lower the tail — it re-times it. The MOU defers the nuclear question to a 60-day negotiation while the HEU stays inside Iran, so the breakout clock moves from the battlefield to the talks table without resetting. The 25–28 Jun armed exchange was the test case for kinetic escalation short of first-use; the 6–8 Jul exchange a second instance; the 8–12 Jul continuation a third; the 13–16 Jul round a fourth — all conventional, with the apex below threshold throughout. The 17–19 Jul window is a fifth iteration, and it moves two markers the prior four did not: US service members were killed, and a strike reached a nuclear-file site — the Darkhovin plant in Khuzestan, per Iran's AEOI. Both stay inside the conventional channel: Darkhovin is a very-early-construction site with no nuclear material found at the IAEA's last inspection, and the ~440kg HEU core goes unmentioned in any of this window's exchanges. The re-timing anchor itself has moved: with Tehran's 18 Jul suspension the 60-day track is no longer running, so the apex window is open-ended rather than deadline-bound. The placement is insensitive to that timing — the tail sits in the long-term allocation either way, which is what the structural-protection decision was for. What stays in the long-term allocation is the nightmare convergence — nuclear Iran + reinvigorated Russia + hedging China + exhausted America + isolated Israel arriving together around summer 2026 as oil buffers run dry; Murrin Article 3’s late-stage US-decline thesis sits on the “exhausted America” leg as backdrop conviction, with the US Strategic Petroleum Reserve at 316.5m barrels as of 10 Jul, the lowest since April 1983, with ~34m contracted barrels left and total US crude stocks at their lowest since 1984 — monitored daily at the tip-of-iceberg layer.

Verdict

Murrin Article 2 cannot be ruled out and remains a live tail risk on a 6–12 month horizon. The apex probability sits below the threshold where another modelled scenario would change the prescription. Israeli unilateral action remains the most plausible trigger — and the armed US-Iran exchanges under the MoU show the deal can turn kinetic through Hormuz directly; the strait and the Lebanon front are both live channels for an escalation that the long-term allocation is built to absorb. A fifth round — first US deaths, a formal Iranian suspension, retaliation across seven countries — extends the pattern at higher stakes without displacing Israeli unilateral action as the most plausible apex trigger. Israel is closer to the exchange than in any prior window: intercepting Iranian drones near its borders, detecting the Aqaba launch, and warning it will re-engage if attacked. The trigger has still not fired.

Stalemate / Resolution / BAU — stance, drivers, and market views.

Per the Eric/team binding decision (22 May): two scenarios modelled, plus the BAU baseline. The apex first-use scenario is held in the long-term allocation. Architecture unchanged in v12; the Resolution tier descriptor moves to Suspended.
