04 · The signed MoU

What the text says — the milestone, Article by Article.

The 14-point text Bloomberg published on 16 June and the version a senior US official read aloud on signing (17 Jun); the Article text below is verbatim from those primary publications. Tehran announced on 18 Jul it is suspending its commitments under this text; the table is carried as the reference for whatever track resumes, with the implementation boxes tracking how each obligation has executed and reversed.

ArticleText (close paraphrase)Our reading
1 · Ceasefire Immediate and permanent end to the war on all fronts including Lebanon; no hostile actions; refrain from the threat or use of force; the final agreement confirms this. Signed edit: a Lebanese-sovereignty guarantee was added. Contested — Iran reads it as obliging an Israeli withdrawal; Israel is not a signatory and keeps striking. The one clause advancing: the Lebanese Armed Forces began deploying into the Rome-brokered pilot zones on 16–17 Jul.
2 · Sovereignty Both parties respect each other’s sovereignty and territorial integrity and refrain from interfering in internal affairs. Implicitly constrains US regime-change; the IRGC structure survives. Iran also leans on this to frame “managing” Hormuz as an internal affair.
3 · 60-day window Negotiate and reach a final agreement within a maximum of 60 days, extendable by mutual consent. The clock for every deferred core. Suspended by Tehran on 18 Jul at roughly the window's halfway mark: Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei declared the signature “invalid” and Deputy FM Gharibabadi announced Iran is suspending its commitments. Neither side has formally withdrawn from the text itself.
4 · US obligations Immediate: lift the naval blockade, prevent interference against Iran, restore traffic within 30 days to pre-war capacity proportional to Iran’s pre-war volume. US withdraws forces from surrounding areas within 30 days after the final agreement. Asymmetry: the blockade lifts at once, but force withdrawal waits on the final agreement. Signed term tested and reversed: the US reinstated the blockade on 14 Jul, targeted at Iranian-flagged vessels and Iranian cargo. Enforcement is now kinetic: since 14 Jul the US Navy has redirected three vessels and disabled one, and the 11th MEU boarded the Curacao-flagged M/T Wen Yao off Duqm on 16 Jul. The 30-day full-traffic-restoration deadline passed in breach on 17 Jul.
5 · Iran obligations (Hormuz) Immediate: resume merchant shipping in the Gulf / Sea of Oman within 30 days to pre-war volume, removing technical obstacles and neutralising mines. Signed edit: a joint Iranian-Omani administration and a 60-day toll bar were added. Iran’s win — the text does not bar Iran from “managing” the strait. Tested and unresolved: Oman’s own two-corridor proposal was rejected by Iran on 12 Jul, and shipping avoids the Omani route despite US escorts. The IRGC Navy now states the regime openly — “not a single drop of oil, gas, or chemical fertilizer” passes without its coordination and permission — and a Thai-flagged vessel was struck on the southern route on 17 Jul. Iran's Environmental Protection Agency floated UNCLOS-based “environmental services” fees on 18 Jul, a new variant of the fee ambition.
6 · $300bn development US and regional partners build a rehabilitation and development plan financing at least $300bn; the implementation mechanism is set within 60 days. External Gulf money, not a US outlay (Trump: “no money changes hands”; Reuters: no US government funds). Gulf-funded, routing the financing into the Gulf-state track.
7 · Sanctions US commits to ending all sanctions — UNSC resolutions, IAEA Board resolutions, all unilateral US primary and secondary sanctions — on a schedule set in the final agreement. Exceeds the JCPOA’s scope in principle, but the schedule is deferred to the final agreement. Signed term tested in the other direction: the US Treasury added over 50 sanctions targets on 14–15 Jul (the Shamkhani network, IRGC procurement) — new sanctions rather than the wind-down the Article commits to.
8 · Nuclear Iran reiterates it will never produce nuclear weapons; the fate of enriched material and all nuclear-related issues, including Iran’s “nuclear needs,” is “adequately addressed” in the final agreement. A restatement of Iran’s standing position. The version read aloud on signing adds on-site down-blending under IAEA supervision as the baseline method — gated to the final agreement and cutting against Iran’s simultaneous hardening of its HEU tunnels. The ~440kg core stays in Iran through the window. The file was touched kinetically for the first time this window: Iran's AEOI says a 19 Jul US strike hit the Darkhovin plant in Khuzestan (IAEA: very early construction, no nuclear material found at its last inspection). Araghchi said on 19 Jul that some nuclear demands may remain “unresolvable.”
9 · Status quo Pending the final agreement, Iran holds the status quo on its nuclear programme and the US imposes no new sanctions and does not strengthen its forces in the region. The strategic freeze: Iran’s programme stays intact while US leverage is capped. The most consequential clause for the deferral read. Both halves are now in breach — new US sanctions landed 14–15 Jul, and more F-16s, F-35s and refuellers are moving to the region.
10 · Oil waivers US Treasury issues waivers immediately after signing for Iranian crude, petrochemicals and derivatives, and related services (banking, insurance, transport). Signed edit tested: the waiver was revoked on 8 Jul — the first rollback of a core MoU economic term. The wind-down completed on 17 Jul: General License X1 expired, revoking all Iranian-oil transaction authority.
11 · Frozen assets Frozen and restricted Iranian funds released and made fully available in line with negotiation progress, directed by the Central Bank of Iran. Sequenced to negotiation progress — Iran’s leverage-extraction path. Vance conditions release on “verified steps” on the HEU; Vahidi insists the funds are not barred from military spending.
12 · Implementation A mechanism to oversee implementation and the commitment to the final agreement. Compliance architecture with no specified membership, voting or enforcement — to be designed in 60 days.
13 · Sequencing gate Nuclear negotiations begin only after Iran receives assurances on the implementation of Articles 4, 5, 10 and 11 (blockade, Hormuz, oil waivers, frozen assets); talks then address only the remaining articles. Iran’s verification gate — it can delay the nuclear talks by contesting any of those four implementations. The Article 10 revocation and the blockade's 14 Jul return each gave Iran grounds to invoke it. The 18 Jul suspension goes past the gate: rather than invoking Article 13 to pause the nuclear talks, Tehran suspended its commitments across the text.
14 · UNSC ratification The final agreement is approved through a binding UN Security Council resolution. China and Russia become structural guarantors — neither the US nor Israel can unilaterally exit the final deal without a UNSC crisis. Iran’s durability anchor.
Implementation — weeks 1–5 (carried)

The week-by-week implementation record through 16 Jul — the two-way first week, the kinetic second, the tanker attacks and waiver revocation of the third, the central-Iran strikes and Muscat rejection of the fourth, and the blockade's return in the fifth — is carried in the v7–v11 tabs. The through-line: each week tested a different Article, and by week five a core term (Article 4) had formally reversed.

Implementation — week 6 · v12

The window (Art 3): Tehran suspended its MoU commitments on 18 Jul — Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei declared Trump's signature “invalid,” Deputy FM Gharibabadi announced the suspension, and Rezaei said diplomacy and negotiations “are over,” while Ghalibaf (16 Jul, state TV) and a Nour News op-ed (18 Jul) still argued for the talks track. ISW's negotiations section for the window: “nothing significant to report.” Casualties: two US service members killed at Muwaffaq Salti Airbase, Jordan (17 Jul, ascribed to the IRGC); a third killed in Iraqi Kurdistan (18 Jul); US toll 17 since 28 Feb. Blockade (Art 4): enforcement kinetic — three vessels redirected, one disabled, the M/T Wen Yao boarded; General License X1 expired 17 Jul, completing the Article 10 reversal; the 30-day full-lift deadline passed in breach the same day. Hormuz (Art 5): the IRGC Navy's permission regime stated openly; a Thai-flagged vessel struck 17 Jul; Kpler counted eight transits on 16 Jul; UNCLOS-based “environmental services” fees floated. Strikes: sixth, seventh and eighth consecutive US nights (16–18 Jul) — Bandar Abbas bridges, rail and telecoms severed, Chabahar, Oghab 44, and the IRGC launch infrastructure behind the Gulf-state attacks; three US officials call them “shaping operations.” Retaliation: seven countries — Kuwait (power, desalination and oil facilities struck three days running), Bahrain, Qatar, Oman (US radar assets on the Salamah Archipelago and Ghanem Island), Saudi Arabia (al Kharj missile, sirens at Yanbu), Syria (al Tanf) and Jordan (Muwaffaq Salti, then four missiles at Aqaba on 19 Jul). Nuclear (Art 8): the AEOI says a US strike hit the Darkhovin plant (19 Jul); Araghchi calls some nuclear demands possibly “unresolvable.” Leadership: Araghchi disclosed a six-person SNSC special committee making on-the-spot decisions while Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei — severely injured in the strikes that killed his father — is reportedly hard to reach; ISW assesses Vahidi very likely a member. Yemen: Reuters (16 Jul, single root source) reports Iran instructed the Houthis to prepare to close Bab el-Mandeb if the US strikes Iranian power plants; Houthi-Saudi cross-border attacks resumed for the first time since 2022. Lebanon (Art 1): the LAF began deploying into the pilot zones 16–17 Jul — the one obligation moving forward.

Implementation — week 7 · v13

The window (20–23 Jul): the suspension holds — ISW's negotiations section still reads “nothing significant to report” — while a four-party written framework (Qatar, Egypt, Pakistan, Oman, ~20 Jul) revives the June interim agreement's shape: a 10-day ceasefire, both Hormuz lanes reopened (southern free of Iranian attack, northern free of US blockade), and the fee question posed as Malacca-style Iranian service fees or an IMO-administered fund. Iran confirmed receipt; no talks are scheduled. Second chokepoint: the Houthis blockaded Saudi Red Sea port calls from 20 Jul, citing the Saudi siege of Sana'a, and struck the Encelia and Layla on 22 Jul (one hit UKMTO-confirmed); Bab el-Mandeb transits fell 64 to 30, recovering to 40 by 23 Jul, with at least seven diversions and an EU Naval Force warning. Strikes: the eleventh and twelfth consecutive US nights reached Bushehr Airport, Larak Island, Behbahan (the IRGC 15th Special Forces Brigade base), western and north-western Iran, and the Parchin complex near Tehran; Iran struck Dhahran's King Abdulaziz Air Base (a first), US early-warning radar in Kuwait (an AN/FPS-117), Jordanian bases and Aqaba. Workarounds: Iran-Pakistan trade to expand from $3bn toward $10bn with five-fold truck traffic — the land answer to the naval blockade. Posture (23 Jul): Trump threatened Iran and the Houthis with “major military punishment” and told Axios he may expand strikes (“They haven't received enough pain yet”); equities fell and the 10Y hit a one-year high; dozens of fighters and refuellers continue moving to the region. Axis widening: Iranian-backed Iraqi militias likely droned Kuwait's Abdali border crossing on 23 Jul — the first Axis attack on a regional border crossing since 11 Jul — with explosions reported on Bubiyan Island; the IRGC claims a southern-corridor tanker struck a naval mine, a claim ISW cannot observe and reads as deterrence messaging against the Omani route.

05 · Stakeholder map

Actor positions through the Stalemate / Resolution lens.

The Roaring Lion A/B/C framework is the analytical reference: Stalemate ≈ persistent Bad B; Resolution ≈ Good A via JPOA 2.0 + Hormuz Council + Security Guarantee. The US and GCC cells move — the US on the framework-and-escalation two-path state, the GCC on the blockade and its mediators returning — and a Houthis / Yemen row enters the map with the second chokepoint; the Iran, Israel, China and Europe cells gain texture.

United States
The US version of the terms — dismantle the nuclear programme, end Axis support — is far harder than Iran’s, so the signed ceasefire sat on an unreconciled core throughout. Trump chose the blockade track on 13–14 Jul, reinstating it against Iranian-flagged shipping, and strikes reached roughly 200km inland by 15 Jul. The campaign then widened past every prior round: three consecutive further strike nights (sixth through eighth) interdicting Iran's internal logistics — the bridges, rail junction and telecoms feeding Bandar Abbas, whose port handles ~85% of Iran's container traffic — plus the IRGC launch infrastructure behind the Gulf-state attacks, and, per Iran's AEOI, the Darkhovin nuclear plant on 19 Jul. Two US service members were killed at Muwaffaq Salti Airbase on 17 Jul and a third in Iraqi Kurdistan on 18 Jul — the first US deaths of the conflict, with no US stand-down following them. Three US officials characterise the strikes as “shaping operations” (Reuters); more F-16s, F-35s and refuelling aircraft are moving to the region, plus additional refuellers to Israel (Axios); WSJ reports a Trump threat against Pickaxe Mountain. US officials also told the WSJ they suspect PRC satellite reconnaissance and Russian locational intelligence are supporting Iranian targeting of US assets. Trump formally notified Congress under the War Powers Resolution on 10 Jul, putting a 60-day clock under the campaign. The pattern this file tracked through four rounds — hardline rhetoric giving way to renewed contact — broke in the fifth: Tehran's suspension landed instead. The 20–23 Jul state holds two paths open at once: Washington is exploring the four-party framework (Axios) while massing forces — Trump played the talks down on 22 Jul, then threatened Iran and the Houthis with “major military punishment” on 23 Jul and told Axios he is close to a decision on expanding strikes — the US “prepared to launch a massive attack”; “They haven't received enough pain yet.” The eleventh and twelfth strike nights reached Parchin, Bushehr Airport, Larak Island and western/north-western Iran; blockade enforcement continues.
Framework in hand ·
“major punishment” vowed
Israel
Citrinowicz: intent intact, capacity to disrupt constrained. A “complete end to the war in Lebanon” is an explicit Iranian red line, so Israel’s Lebanon campaign is a direct obstacle to any settlement; Israel is not a party to the signed Clause 1 and remains in southern Lebanon. Israeli sources quoted by Nadav Eyal expect a prolonged “war between the wars” rather than full resumption, with Israel not wanting the MoU to collapse outright ahead of the 27 Oct elections. There is still no unilateral Israeli strike on Iran. Israel is nonetheless closer to the exchange than in any prior window: it intercepted an Iranian drone near the Syria border (19 Jul), its air defences near Eilat engaged debris from the Aqaba launch, and Defence Minister Katz warned Iran that Israel will re-engage if attacked. On the Lebanon front the Rome-brokered pilot-zones plan moved from structure to implementation — the Lebanese Armed Forces began deploying into the first zones on 16–17 Jul, with the next zone expected at Zawtar al Charqiyeh. Citrinowicz's current read (19 Jul): the greatest challenge is now escalation management — events slipping beyond anyone's control, mediators exhausted and losing leverage, key IRGC figures convinced by the funeral turnout that they have domestic backing to intensify. WSJ (22 Jul): Israel is deliberately sitting the campaign out — the trigger still unfired — while Axios reports the IDF preparing a possible full-scale joint campaign within days and Netanyahu warning that any Iranian attack draws massive strikes.
Good A/C →
regime change
Iran
Fractured — and the hardline camp is winning the fracture. Iran’s primary Hormuz objective is recognised sovereignty over the strait; it has used force to assert that control five times now, striking vessels on the Omani route and retaliating against the Gulf hosts, and on 16 Jul it named the strait an “inviolable red line.” On 18 Jul Tehran formally suspended the MoU — Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei declared Trump's signature “invalid,” Deputy FM Gharibabadi announced the suspension — and the red-line warning became execution: retaliation reached seven countries, adding Saudi Arabia (first strike on Saudi territory in about three months, with sirens at Yanbu), Syria (al Tanf) and southern Jordan (Aqaba), and Kuwaiti power, desalination and oil facilities were struck three days running. The killing of US troops crossed the one line every prior round had observed. The regime is split: Rezaei declares diplomacy over and threatens “full-scale destruction,” while Ghalibaf — branded a traitor by hardliners — defended the talks track on state TV on 16 Jul and a Nour News op-ed still argues for negotiation; Araghchi says some nuclear demands may remain “unresolvable.” Decision-making has its own new structure: Araghchi disclosed a six-person SNSC special committee (himself, Pezeshkian, Ghalibaf, Zolghadr, two unnamed — ISW assesses Vahidi very likely one) making on-the-spot decisions while Mojtaba, severely injured in the strikes that killed his father and unseen in public since, is reportedly hard to reach. Vali Nasr's structural read (FT): the MoU froze a balance of power both sides set out to change, Tehran has concluded restraint invites pressure, and it bets that closing Hormuz — potentially with Bab el-Mandeb and the Red Sea — forces Washington to blink first. This window Iran confirmed receipt of the four-party framework while vowing to “crush the source of US aggression” (Baghaei), put first missiles on Dhahran's King Abdulaziz Air Base, targeted US early-warning radar in Kuwait, and opened a land-trade workaround with Pakistan ($3bn toward $10bn).
MoU suspended ·
Gulf-wide retaliation
executed
China
Publicly pushes Resolution (ceasefire, lanes reopened, Xi/Putin Beijing meeting), but the pivot is partly performative — PRC kept sending missile components and fuel precursors during the war. Per Pape, the war already shifted Beijing’s read of US resolve; munitions depletion (FT, Global21) reinforces it. Two new data points this window: US officials told the WSJ they suspect a Chinese reconnaissance satellite is supporting Iranian targeting of US assets — a step past performative neutrality if borne out — and China's state LNG buyers are contracting around the Gulf structurally (Qatari LNG to China down ~98% year on year Apr–Jun; Sinopec and PetroChina exploring 10+ year non-Gulf supply), the first permanent demand-side Gulf de-risking of the war. The intake's China read (Xie, Zakaria; circulated by David for the six-month scenario work): Beijing is absorbing the shock from its ~1.3–1.4bn-barrel reserve rather than cushioning prices for others, imports are down ~3.5–4mn bpd on stockpile draw, and some Hormuz transits reportedly settled in renminbi.
Pushes Resolution
Russia
Aligned with China publicly; has formally offered to take Iran’s HEU (acknowledged by Araghchi, “not currently under discussion”). Accepting HEU under the Security Guarantee architecture would be the technical pathway. Preserves Iran ties privately (drones via Caspian). WSJ (18 Jul): US officials suspect Russia is sharing locational intelligence on US warships and aircraft and advising on drone tactics.
HEU custodian role available
Houthis / Yemen
The second front has an owner. The blockade of Saudi port calls — declared 20 Jul by spokesman Yahya Saree after two days of warnings to Saudi-owned ships, citing the Saudi siege of Sana'a — is the Houthis' first direct move against Riyadh in this war, and enforcement is calibrated: claimed missile and drone strikes on the Encelia and Layla (22 Jul), one hit confirmed by UK Maritime Trade Operations, a bow fire and no casualties per Saudi state media. Bab el-Mandeb transits fell from 64 to 30 before recovering to 40 by 23 Jul; at least seven vessels diverted and southern-Red-Sea war-risk cover doubled for some carriers. The Iranian hand is documented alongside the Houthis' own agenda: Reuters and ISW report a 13 Jul Mahan Air flight into Yemen carrying 10–21 IRGC personnel including senior commanders, missile- and drone-related equipment, training for “new missile systems” and gold to fund operations — diverted to Hudaydah when Saudi-backed forces struck Sanaa airport to block the landing. ISW: the Houthis “pursue their own agenda, but they remain a key Iranian partner,” and the Red Sea expansion serves Iran's campaign objective — degrading US willingness and ability to keep striking, and coercing acceptance of Iranian control over Hormuz. Trump fuses the two: the Houthis are a “Surrogate and/or Proxy of Iran,” and further attacks bring “major military punishment” on both. The stated off-ramp is bilateral — the blockade holds until Saudi Arabia lifts its blockade on Yemeni ports and airports.
Blockade on Saudi calls ·
Iranian hand reported
GCC / Pakistan
Mediation delivered the ceasefire, and all GCC bar Oman reject permanent Iranian control of Hormuz; the bloc’s exposure has widened round by round as Iran coerces the hosts. This window the bloc absorbed the heaviest and widest round yet: Kuwait's civilian critical infrastructure struck three days running, Saudi territory struck for the first time in months with sirens at Yanbu — the Red Sea terminal now carrying ~4mn bpd, ~60% of pre-war Saudi crude exports, as the Hormuz bypass — and Oman moved from rebuffed mediator to target, with US radar assets on Omani territory struck and Tehran accusing Muscat of yielding to US pressure. Saudi Arabia, the UAE and Qatar condemned the infrastructure strikes and Jordan summoned an Iranian diplomat. The Yemen dimension deepened: Houthi-Saudi cross-border attacks resumed for the first time since 2022, and Reuters reports Iran instructed the Houthis to prepare to close Bab el-Mandeb — which would trap the Yanbu bypass and put Abqaiq, the single upstream node behind it, at the centre of the board. Kuwait is reportedly negotiating an expanded defence pact with Pakistan (Reuters, early stage). A Bloomberg feature flags the ~$3tn Saudi-UAE rivalry for regional capital as a fault line between the two bypass-hosting states. The prepared closure executed this window: a declared blockade of Saudi Red Sea port calls from 20 Jul and claimed strikes on two Saudi tankers on 22 Jul (one UKMTO-confirmed), with transits down 64 to 30 before recovering to 40 and war-risk cover doubling — the full picture is in the Houthis / Yemen row above. Saudi territory took its first Dhahran strike, and the bloc's mediators answered at larger scale: the Qatar/Egypt/Pakistan/Oman framework is the bloc's own product. The Saudi–Pakistan airspace cross-promise still binds Israeli unilateral action.
Blockaded at Bab el-Mandeb ·
mediation retried
Europe
Aligns with the diplomatic track; refused the US request to reopen the Strait, so it is constrained from leading any post-war architecture. A NATO contribution to the HEU Security Guarantee would be the test. Switzerland hosted the formal ceremony and the first implementation round; the post-war security architecture would sit with other parties. The UK moved first: Trump and PM Burnham agreed on 19 Jul that Britain will take an active role in demining and reopening Hormuz — the first operational European alignment with the reopening effort, alongside the defensive-mandate EU naval presence.
Diplomatic backer
Japan / Korea
Lean on the US for Resolution; ~60% of energy via Hormuz. The US warned Japan of up-to-two-year Tomahawk delays as the Pentagon prioritises Middle East restocking — US overstretch landing on an Indo-Pacific ally, reinforcing the hedge. Dormant nuclear conversations re-open if the scenario shifts to apex. Capital Economics names Japan, with Pakistan and the Philippines, among the economies most exposed to physical fuel shortage under a dual-chokepoint closure.
Hedging

The 8 Jun – 23 Jul: whipsaw, signing, breakdown, a war with US casualties, and a second chokepoint

8–17 Jun
Ceasefire collapses then resolves onto de-escalation: Israeli and US strikes inside Iran (8–10 Jun); Trump declares the deal “complete” (15 Jun); the US and Iran sign the MoU two days early (17 Jun), with signed edits to Clause 1 (Lebanon) and Clause 5 (Hormuz). The blockade lifts and oil sanctions waive on signing.
19 Jun–12 Jul
Implementation, then four escalation rounds of rising scale: the first 60-day-track round at Bürgenstock (21–22 Jun); the Ever Lovely exchange and stand-down (25–30 Jun); the tanker attacks and oil-waiver revocation (6–8 Jul); the ~140-target wave reaching central Iran, five-Gulf-state retaliation, and Iran's rejection of Oman's two-corridor proposal (9–12 Jul). Talks resume at Iran's request (10 Jul) even as Trump maintains the ceasefire is over.
13–16 Jul
The blockade returns (14 Jul), targeted at Iranian-flagged shipping; the 20% fee floats and drops within a day; strikes reach roughly 200km inland (15 Jul); Iran strikes three vessels on the Omani route, retaliates against Kuwait, Bahrain and Jordan, and on 16 Jul declares the strait an “inviolable red line.” The Iran-Oman channel goes quiet; the mediation-breakdown trigger fires (v11).
17 Jul
An Iranian missile attack on Muwaffaq Salti Airbase, Jordan kills two US service members, with one missing — the first US deaths of the conflict. The sixth consecutive US strike night severs the bridges, rail and telecoms feeding Bandar Abbas; Iran strikes a Kuwaiti power-and-desalination plant and, for the first time, near al Tanf in Syria; a Thai-flagged vessel is struck on the Omani route; Kpler counts eight strait transits the prior day. General License X1 expires, revoking all Iranian-oil transaction authority; the MoU's 30-day full-lift deadline passes in breach. Murrin publishes WW3 116, declaring Chapter Two of the war.
18 Jul
Tehran formally suspends the MoU: Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei declares Trump's signature “invalid” and Deputy FM Gharibabadi announces suspension of commitments. The seventh US strike night; Iran fires a ballistic missile at a US base near al Kharj, Saudi Arabia — its first strike on Saudi territory in about three months, with sirens at Yanbu — and hits Kuwaiti power, desalination and oil facilities a second day. Brent settles ~$88, up 4.6%, the biggest weekly advance since April. A third US service member is fatally wounded in Iraqi Kurdistan disposing of ordnance from a downed Iranian drone.
19 Jul
CENTCOM announces the third US death. Iran's AEOI says a US strike hit the Darkhovin nuclear plant in Khuzestan (IAEA: very early construction, no nuclear material at the last inspection). Four ballistic missiles at Aqaba, southern Jordan — a first; Kuwaiti infrastructure hit a third day running. Araghchi disclosed a six-person SNSC committee deciding on the spot while the Supreme Leader is reportedly hard to reach, and says some nuclear demands may remain “unresolvable.” The eighth consecutive US strike night (18 Jul) included the IRGC unit behind the Jordan attack.
20 Jul
The IRGC Navy states its permission regime openly after halting four vessels: “not a single drop of oil, gas, or chemical fertilizer” passes without its coordination. The US moves more F-16s, F-35s and refuellers to the region; Israel's Katz warns Iran that Israel will re-engage if attacked. Saudi Arabia, the UAE and Qatar condemn the infrastructure strikes; Jordan summons an Iranian diplomat; China and Pakistan urge resumed dialogue. The Houthis declare a maritime blockade on Saudi Arabia effective immediately (Yahya Saree), citing the Saudi siege of Sana'a; the Qatar/Egypt/Pakistan/Oman written framework circulates, and Iran's FM spokesperson confirms receipt.
21–22 Jul
The eleventh and twelfth consecutive US strike nights reach Bushehr Airport, Larak Island, Behbahan, western and north-western Iran, and the Parchin complex near Tehran. Iran puts first missiles on Dhahran's King Abdulaziz Air Base and strikes US early-warning radar in Kuwait. The Houthi blockade turns kinetic: missile and drone strikes claimed on the Saudi tankers Encelia and Layla (one hit later UKMTO-confirmed), Bab el-Mandeb transits down 64 to 30, an EU Naval Force warning out. Ukraine strikes the Caspian Pipeline Consortium, halting ~1mn bpd of Kazakh flow. Brent settles $88.52 on 21 Jul, the highest since 11 Jun.
23 Jul
Brent settles $100.69, up 7%, after touching $102 — the first settle above $100 since May — on the Red Sea tanker attacks. Trump threatens Iran and the Houthis with “major military punishment” if the attacks continue — the Houthis named a “Surrogate and/or Proxy of Iran” — and tells Axios he may expand strikes (“They haven't received enough pain yet”), squaring the desk digest's “major operation” line. US equities fall (S&P -1.2%, Nasdaq -2.2%, Tesla and Alphabet earnings-day falls compounding) and the 10Y moves through 4.65% to 4.69%. Rubio: Tehran's leadership is “not ready to make a deal” (BBC). Dozens of fighters and refuellers continue deploying; no talks are scheduled on the framework. UKMTO confirms a Saudi-tanker hit; transits recover to 40. Iranian-backed Iraqi militias likely drone Kuwait's Abdali border crossing (material damage, no casualties) with explosions on Bubiyan Island; the IRGC claims a southern-corridor tanker mined — unobserved by ISW. The 13th consecutive US strike night follows into 24 Jul, with missiles reported on Qeshm Island in the strait; the House votes to direct a halt to the campaign, and the Senate blocks a similar measure hours later.
What it confirms: the contest now runs across two chokepoints, and a war path and a framework path are open at once — a written proposal with confirmed receipt sitting against a threatened expansion of the campaign. What it does not change: the bifurcated architecture, the apex held in the long-term allocation, the Murrin DD, the Brent bands. Watch-list: no new trigger fires this window. israeli-strike stays unfired — WSJ reports Israel deliberately sitting out while the IDF prepares options. The mediation development runs opposite to the fired breakdown trigger: a framework tabled, no table convened. Saudi-Pakistan airspace: Pakistan appears as mediator and trade partner, with no collapse content. The live channels: whether talks convene on the four-party framework, whether the threatened expansion launches, the Bab el-Mandeb enforcement scope against non-Saudi shipping, and the Lebanon pilot zones — the LAF search-mechanism question now with the judiciary.
The map — the deal suspends, the contest turns to leverage destruction

Stalemate stays modal because the US–Iran nuclear divide is wide and the hardliner faction blocks Good A. The ceasefire was a Stage-1 instrument that deferred every core; five escalation rounds of rising scale tested it, and in the fifth Tehran suspended its commitments outright while the exchange produced the first US deaths. The question v11 carried — whether hardline rhetoric again gives way to renewed contact — resolved toward breakdown: Ghalibaf's 16 Jul defence of the talks track was followed within two days by the suspension, and ISW records nothing on the negotiations file. What replaces it as the open question is narrower: whether a successor venue convenes (Qatar, Pakistan and Turkey circulate in intake reporting), and whether the split inside Tehran — a six-person committee deciding while the Supreme Leader is reportedly incapacitated, hardliners demanding revenge, Ghalibaf and Araghchi still holding the negotiation brief — resolves toward a reopened channel. The structural fault ISW identifies — Clause 5 read by Washington as restored passage and by Tehran as affirmed Iranian authority — is the same unresolved question the file has carried since v3, now stated by the primary source. v13 answers the venue half: the framework exists, in writing, with receipt confirmed; what has not happened is a table. The test the window sets is near-term — talks convene on the framework, or the escalation the President threatened on 23 Jul launches.

06 · Market & thematic implications

Scenario views across equities, Brent, rates, and rotation.

Views only. Brent bands and the scenario architecture are unchanged from v12; the second chokepoint is live at Bab el-Mandeb, and settled prints press the floor of the Resolution band.

ScenarioEquitiesBrentRatesSector & thematic rotation
Stalemate
Modal		 Mid-teens downside; EPS holds until ~2027 demand destruction. The 17 Jul session saw a broad US fall with SPX ending the week -1.5%, about 2% off its highs; Vanda (18 Jul) reads the right tail as “all but disappeared” with positioning still elevated and systematic flows biased down — the modal outcome flat, the skew down. The oil-to-equity transmission has compressed: roughly 1.5% S&P downside per 10% oil rise, versus ~4% in March. The 23 Jul session: Dow -1.0%, S&P -1.2%, Nasdaq -2.2% — oil pressure compounded by Tesla (-14.5%) and Alphabet (-7.1%) earnings-day falls — with European energy outperforming (Shell +1.6%, BP +3.1%) and American and Southwest down 8.4% and 6.2% on fuel bills despite earnings beats (AP). Tight, elevated, drawing inventory. The prints landed: Brent closed $82.96 on 16 Jul (desk series) and settled ~$88 on 17 Jul per Bloomberg — up 4.6%, the biggest weekly advance since April, on the troop deaths, the Saudi extension and the Axios refueller report — with the front month in backwardation (~-$1.3), the prompt-tightness signature of a strait that has not reopened. Kpler counted eight transits on 16 Jul. SPR 316.5m barrels (10 Jul, lowest since April 1983, ~34m contracted barrels left); total US crude stocks 730.8m, lowest since 1984; US gasoline ~$4.50–4.56. The gas complex has no bypass shield: Asia LNG +94% and TTF +79% versus pre-war. External calls bracket this file's bands — Barclays $65–80 below the floor (carried watch item), Murrin above $150 (his call, logged at section 01), CE +$15–20/bbl on a dual-chokepoint closure. This window: Brent settled $88.52 on 21 Jul, $89.75 intraday on 22 Jul, then $100.69 on 23 Jul — up 7%, $102 intraday, highest since May, YTD +62% (AP) — the first settled print inside the $90–110 band, on the Red Sea tanker attacks; three weeks earlier it was below $72. Gasoline prints diverge and are flagged: AAA $4.09 per AP (23 Jul) against the desk monitor's $4.65 AAA series (20 Jul), with the DOE weekly at $4.00; UK gas ~150p/therm from 98p at end-June (BBC). TTF took its first EUR60 handle since March and Asia LNG has doubled versus pre-war; the CPC strike subtracts ~1mn bpd of Kazakh supply outside the Gulf. The dual-chokepoint contingency is partially live: Saudi-flagged traffic is blocked at Bab el-Mandeb while Hormuz runs the IRGC permission regime. The re-closure scenario the market prices as a ~$150 shock remains the near-term, non-nuclear face of the escalation leg. Fiscal pressure lifts yields; energy tightening Jul–Sep adds to the curve. The 10Y sits near 4.60–4.65% (Vanda); CE traces the June rise in the 30-year mortgage rate (6.59%) partly to Iran-driven Treasury yields. The 10Y closed 4.628% on 21 Jul — 2.2bp from the shock monitor's Stage-4 threshold, the war's closest approach — then moved through it on 23 Jul — 4.69%, a one-year high (3.97% pre-war); the 30Y at 5.131%; MOVE back above its pre-war level; Fed-hike odds for next week jumped to 36% from 12% (CME via AP), and the ECB held. Iran-axis exposure screens unattractive on the retaliation vectors (UAE data centres, regional fintech, Gulf cable-dependent payments) — Jordan and Oman directly-struck hosts. The screen widens on two fronts: Gulf civilian utilities and energy infrastructure are now realised targets (Kuwait's desalination-dependent water system; the KPC oil facility), and the Red Sea / Bab el-Mandeb vector puts the Yanbu bypass (~4mn bpd, ~60% of pre-war Saudi crude exports) and Abqaiq — the single upstream node behind it — on the board. China's LNG contracting away from Qatar (down ~98% year on year) is the first structural demand-side shift. China sleeve re-rates up on the Beijing read of US weakness; munitions depletion (FT, Global21) is the observable marker. Energy-paradigm shift favours rare earths, security and infrastructure; defensive on regional refining. The undersea-cables / SWIFT non-oil vector carries. Two additions: the Red Sea leg is live risk (war-risk cover pulled on Saudi calls and southern-Red-Sea cover doubled for some carriers on 23 Jul — Reuters; the ~2.5mn bpd Asia-bound Yanbu leg rerouting via the Cape at 10–14 extra days), and a reported IRGC strike on an Amazon data centre in Bahrain (13D, single report) moves Gulf digital infrastructure from screened vector to claimed event.
Resolution
Suspended		 Mid-single-digit upside. Declines toward $90–110 over months; market below the band throughout the deal's life — $82.96–~$88 on this window's settled prints, the move driven by escalation with no Resolution progress behind it — pricing a clean reopening the substance does not support; trust rebuild takes quarters. Yields ease as the energy premium unwinds. Iran-axis exposure screens better selectively; the energy/renewables opportunity set persists on the structural energy-paradigm shift; the China sleeve recalibrates on a firmer Hormuz-access outcome.

Apex / tail: escalation toward $130–160+ Brent is held in the long-term allocation; first-use ($200+, SPX ~3,500) sits below the scenario-modelling threshold as structural tail protection. The deal's re-timing of this tail to the 60-day window is itself suspended as of 18 Jul, so the window is open-ended; the HEU stays in Iran, hardened, and unmentioned in this window's exchanges. The Darkhovin strike is the first kinetic touch on the nuclear file this cycle — an early-construction site with no nuclear material at the last IAEA inspection — and Murrin's $150+ call sits inside this leg as his conviction, ahead of the file's own. Two proliferation-adjacent data enter the log: WSJ reports Washington will greenlight a 30-year US-Saudi civil nuclear agreement potentially permitting Saudi enrichment, and the Pickaxe Mountain chatter extends to unattributed tactical-nuclear talk (13D) — both logged, neither carried. The Taiwan-war and US-default extrapolations are held as backdrop only, outside the modelled set.

The $300bn in the signed terms is external Gulf investment funded by sanctions relief — not a US Treasury outlay — which reconciles the terms with Iran’s frozen-asset demand; the relief flows through GCC capital.

Resolution mechanics

The Resolution path follows the framework David specified: Mearsheimer’s “humble pie” off-ramp; a JPOA 2.0 nuclear architecture; the Ferguson Hormuz Council (Iran as Chair, a 45% / 30% / balance revenue split, transit fees at cost); HEU export under a Security Guarantee with Russia available as custodian; and a post-war Gulf Treaty Organisation with China and Russia as signatories. The realised Stage-1 MOU diverged from the Council mechanic on Hormuz — toll-free reopening under Iranian and Omani management rather than a chaired revenue split — and the deeper cores (HEU ~440kg, enrichment, sanctions sequencing) remained for the 60-day track. Bremmer's independent framing of the eventual off-ramp — institutionalised, divided Iranian management funded by a Gulf collective, on the Bosphorus or Malacca model — carries as a variant of the deferred mechanism. This window put the primary source behind the fault line: ISW assesses the two sides interpreted Clause 5 in fundamentally different ways — Washington as a return to pre-war passage, Tehran as affirmation of Iranian authority with safe passage only through Iran's traffic-separation scheme — and that the divergence contributed to the collapse; the Gulf states rejected Iranian management and fees. Nasr's structural read runs the same direction from the Iranian side: the MoU froze a balance of power both parties set out to change, so the return to war is deliberate policy rather than a misreading of terms. With the track suspended and Oman compromised as a channel, the mechanics carry unchanged as the reference for whatever venue eventually resumes them — Qatar, Pakistan and Turkey circulate in intake reporting as candidates, with none convened. The four-party framework is the first written variant of the deferred mechanism to reach the desk: dual-lane reopening with the fee question posed as Malacca-style Iranian service fees or an IMO-administered fund — the sovereignty-versus-fees question the file has carried since v3, now in proposal text.

v14 · Mutual pause, Hormuz table convenes · 29 Jul 2026
Architecture, verdict & Brent bands held · US chip flipped · Iran chip flipped · Resolution tier Suspended → Hormuz track convened · Brent retreats below the band floor · Essential Lessons Part I graded into the DD · no new watch-list trigger

The architecture holds — Stalemate (modal) / Resolution (Hormuz track convened) / apex in the long-term allocation. The 24–28 Jul window reversed direction. The US conducted no strikes in Iran after 24 Jul — the first pause in 13 nights, the hold reportedly ordered hours after an Omani delegation arrived in Tehran — and Iranian attacks wound down with it, one final strike on a US base in Bahrain in the first window, then none (ISW); a senior Iranian official told Reuters (26 Jul) the halt holds as long as Washington's does, with the message conveyed directly. The table the four-party framework could not produce has convened in a narrower form: Iranian and Omani delegations met in Tehran on 24–25 Jul on restarting strait traffic — the lead proposal reopening the likely-mined middle passage — with Qatar, Pakistan, Egypt and the Witkoff/Kushner envoy channel involved and Omani negotiators expecting announceable progress within days (Bloomberg); a Hormuz agreement would gate resumed talks on ending the war. ISW's 28 Jul edition carries the counter-read: the mediators' formal 10-day truce proposal — the strait reopened against the blockade lifted — is meeting Iranian refusal to concede control of the strait. The market moved first — Brent fell roughly 16% over three sessions, the worst run since April 2020, settling $84.09 on 28 Jul, back below the $90–110 floor on positioning — Hormuz flow has not normalised. The Saudi front did not pause: Houthi missiles targeted Yanbu (intercepted) and impacted Jizan, the oil-transport corridor to the Red Sea hubs was claimed struck (the Abqaiq fire unclaimed by any actor), Saudi-reported shoot-downs of drones from Iraq aimed at Riyadh and Eastern Province petroleum facilities, and US blockade enforcement turned lethal at the Lavine. Two chips move; the Resolution tier moves to Hormuz track convened; the bands hold.

What moved from v13:

  • The strikes stopped: no US overnight wave into 25 Jul — the first pause since 12 Jul — after Trump reportedly ordered a hold hours into the Omani delegation's Tehran visit, and none has followed through the 28 Jul ISW cutoff. Iranian attacks on US bases and Gulf hosts ended with a single Bahrain strike in the first window; a senior Iranian official told Reuters (26 Jul) Iran will hold as long as the US does, conveyed to Washington directly. ISW's caution rides along: Iran uses pauses to reconstitute, as it did after the 8 Apr ceasefire.
  • The table convened — Hormuz first: Iranian and Omani delegations met in Tehran 24–25 Jul on restarting strait traffic — one proposal reopens the likely-mined middle passage, with the UK and France offering demining once safe and Iran seeking a joint management system likely to include fees (Bloomberg, 27 Jul). Qatar, Pakistan and Egypt are involved alongside Witkoff and Kushner; Omani negotiators expect announceable progress within days; Hegseth is pressing the UK to host a maritime-security summit. Baghaei claims “progress” while stating strait traffic is unchanged, and Iranian parliamentarians insist control is not returning to pre-war conditions. A Hormuz agreement would gate resumed war-ending talks. The terms are now reported (ISW, 28 Jul): a formal 10-day truce — Iran reopens the strait; the US lifts the naval blockade and considers unfreezing assets — plus an Omani proposal for a regional consortium to manage the strait, the shape the defunct MoU's Clause 5 consultation required. Iran is refusing to concede control (US official and mediators to Western media, 27 Jul); ISW reads the refusal as showing Iran may calculate it can win the blockade-lift, or further economic concessions, without giving up the strait.
  • US chip flips: “Framework in hand · 'major punishment' vowed” → “Strikes halted 24 Jul · 'deep talks' claimed.” The threatened expansion resolved into a hold: a White House options meeting on 24 Jul, then the ordered pause; White House communications director Steven Cheung (26 Jul) frames the stance as diplomacy-preferred with military options live if Iran attacks allies or strait shipping; Trump says the US “is talking,” tells Axios “deep talks” are under way, and met Netanyahu in Washington (Bloomberg, 28 Jul), telling reporters there is a “good chance” talks with Iran make progress. The blockade stays, and enforcement turned lethal — the LPG carrier Lavine disabled on 24 Jul, two sailors reported killed per Iranian media; the Charminar boarded 25 Jul and released.
  • Iran chip flips: “MoU suspended · Gulf-wide retaliation executed” → “Reciprocal pause · strait red line held.” The suspension stands and no attack has followed the first window's single Bahrain strike; the stated terms are unchanged — officials tell the NYT Iran accepts no temporary deal that leaves Hormuz status unresolved. The NYT-reported 23 Jul rejection of the US framework carried by Iraqi PM Zaydi is held as contested — Zaydi's office denied it on 24 Jul. Mojtaba Khamenei is reported out of contact “due to certain issues” (Haddad Adel, 25 Jul), extending the incapacitation question, and his 26 Jul statement makes Hezbollah's preservation the “first condition” for resolving the war. ISW's 28 Jul edition maps three factions on negotiations policy — an IRGC-Vahidi line (negotiate, keep total control) likely guiding it, a Pezeshkian/Araghchi/Ghalibaf camp differing over means (it too seeks control, defined less expansively), and the Paydari Front rejecting talks outright — against an economy ISW pairs with the first mass protest since January (Malekshahr, 28 Jul) and a US intelligence assessment that the regime struggles to pay its forces.
  • The Saudi front ran through the pause: Houthi missiles at Yanbu intercepted by Greek-operated Saudi defences while munitions impacted Jizan (24 Jul), the NCC Masa attacked, claimed strikes on the oil-transport corridor linking the eastern fields to the Red Sea hubs (27 Jul), and a fire at Abqaiq — unclaimed by any actor, documented by satellite. Saudi Arabia says it shot down drones from Iraq aimed at Riyadh and Eastern Province petroleum facilities (27 Jul; ISW assesses Iranian-backed Iraqi militias, preserving Tehran's deniability); the Saudi coalition struck Hudaydah, Jawf and Marib; the Houthis downed a Saudi Akinci drone and vow enforcement until the Sana'a blockade lifts. Bab el-Mandeb enforcement is tied to the Sana'a blockade, so the US-Iran pause does not bind it. A third claimed strike on Saudi shipping followed on 28 Jul — ballistic missiles on the tanker NCC Ghazal, UKMTO-unconfirmed — while the Islamic Resistance in Iraq denies the attacks originated from Iraq and Baghdad opens an investigation, meeting Saudi officials on 28 Jul.
  • Prints round-trip the band — bands hold: Brent settled $84.09 on 28 Jul (WTI $79.26), roughly 16% off the 23 Jul $100.69 in three sessions — the worst run since April 2020 — with CTA Brent length cut 73% → 62% in a day and CIBC reading the move as positioning on “MOU 2.0” hopes; Hormuz physical flow has not normalised and dark transits continue. December Brent futures sit below $82.30. Macquarie projects a ~2mn bpd Q4 surplus if a deal lands; OPEC+ is expected to pause quota hikes after September. The $90–110 floor and the escalation legs are untouched.
  • Murrin DD increment: “Essential Lessons from the Iran Conflict — Part I” (21 Jul; via Gary, 26 Jul) is graded claim by claim at section 01 — two lessons corroborated by ISW's own assessments (air power failing to move the strait file; pauses as reconstitution), the credibility verdict a live dispute between Pape and Mead, the inevitability prediction his conviction. His WW3 116 oil-above-$150 call now tracks against the round trip through $84.09, with its “weeks ahead” horizon not fully elapsed. Houthi negotiator Abdul Salam's 24 Jul statement — the blockade applies to Saudi Arabia alone — and ISW's record of the Houthis resisting Iran's full-closure ask for months add a datum toward his agency read.
  • No new watch-list trigger fires. The iran-formal-response candidate — the NYT-reported rejection via the Zaydi channel — is denied by the carrier's own office and stays a contested claim; the reported refusal inside the Omani track (27 Jul) is negotiation-state reporting within convened talks, short of a formal response. Mediation moved toward convening — the standing trigger covers breakdown, so the move is carried through the Resolution tier. israeli-strike stays unfired: Israel remains out of the campaign and the Abqaiq fire is unclaimed. Pakistan appears as mediator and proposal co-author, with no airspace content.

The scenario architecture, the verdict on Murrin Article 2, the Brent bands and the apex placement are unchanged.

Bottom line · a mutual pause holds; the Hormuz table convenes

The scenario set stays bifurcated: Stalemate (modal) and Resolution (Hormuz track convened), with the apex tail held in the long-term allocation below the scenario-modelling threshold. The US halted its strike campaign after 13 consecutive nights — the hold reportedly ordered hours after an Omani delegation reached Tehran on 24 Jul — and Iranian attacks ended with a single Bahrain strike in the first window — Tehran telling Washington directly it will hold as long as the US does (Reuters, 26 Jul). Iranian and Omani negotiators are working a restart of strait traffic — the lead proposal reopening the likely-mined middle passage, UK/French demining on offer, joint management with fees the Iranian ask — with Qatar, Pakistan, Egypt and the Witkoff/Kushner channel involved; a Hormuz agreement would gate resumed war-ending talks, and Omani negotiators expect announceable progress within days. The suspension of the MoU stands on paper and Iran's red line is unmoved — no temporary deal that leaves Hormuz status unresolved; the NYT-reported 23 Jul rejection via Iraqi PM Zaydi is carried as contested after his office denied it. The Saudi front did not pause: missiles at Yanbu and Jizan, a fire at Abqaiq unclaimed by any actor, Saudi-reported drone shoot-downs from Iraq, the blockade of Saudi Red Sea calls enforced, a third claimed attack on Saudi shipping on 28 Jul. ISW's 28 Jul edition adds the counter-read inside the talks: a formal 10-day truce is tabled — the strait reopened against the blockade lifted — and Iran is refusing to concede control. Brent gave the escalation premium back — roughly 16% in three sessions to an $84.09 settle on 28 Jul, below the $90–110 floor, with Hormuz traffic still muted and transponders dark; Fed-hike odds sit near one-in-three into the FOMC. Murrin’s Article 2 nuclear scenario remains the analytical foundation under structural tail protection. Full scenario stances, drivers, equity and Brent views are in section 03.

01 · Due Diligence on Murrin

Verdict unchanged — cannot rule it out. Essential Lessons Part I is retrospective; the tiers hold.

Capabilities corroborated (Tier 1); interpretive layers held as hypotheses (Tier 2); the policy conclusion treated as advocacy (Tier 3). The full claim-by-claim verifiability table sits in the v1 tab.

Murrin’s capabilities case is sound and OSINT-sourced. Timeline sits at the aggressive end of a 6–12 month band that overlaps Roaring Lion’s own assessment. The preemptive-nuclear-use conclusion is his advocacy. The Gallant “440 Kilograms” piece restates the ~440kg 60% HEU stockpile that anchors the capabilities case, and ISW, citing five US-intelligence sources via CNN, reports Iran is physically hardening that stockpile — collapsing tunnels and mining storage entrances to defeat any seizure — so the fissile core is intact, deferred, and being entrenched. Iran agreed to readmit IAEA inspectors, the first forward step on the nuclear file; it is not down-blending. That step has stalled: the inspection access is unresolved, with Grossi expecting entry while Iran ties it to a final deal and sanctions relief, and Iran has signalled it could suspend the talks over the armed exchange. The ~440kg core stays inside Iran behind the entrenched tunnels. “Cannot rule out the nuclear scenario” holds as the analytical foundation; the apex is handled via the long-term allocation rather than modelled scenarios.

WW3 116, “The Battle for the Strait is On, so Prepare for Escalation” (17 Jul), is the tracked author's read of the current exchange. Murrin declares “Chapter Two” of the war — an existential contest for the strait and the Red Sea sea lanes that in his framing ends only with a victor and a vanquished. It is commentary rather than reporting: twenty-four distinct claims with no primary text of his own behind them, and the two that carry real weight are corroborated from outside the piece. Four of his headline claims have nothing on this desk that tests them either way.

ClaimTierChecked againstOur read
The MoU. An appeasement dwarfing Chamberlain's, and in full form it would have destroyed US maritime hegemony at a stroke. Interpretive The signed 14-point text is on file. ISW (18 Jul) attributes the collapse to divergent readings of Clause 5 — Iran reading it as affirming Iranian authority over the strait, Washington expecting pre-war traffic conditions. Corroborated at the level of mechanism: Clause 5 is where the deal failed, the leg he identified on signing day from the published clauses. The Chamberlain scaling and the hegemony conclusion are his register and carry no checkable content.
Breakout. The IRGC used the pause to repair and replenish while moving closer to a weapon day by day. Advocacy The IAEA puts the stockpile at 441kg of 60% HEU as of June 2025; ISW, citing US intelligence via CNN, reports it being physically hardened in tunnels. Nothing on the desk tests imminence. Retention and hardening are corroborated and stand independently of him. The imminence claim is his third dated one and carries no enrichment figure, no breakout estimate and no date, so it cannot be tested on its own terms. Timing stays at the aggressive end of the 6–12 month band.
Total war. An existential contest for the shipping lanes, resolved only with a victor and a vanquished; America should commit to defeating the IRGC with overwhelming firepower. Advocacy A prescription. No source is offered and nothing on the desk bears on it. Reported as his conviction and kept in quotation. The escalation direction is consistent with the 6–19 Jul record. The grand-strategy prescription carries no falsifiable content.
Trump's resolve. Hardened: the midterm gas-price motive is dead on poor ratings, and he reads Iran as having acted in bad faith. Interpretive The “scum” remark is on the record from the Ankara NATO summit and already carried here. Tehran suspended the MoU on 18 Jul. No polling has reached the desk. The hardening direction is consistent with the observable sequence. The popularity leg is asserted without a pollster and is not carried. His own oil call runs against it — a print above $150 would restore the gas-price motive he argues is dead.
Assassination plot. A reported IRGC plot against Trump, passed via Israeli intelligence, corroborated by the Secret Service switching him back to the older Air Force One, and possibly the decisive factor driving him to destroy the IRGC. Interpretive A single unnamed Israeli-intelligence channel, hedged by Murrin himself. The aircraft switch is asserted without source and is unverified here, including the donor state of the aircraft he names. Uncorroborated on this desk. That the switch evidences the plot is his inference rather than anyone's report. The escalation-driver conclusion rests on that unverified chain plus a psychological read of Trump.
China and Russia. Iran fights on with their approval, confirmed by supplies flowing into its replenishment; US strikes on supply-route bridges acknowledge it. Interpretive ISW (17–18 Jul) confirms strikes on bridges, roads, a tunnel and telecoms around Bandar Abbas, a senior US official (WSJ, 17 Jul) describing the aim as disrupting supply routes to the city — routes ISW describes as Iran's own interior-to-coast logistics. ISW separately assesses the PRC and Russia are likely supporting Iranian targeting, citing a PRC reconnaissance satellite, Russian geolocation of US warships and aircraft, and Russian drone tactics. Both component facts are corroborated; joining them is his. What ISW documents is targeting intelligence, and no source on the desk reports Chinese or Russian materiel entering Iran. Held as a hypothesis.
Interceptor stocks. Every month of war consumes US holdings of PAC-3, THAAD and SM-3. Corroborated CSIS (Cancian and Park, 30 May 2026): PAC-3 MSE runs at roughly 650 a year with half going to allies, against a US decade-average buy of 225; the 3,203 Patriots requested for FY2027 first deliver May 2029. THAAD runs at 96 a year against an 857-interceptor request first delivering mid-2029. Both sit in the slowest three-plus-year replacement tier. Corroborated for two of his three named systems, from a defence-budget source rather than a strategic commentator. SM-3 is excluded: CSIS records less use than ground-launched systems and attributes the lag to small past orders and 36–39 month lead times. The monthly-burn framing is not how CSIS measures it, and the piece predates three further escalation rounds.
Who gains. Depletion benefits Moscow, leaving Ukraine exposed as PAC-3 stocks fall, and benefits Beijing more still by raising first-strike confidence before US stocks recover. Interpretive CSIS documents the allocation friction — THAAD deliveries re-sequenced away from allies, Japan's Tomahawks possibly delayed, Ukraine Patriots pushed back in the queue — and states the depleted inventories created a window of vulnerability in the Western Pacific. It contests the conclusion: demonstrated US combat experience against a PLA with none may preserve deterrence until inventories are restored. It says nothing about Russia benefiting. The queue and the Western Pacific window are corroborated in the source's own words, stronger support than he offers himself. The further step — that Moscow and Beijing are prolonging the conflict for this reason — has no support on the desk.
Mobile launchers. The hard target, rather than the underground missile cities; the fix is compressing the kill chain with persistent armed ISR striking within minutes. Advocacy Self-labelled house conjecture. The only adjacent datum is one unelaborated Reuters line (three US officials, 16 Jul) describing recent strikes as “shaping operations.” Every missile-related strike ISW reports in the window is a fixed site — the Lar launch site, the Yazd Missile Base, Konarak airbase. No in-window evidence of the shift in either direction. One detail runs the other way: the 116 downed telecom towers and the strike on the Radio and Communications Regulatory Authority degrade Iran's control of its own Shahed drones, which is fixed-infrastructure suppression.
Order of battle. 135 MQ-9s, 18 RQ-4s, roughly 50 MQ-4C Tritons and an operational RQ-180 for that mission, with the binding constraint numerical — “not dozens but hundreds” of platforms. Interpretive No independent check on the desk. Every figure is asserted without source, and no coverage modelling sits behind the hundreds. Not carried, consistent with the numeric record in his work — 300kg and 460kg for the same HEU stock inside one article, “Fatyie Ii” for the Fattah-2, a moving SPR figure. The RQ-180 entry is the exception and is credited: he hedges it as classified and unconfirmed. The mass-over-sophistication constraint is sound in kind; the figure is illustrative.
The Ukraine model. The 250km strike-drone campaign that largely isolated Crimea by land transfers to Hormuz — drones from the Omani Peninsula interdicting Iran's coastal batteries. Advocacy The Crimea-isolation premise is stated flatly and unsourced. Nothing on the desk tests the 250km radius or the Omani basing proposition. Prescription. ISW's own Hormuz mechanic cuts against the payoff — a single successful attack, or the persistent threat of one, deters transits — so suppressing launchers need not restore traffic. Logged, not carried.
Bab el-Mandeb. Yemeni sources report the Houthis preparing to close it with al-Shabaab, holding closure in reserve as their own asset; the strait carries 10–12% of seaborne trade, and simultaneous disruption with Hormuz would exceed any modern maritime crisis. Interpretive A closure vector is independently reported (Reuters, 16 Jul), reaching this desk inside the Capital Economics note rather than read directly, and on a different causal account: Iran instructing the Houthis conditional on US strikes against Iranian power plants. ISW's 17 Jul edition carries no Yemen, Houthi, Bab el-Mandeb or al-Shabaab content at all. Bremmer and Barclays both attribute Houthi behaviour to a payments dispute with Riyadh. The trade share is asserted without source; Capital Economics instead quantifies the consequence at roughly 4mn bpd rerouted via Yanbu and +$15–20/bbl on a dual closure. The vector is live and the Sanaa-airport leg is already on this file. His account of agency — a Houthi-owned asset held in reserve, largely independent of Tehran — is not the account our sources give. The al-Shabaab coordination and drone-transfer legs are uncorroborated rather than contradicted, resting on unnamed sourcing alone. The consequence arithmetic is usable; the trade share is not carried. v13: the blockade landed 20 Jul on the Houthis' own Sana'a-siege grievance — a datum for his agency read — but Reuters and ISW report a 13 Jul flight carrying IRGC commanders, missile equipment and gold into Yemen, and ISW's read (“their own agenda… a key Iranian partner”) lands between his account and the instruction account; the scope, Saudi-flagged vessels and Saudi port calls, is narrower than the full closure he sketches. v14: Houthi negotiator Abdul Salam states the blockade applies to Saudi Arabia alone (24 Jul), and ISW records the Houthis resisting Iran's request to close the strait fully for several months — both closer to his reserve-asset read — while NYT-cited officials say Iran would seek full closure if Trump strikes Tehran or critical infrastructure.
Oil above $150. A surge in the weeks ahead as risk premia rise, with significant pressure on government bond markets. Advocacy $150 sits inside the $130–160+ escalation band this file already holds. Brent settled around $88 on 17 Jul. Barclays' $65–80 range is the genuine out-of-band divergence. The bond call names no market, tenor, level or horizon. The level is inside our own range. He carries $150 as a base expectation within weeks; this file holds it as a tail. No supply-outage volume or trigger level is given and the horizon has not elapsed, so the call is logged unscored. His section heading asserting oil is set to rocket higher overstates his own hedged text. v14: the fortnight since has run a round trip — $100.69 settled 23 Jul, then the worst three-day fall since April 2020 to $84.09 on 28 Jul. No $150 print; the call tracks against, with his “weeks ahead” horizon not fully elapsed. Still unscored.

Track record — prior calls against the 20 May to 20 Jul record

The capability, structure and chokepoint calls have held, and three of them are now working assumptions in this file. Timing and agency are where he fails, consistently and across every piece. The rows below are not independent trials — the Israel-trigger thesis recurs in four pieces and is entered once, and several draw on the same escalation sequence — so this reads as a map of where he is reliable rather than a hit rate. Unfalsifiable framing and advocacy carrying no forecast content are excluded, as is a 2024 Lebanon ground-campaign piece re-stamped 29 May 2026 and circulated as current with its body text never revised.

13 May		 Control of Hormuz is a non-negotiable Iranian interest functioning as a substitute deterrent of nuclear-equivalent value. Borne out The 17 Jun MoU reopened the strait toll-free under Iranian-Omani management. Iran rejected Oman's two-corridor compromise on 11–12 Jul despite its own preferred mediator brokering it, declared the strait an inviolable red line on 16 Jul, and struck vessels on 25 Jun, 6–8 Jul and 13 Jul to enforce the position. Called when much commentary still treated the strait as a bargaining chip.
Trump 33
On the signed MoU, 17 Jun		 The MoU is a pause that will break down, and the Clause 5 wording concedes effective strait control to Iran. Borne out The first armed exchange came within eight days, five rounds by 19 Jul, and Tehran suspended the MoU on 18 Jul. ISW's 18 Jul report attributes the collapse to divergent readings of Clause 5 — the mechanism he named on signing day, read off the published clauses while most commentary worked from Trump's summary. The one place he is right for the reason he gave.
WW3 114
Chinese HGV, 5 Jun		 Multi-vector saturation defeats layered missile defence, including allied architectures considerably less capable than Israel's. Borne out Repeated leakers against Gulf and US defences 8–19 Jul, Kuwaiti power, desalination and oil facilities hit three days running, a first strike on Saudi territory, three US deaths on 17–19 Jul. Scope limit: the Israeli system supplying the piece's own evidence was never engaged in the window, so the core case went untested.
WW3 114
Key Predictions 3–5, 5 Jun		 Munitions and interceptor depletion becomes the binding constraint and forces the West into a defence-industrial rebuild. Partly CSIS (30 May) documents the shift: large munitions procurement inside the FY2027 budget, a war supplemental expected to rebuild above pre-war levels, Lockheed scaling PAC-3 toward 2,000 a year and THAAD toward 400. The FY2027 cycle predates his 5 Jun piece, so the credit is for reading the direction. CSIS also holds that demonstrated US combat experience may preserve Pacific deterrence in the interim.
WW3 112 · IWI 13 · WW3 116
13 May – 17 Jul		 Nuclear breakout is imminent — “on the cusp,” months away, “looms large.” Not borne out No breakout as of 20 Jul, through maximal provocation: Khamenei killed, US strikes ~200km inland, the Darkhovin plant struck per Iran's AEOI, the MoU suspended. The measuring window is this desk's construction rather than his. His numbered prediction text hedges while titles and headings assert, so the graded claim is the heading; the months-away version is now 22 months old.
Standing thesis
Across four pieces		 Israel is the uncontainable spoiler, and the IDF is the actor that moves on Iran's nuclear file. Not borne out Entered once though it recurs in four pieces. No Israeli unilateral strike on Iran occurred in the window. Hezbollah is absent from the escalation ledger; retaliation ran as direct Iranian state missile and drone fire against Gulf states and US bases; the nuclear-adjacent strike at Darkhovin was American; the Lebanon track moved toward a Rome-brokered pilot-zones structure on 14–15 Jul.
Trump 33 · Article 3 · IWI 13
22 May – 17 Jun		 Breakout inevitable inside the 60-day track; China freed to move on Taiwan; the Pacific as the primary theatre; the clerical leadership and the IRGC destroyed. Too early The 60-day track runs to roughly mid-August and has not elapsed. No Pacific escalation event has reached the desk in eight weeks, while the confrontation consuming US strike assets, munitions and diplomatic bandwidth was Hormuz. Khamenei was killed and Mojtaba succeeded him, but ISW assesses the strait attacks as chain-of-command decisions by senior IRGC officers, so the institution is functioning.

WW3 116 moves nothing in the scenario architecture, the Brent bands or the verdict. The two claims in it that carry weight are corroborated from outside the piece: CSIS puts PAC-3 and THAAD replenishment on a three-plus-year queue with FY2027 deliveries starting in 2029, supplying the numbers behind an interceptor mechanism he asserts without any, and ISW's Clause 5 finding vindicates the MoU read he made on signing day. The four claims nothing here tests are the assassination plot and the aircraft switch offered as its corroboration, the al-Shabaab coordination and drone transfer, the persistent-armed-ISR doctrine shift, and the Bear Island and Japan-Okinawa passages. The Bab el-Mandeb vector is the one genuinely new watch item, and the account of agency behind it is not his: Reuters has Iran instructing the Houthis conditional on US strikes against Iranian power plants, while Bremmer and Barclays both attribute Houthi behaviour to a payments dispute with Riyadh. The capability, structure and chokepoint reads are the durable part of his work, and three of them are now working assumptions in this file. His quantities do not travel into our text without independent confirmation. His timelines and his assignment of agency to Israel have failed repeatedly across the file.

Essential Lessons Part I — the retrospective, graded

“Essential Lessons from the Iran Conflict — Part I: Strategic Realities” (Murrinations, 21 Jul; reached the desk 26 Jul via Gary) is Murrin grading the war he has covered since WW3 112. It is doctrine — seven lessons and five predictions, one of them dated only as “inevitable.” The checkable lessons land close to sources this file already holds. It moves nothing in the architecture, the bands or the verdict. A Part II on operational lessons is flagged and will be read when it arrives.

ClaimTierChecked againstOur read
Air power cannot win alone. The campaign failed to force submission; Iran's hardened, dispersed depth defeated it. Corroborated ISW's own key assessment (26 Jul): the current level and type of US strikes have “likely failed to meaningfully affect” Iran's pursuit of strait control — ~1,000 targets since 12 Jul against ~13,000 in the war's first phase, with Iranian control consolidating via the mine threat. Corroborated by the primary source this file weights most, in the same week he published. The Vietnam and Kosovo parallels and the JCS air-power-bias counterfactual are his framing; the operative claim stands on ISW alone.
Time as a weapon. Iran survives the assault, uses pauses to repair and replenish, and emerges stronger. Corroborated ISW (27 Jul) assesses Iran is using the current pause to reconstitute, quoting a regime-linked analyst on rebuilding combat capacity, with the 8 Apr ceasefire as precedent. Corroborated in the current window — the pause this version writes into the file is the mechanism he describes. It cuts both ways for the diplomatic track: the pause that enables the table also lets Iran reconstitute (ISW, 27 Jul).
“Could hardly have been worse.” A mortal blow to US credibility; allies will question the security umbrella. Interpretive Pape (Foreign Affairs, same intake) argues the same direction: the 1991-dated unipolar era is over, with the Iran war as the demonstration. Mead (WSJ, 28 Jul) argues a tactical pause inside a longer campaign by a president “unlikely to give up.” Desk observables cut both ways — Japan's Tomahawk delays and Kuwait's reported Pakistan pact exploration against a blockade still enforced. A live dispute between tracked sources. The file's “exhausted America” leg already carries the credibility question as backdrop; the piece sharpens it without settling it. The “mortal blow” grading is his register.
Chokepoints are the US blind spot. The Houthi A2/AD episode is a test bed for Iranian and Chinese strategy. Interpretive ISW's standing assessment has the Red Sea expansion serving Iran's Hormuz objective; Pape extends the same logic to Taiwan (drone-armed denial of Chinese ports). Nothing on the desk addresses US Navy institutional posture. The chokepoint mechanism is a working assumption of this file. The institutional blind-spot claim and the sea-power-chairman counterfactual are untestable here; the Taiwan read-through is logged as backdrop alongside Pape's.
The next war. A future US-Iran confrontation is “inevitable” — economic pressure, cyber, proxies, drones — on Iran's timetable, from strength. Advocacy No horizon and no falsifiable content. ISW's reconstitution read is consistent in kind; nothing tests inevitability. His conviction, kept in quotation. The file's architecture already carries the substance — Stalemate modal, the apex re-timed and open-ended — without the inevitability claim.
02 · Scenario test

Live tail risk on a 6–12 month horizon — below the scenario-modelling threshold.

The conclusion is unchanged; the operational decision is to bear this tail in the long-term allocation rather than model it.

The apex first-use event is paradigm-break, so protection is structural rather than model-derived. As David noted, “in short, I probably don’t need to know what the damage would look like.” The deal does not lower the tail — it re-times it. The MOU defers the nuclear question to a 60-day negotiation while the HEU stays inside Iran, so the breakout clock moves from the battlefield to the talks table without resetting. The 25–28 Jun armed exchange was the test case for kinetic escalation short of first-use; the 6–8 Jul exchange a second instance; the 8–12 Jul continuation a third; the 13–16 Jul round a fourth — all conventional, with the apex below threshold throughout. The 17–19 Jul window is a fifth iteration, and it moves two markers the prior four did not: US service members were killed, and a strike reached a nuclear-file site — the Darkhovin plant in Khuzestan, per Iran's AEOI. Both stay inside the conventional channel: Darkhovin is a very-early-construction site with no nuclear material found at the IAEA's last inspection, and the ~440kg HEU core goes unmentioned in any of this window's exchanges. A second adjacency followed on 22 Jul: US strikes hit the Parchin complex in southeastern Tehran Province — the munitions site whose Taleghan 2 facility carries the pre-2003 weapons-test history — with no claim against the HEU itself. Washington chatter about striking Pickaxe Mountain, extending to unattributed tactical-nuclear talk (13D), is logged as apex texture and not carried. The sixth round (20–23 Jul) stayed conventional through the second-chokepoint window; the seventh (24–28 Jul) is a pause — the US flew no strike waves after 24 Jul, Iranian attacks ended with a single Bahrain strike, and the HEU went unmentioned. Two proliferation-adjacent items log without carrying: the NYT reports US intelligence assesses Mojtaba Khamenei more interested in a weapon than his father, favouring a miniaturised thermonuclear design, while Albright (24 Jul) assesses the programme too degraded for that pursuit now. ISW assesses Iran is using the pause to reconstitute, as after the 8 Apr ceasefire — the apex clock runs through the pause. The re-timing anchor itself has moved: with Tehran's 18 Jul suspension the 60-day track is no longer running, so the apex window is open-ended rather than deadline-bound. The placement is insensitive to that timing — the tail sits in the long-term allocation either way, which is what the structural-protection decision was for. What stays in the long-term allocation is the nightmare convergence — nuclear Iran + reinvigorated Russia + hedging China + exhausted America + isolated Israel arriving together around summer 2026 as oil buffers run dry; Murrin Article 3’s late-stage US-decline thesis sits on the “exhausted America” leg as backdrop conviction, with the US Strategic Petroleum Reserve at 316.5m barrels as of 10 Jul, the lowest since April 1983, with ~34m contracted barrels left and total US crude stocks at their lowest since 1984 — monitored daily at the tip-of-iceberg layer.

Verdict

Murrin Article 2 cannot be ruled out and remains a live tail risk on a 6–12 month horizon. The apex probability sits below the threshold where another modelled scenario would change the prescription. Israeli unilateral action remains the most plausible trigger — and the armed US-Iran exchanges under the MoU show the deal can turn kinetic through Hormuz directly; the strait and the Lebanon front are both live channels for an escalation that the long-term allocation is built to absorb. A fifth round — first US deaths, a formal Iranian suspension, retaliation across seven countries — extends the pattern at higher stakes without displacing Israeli unilateral action as the most plausible apex trigger. Israel is closer to the exchange than in any prior window: intercepting Iranian drones near its borders, detecting the Aqaba launch, and warning it will re-engage if attacked. The trigger has still not fired. A sixth round (20–23 Jul) adds the second chokepoint and the Parchin strike without displacing that read; WSJ (22 Jul) reports Israel deliberately sitting the campaign out while the IDF prepares options. A seventh window (24–28 Jul) is the first pause since the suspension and does not displace that read; Trump met Netanyahu in Washington (Bloomberg, 28 Jul) with Israel still out of the campaign.

03 · The scenario set

Stalemate / Resolution / BAU — stance, drivers, and market views.

Per the Eric/team binding decision (22 May): two scenarios modelled, plus the BAU baseline. The apex first-use scenario is held in the long-term allocation. Architecture held through v13 with the tier Suspended pending a convened table. v14: the table convened — the tier moves to Hormuz track convened.
