Bottom line · the pause breaks; Iran rejects the Omani design

The scenario set stays bifurcated: Stalemate (modal) and Resolution (proposal rejected), with the apex tail held in the long-term allocation below the scenario-modelling threshold. The four-day pause ended over the strait question: IRGC ballistic missiles targeted Muwaffaq Salti Airbase in Jordan on 28 Jul — an “attempted surprise attack” per CENTCOM, five intercepted — shortly after Iran rejected compromise on strait control, and on 29 Jul a senior Iranian official told Reuters that Oman's regional-management and voluntary-fee proposal was “unrealistic,” Gharibabadi having said a day earlier that Iran would go to war for strait sovereignty (ISW). The IRGC Navy claims three tanker strikes on the southern route. Joint US-Saudi strikes on militia sites across Iraq — retaliation for militia attacks on Saudi oil infrastructure and US interests (ISW) — reportedly killed at least four Quds Force officers, the first Saudi offensive strikes beyond Yemen we have on record; Trump promises to hit Iran hard, and through ISW's cutoff no US military response to the Jordan attack itself had followed. The Fed held with no forward guidance and the 30Y went through its 2007 high; the 29 Jul session sold off on the break and the AI leg turned the 30 Jul session (Microsoft's record one-day add), with Brent at ~$88 settled 30 Jul — below the floor, the physical market still dire (CE, 30 Jul) and Hormuz under 10 ships/day. A claimed ~50-nation Saudi coalition to force Bab el-Mandeb open is carried as a single-source watch item. Murrin’s Article 2 nuclear scenario remains the analytical foundation under structural tail protection. Full scenario stances, drivers, equity and Brent views are in section 03.

01 · Due Diligence on Murrin

Verdict unchanged — cannot rule it out. WW3 117's call for total war is his own advocacy; his checkable claims hold.

Capabilities corroborated (Tier 1); interpretive layers held as hypotheses (Tier 2); the policy conclusion treated as advocacy (Tier 3). The full claim-by-claim verifiability table sits in the v1 tab.

Murrin’s capabilities case is sound and OSINT-sourced. Timeline sits at the aggressive end of a 6–12 month band that overlaps Roaring Lion’s own assessment. The preemptive-nuclear-use conclusion is his advocacy. The Gallant “440 Kilograms” piece restates the ~440kg 60% HEU stockpile that anchors the capabilities case, and ISW, citing five US-intelligence sources via CNN, reports Iran is physically hardening that stockpile — collapsing tunnels and mining storage entrances to defeat any seizure — so the fissile core is intact, deferred, and being entrenched. Iran agreed to readmit IAEA inspectors, the first forward step on the nuclear file; it is not down-blending. That step has stalled: the inspection access is unresolved, with Grossi expecting entry while Iran ties it to a final deal and sanctions relief, and Iran has signalled it could suspend the talks over the armed exchange. The ~440kg core stays inside Iran behind the entrenched tunnels. “Cannot rule out the nuclear scenario” holds as the analytical foundation; the apex is handled via the long-term allocation rather than modelled scenarios.

WW3 117 — the total-war call, graded

“WW3 117: Trump's Watershed Moment in Recognising a Common Autocratic Enemy” (28 Jul; reached the desk an hour after the v14 send) is Murrin's escalation call. The frame is a single integrated struggle — Ukraine, the Middle East and a coming Indo-Pacific theatre against one autocratic axis — hung on Senator Graham's funeral as the catalyst, and it concludes that Trump “has only one choice” — total war with Iran alongside Israel. It moves nothing in the architecture, the bands or the verdict.

ClaimTierChecked againstOur read
One integrated war. The regional wars are a single struggle against a shared autocratic axis; the fires merge. Interpretive BCA's Gertken (29 Jul, same intake) models the opposite as his base case: the wars stay separate on US political-will limits, Russia's two-front aversion and China's external constraints — with a Hormuz oil shock the tail that merges them. Pape frames the same linkages as read-through. The couplings themselves are documented on this desk: shared munitions strain, chokepoint tactics, Russian satellite targeting support. A live dispute between tracked sources. The file carries the wars as coupled through observable channels without adopting the single-war frame; the Graham-funeral catalyst is narrative. Gertken's own tail condition — an oil shock that kills demand — is the same transmission this file holds in the escalation legs.
“Iran's control of the strait remains unchallenged” after ~1,000 US strikes. Corroborated ISW's 26 Jul key assessment: the strikes have “likely failed to meaningfully affect” Iran's pursuit of strait control. Barclays (28 Jul): degradation achieved, “nothing that will meaningfully impact the IRGC's strategic calculus”; transits 3–6/day. Corroborated by both primary trackers — the third consecutive piece whose checkable core lands on ISW's own assessment. His ~1,000-strike figure matches ISW's count for the phase.
Axis logistics. The Caspian vessel strike, Russian satellite targeting for Iran, and Geran drones refitted with Chinese jet engines flowing back to Iran. Interpretive Ukraine's Caspian strikes on sanctioned vessels and Zelensky's satellite-imagery statements are already on this file (ISW, 25–26 Jul). The Chinese-engine transfer leg is his “believed to be,” unsourced; ISW separately reports Iran seeking PRC air-defence assistance. The component facts are carried from primary sources; the engine-transfer leg is uncorroborated; assembling them into one axis supply system is his frame. Held as a hypothesis, consistent with the WW3 116 treatment of the China-Russia claim.
Trump's “only one choice” — total war with Iran alongside Israel. Advocacy A prescription. The observable record at the cutoff is narrower: no US military response to the Jordan attack had followed, the day's joint US-Saudi strikes in Iraq retaliated for militia attacks on Saudi oil infrastructure and US interests, and Trump's promise to hit Iran hard is unexecuted against Iran itself; Israel remains out of the campaign. His conviction, kept in quotation — the same total-war advocacy graded at WW3 116 and Essential Lessons. The durable adjacent question is his interceptor-depletion cross-reference, already CSIS-corroborated in this file.

WW3 116, “The Battle for the Strait is On, so Prepare for Escalation” (17 Jul), is the tracked author's read of the current exchange. Murrin declares “Chapter Two” of the war — an existential contest for the strait and the Red Sea sea lanes that in his framing ends only with a victor and a vanquished. It is commentary rather than reporting: twenty-four distinct claims with no primary text of his own behind them, and the two that carry real weight are corroborated from outside the piece. Four of his headline claims have nothing on this desk that tests them either way.

ClaimTierChecked againstOur read
The MoU. An appeasement dwarfing Chamberlain's, and in full form it would have destroyed US maritime hegemony at a stroke. Interpretive The signed 14-point text is on file. ISW (18 Jul) attributes the collapse to divergent readings of Clause 5 — Iran reading it as affirming Iranian authority over the strait, Washington expecting pre-war traffic conditions. Corroborated at the level of mechanism: Clause 5 is where the deal failed, the leg he identified on signing day from the published clauses. The Chamberlain scaling and the hegemony conclusion are his register and carry no checkable content.
Breakout. The IRGC used the pause to repair and replenish while moving closer to a weapon day by day. Advocacy The IAEA puts the stockpile at 441kg of 60% HEU as of June 2025; ISW, citing US intelligence via CNN, reports it being physically hardened in tunnels. Nothing on the desk tests imminence. Retention and hardening are corroborated and stand independently of him. The imminence claim is his third dated one and carries no enrichment figure, no breakout estimate and no date, so it cannot be tested on its own terms. Timing stays at the aggressive end of the 6–12 month band.
Total war. An existential contest for the shipping lanes, resolved only with a victor and a vanquished; America should commit to defeating the IRGC with overwhelming firepower. Advocacy A prescription. No source is offered and nothing on the desk bears on it. Reported as his conviction and kept in quotation. The escalation direction is consistent with the 6–19 Jul record. The grand-strategy prescription carries no falsifiable content.
Trump's resolve. Hardened: the midterm gas-price motive is dead on poor ratings, and he reads Iran as having acted in bad faith. Interpretive The “scum” remark is on the record from the Ankara NATO summit and already carried here. Tehran suspended the MoU on 18 Jul. No polling has reached the desk. The hardening direction is consistent with the observable sequence. The popularity leg is asserted without a pollster and is not carried. His own oil call runs against it — a print above $150 would restore the gas-price motive he argues is dead.
Assassination plot. A reported IRGC plot against Trump, passed via Israeli intelligence, corroborated by the Secret Service switching him back to the older Air Force One, and possibly the decisive factor driving him to destroy the IRGC. Interpretive A single unnamed Israeli-intelligence channel, hedged by Murrin himself. The aircraft switch is asserted without source and is unverified here, including the donor state of the aircraft he names. Uncorroborated on this desk. That the switch evidences the plot is his inference rather than anyone's report. The escalation-driver conclusion rests on that unverified chain plus a psychological read of Trump.
China and Russia. Iran fights on with their approval, confirmed by supplies flowing into its replenishment; US strikes on supply-route bridges acknowledge it. Interpretive ISW (17–18 Jul) confirms strikes on bridges, roads, a tunnel and telecoms around Bandar Abbas, a senior US official (WSJ, 17 Jul) describing the aim as disrupting supply routes to the city — routes ISW describes as Iran's own interior-to-coast logistics. ISW separately assesses the PRC and Russia are likely supporting Iranian targeting, citing a PRC reconnaissance satellite, Russian geolocation of US warships and aircraft, and Russian drone tactics. Both component facts are corroborated; joining them is his. What ISW documents is targeting intelligence, and no source on the desk reports Chinese or Russian materiel entering Iran. Held as a hypothesis.
Interceptor stocks. Every month of war consumes US holdings of PAC-3, THAAD and SM-3. Corroborated CSIS (Cancian and Park, 30 May 2026): PAC-3 MSE runs at roughly 650 a year with half going to allies, against a US decade-average buy of 225; the 3,203 Patriots requested for FY2027 first deliver May 2029. THAAD runs at 96 a year against an 857-interceptor request first delivering mid-2029. Both sit in the slowest three-plus-year replacement tier. Corroborated for two of his three named systems, from a defence-budget source rather than a strategic commentator. SM-3 is excluded: CSIS records less use than ground-launched systems and attributes the lag to small past orders and 36–39 month lead times. The monthly-burn framing is not how CSIS measures it, and the piece predates three further escalation rounds.
Who gains. Depletion benefits Moscow, leaving Ukraine exposed as PAC-3 stocks fall, and benefits Beijing more still by raising first-strike confidence before US stocks recover. Interpretive CSIS documents the allocation friction — THAAD deliveries re-sequenced away from allies, Japan's Tomahawks possibly delayed, Ukraine Patriots pushed back in the queue — and states the depleted inventories created a window of vulnerability in the Western Pacific. It contests the conclusion: demonstrated US combat experience against a PLA with none may preserve deterrence until inventories are restored. It says nothing about Russia benefiting. The queue and the Western Pacific window are corroborated in the source's own words, stronger support than he offers himself. The further step — that Moscow and Beijing are prolonging the conflict for this reason — has no support on the desk.
Mobile launchers. The hard target, rather than the underground missile cities; the fix is compressing the kill chain with persistent armed ISR striking within minutes. Advocacy Self-labelled house conjecture. The only adjacent datum is one unelaborated Reuters line (three US officials, 16 Jul) describing recent strikes as “shaping operations.” Every missile-related strike ISW reports in the window is a fixed site — the Lar launch site, the Yazd Missile Base, Konarak airbase. No in-window evidence of the shift in either direction. One detail runs the other way: the 116 downed telecom towers and the strike on the Radio and Communications Regulatory Authority degrade Iran's control of its own Shahed drones, which is fixed-infrastructure suppression.
Order of battle. 135 MQ-9s, 18 RQ-4s, roughly 50 MQ-4C Tritons and an operational RQ-180 for that mission, with the binding constraint numerical — “not dozens but hundreds” of platforms. Interpretive No independent check on the desk. Every figure is asserted without source, and no coverage modelling sits behind the hundreds. Not carried, consistent with the numeric record in his work — 300kg and 460kg for the same HEU stock inside one article, “Fatyie Ii” for the Fattah-2, a moving SPR figure. The RQ-180 entry is the exception and is credited: he hedges it as classified and unconfirmed. The mass-over-sophistication constraint is sound in kind; the figure is illustrative.
The Ukraine model. The 250km strike-drone campaign that largely isolated Crimea by land transfers to Hormuz — drones from the Omani Peninsula interdicting Iran's coastal batteries. Advocacy The Crimea-isolation premise is stated flatly and unsourced. Nothing on the desk tests the 250km radius or the Omani basing proposition. Prescription. ISW's own Hormuz mechanic cuts against the payoff — a single successful attack, or the persistent threat of one, deters transits — so suppressing launchers need not restore traffic. Logged, not carried.
Bab el-Mandeb. Yemeni sources report the Houthis preparing to close it with al-Shabaab, holding closure in reserve as their own asset; the strait carries 10–12% of seaborne trade, and simultaneous disruption with Hormuz would exceed any modern maritime crisis. Interpretive A closure vector is independently reported (Reuters, 16 Jul), reaching this desk inside the Capital Economics note rather than read directly, and on a different causal account: Iran instructing the Houthis conditional on US strikes against Iranian power plants. ISW's 17 Jul edition carries no Yemen, Houthi, Bab el-Mandeb or al-Shabaab content at all. Bremmer and Barclays both attribute Houthi behaviour to a payments dispute with Riyadh. The trade share is asserted without source; Capital Economics instead quantifies the consequence at roughly 4mn bpd rerouted via Yanbu and +$15–20/bbl on a dual closure. The vector is live and the Sanaa-airport leg is already on this file. His account of agency — a Houthi-owned asset held in reserve, largely independent of Tehran — is not the account our sources give. The al-Shabaab coordination and drone-transfer legs are uncorroborated rather than contradicted, resting on unnamed sourcing alone. The consequence arithmetic is usable; the trade share is not carried. v13: the blockade landed 20 Jul on the Houthis' own Sana'a-siege grievance — a datum for his agency read — but Reuters and ISW report a 13 Jul flight carrying IRGC commanders, missile equipment and gold into Yemen, and ISW's read (“their own agenda… a key Iranian partner”) lands between his account and the instruction account; the scope, Saudi-flagged vessels and Saudi port calls, is narrower than the full closure he sketches. v14: Houthi negotiator Abdul Salam states the blockade applies to Saudi Arabia alone (24 Jul), and ISW records the Houthis resisting Iran's request to close the strait fully for several months — both closer to his reserve-asset read — while NYT-cited officials say Iran would seek full closure if Trump strikes Tehran or critical infrastructure.
Oil above $150. A surge in the weeks ahead as risk premia rise, with significant pressure on government bond markets. Advocacy $150 sits inside the $130–160+ escalation band this file already holds. Brent settled around $88 on 17 Jul. Barclays' $65–80 range is the genuine out-of-band divergence. The bond call names no market, tenor, level or horizon. The level is inside our own range. He carries $150 as a base expectation within weeks; this file holds it as a tail. No supply-outage volume or trigger level is given and the horizon has not elapsed, so the call is logged unscored. His section heading asserting oil is set to rocket higher overstates his own hedged text. v14: the fortnight since has run a round trip — $100.69 settled 23 Jul, then the worst three-day fall since April 2020 to $84.09 on 28 Jul. No $150 print; the call tracks against, with his “weeks ahead” horizon not fully elapsed. Still unscored. v15: two weeks on, the peak is $100.69 on the AP settle and ~$94 on the desk series (23 Jul; divergence flagged at section 06), with Brent at ~$88 — the call tracks against on either series.

Track record — prior calls against the 20 May to 20 Jul record

The capability, structure and chokepoint calls have held, and three of them are now working assumptions in this file. Timing and agency are where he fails, consistently and across every piece. The rows below are not independent trials — the Israel-trigger thesis recurs in four pieces and is entered once, and several draw on the same escalation sequence — so this reads as a map of where he is reliable rather than a hit rate. Unfalsifiable framing and advocacy carrying no forecast content are excluded, as is a 2024 Lebanon ground-campaign piece re-stamped 29 May 2026 and circulated as current with its body text never revised.

The Bab el-Mandeb vector is the one genuinely new watch item, and the account of agency behind it is not his: Reuters has Iran instructing the Houthis conditional on US strikes against Iranian power plants, while Bremmer and Barclays both attribute Houthi behaviour to a payments dispute with Riyadh. The capability, structure and chokepoint reads are the durable part of his work, and three of them are now working assumptions in this file. His quantities do not travel into our text without independent confirmation. His timelines and his assignment of agency to Israel have failed repeatedly across the file.

Essential Lessons Part I — the retrospective, graded

“Essential Lessons from the Iran Conflict — Part I: Strategic Realities” (Murrinations, 21 Jul; reached the desk 26 Jul via Gary) is Murrin grading the war he has covered since WW3 112. It is doctrine — seven lessons and five predictions, one of them dated only as “inevitable.” The checkable lessons land close to sources this file already holds. It moves nothing in the architecture, the bands or the verdict. A Part II on operational lessons is flagged and will be read when it arrives.

ClaimTierChecked againstOur read
Air power cannot win alone. The campaign failed to force submission; Iran's hardened, dispersed depth defeated it. Corroborated ISW's own key assessment (26 Jul): the current level and type of US strikes have “likely failed to meaningfully affect” Iran's pursuit of strait control — ~1,000 targets since 12 Jul against ~13,000 in the war's first phase, with Iranian control consolidating via the mine threat. Corroborated by the primary source this file weights most, in the same week he published. The Vietnam and Kosovo parallels and the JCS air-power-bias counterfactual are his framing; the operative claim stands on ISW alone.
Time as a weapon. Iran survives the assault, uses pauses to repair and replenish, and emerges stronger. Corroborated ISW (27 Jul) assesses Iran is using the current pause to reconstitute, quoting a regime-linked analyst on rebuilding combat capacity, with the 8 Apr ceasefire as precedent. Corroborated in the v14 window — the pause v14 wrote into the file is the mechanism he describes. It cuts both ways for the diplomatic track: the pause that enables the table also lets Iran reconstitute (ISW, 27 Jul).
“Could hardly have been worse.” A mortal blow to US credibility; allies will question the security umbrella. Interpretive Pape (Foreign Affairs, same intake) argues the same direction: the 1991-dated unipolar era is over, with the Iran war as the demonstration. Mead (WSJ, 28 Jul) argues a tactical pause inside a longer campaign by a president “unlikely to give up.” Desk observables cut both ways — Japan's Tomahawk delays and Kuwait's reported Pakistan pact exploration against a blockade still enforced. A live dispute between tracked sources. The file's “exhausted America” leg already carries the credibility question as backdrop; the piece sharpens it without settling it. The “mortal blow” grading is his register.
Chokepoints are the US blind spot. The Houthi A2/AD episode is a test bed for Iranian and Chinese strategy. Interpretive ISW's standing assessment has the Red Sea expansion serving Iran's Hormuz objective; Pape extends the same logic to Taiwan (drone-armed denial of Chinese ports). Nothing on the desk addresses US Navy institutional posture. The chokepoint mechanism is a working assumption of this file. The institutional blind-spot claim and the sea-power-chairman counterfactual are untestable here; the Taiwan read-through is logged as backdrop alongside Pape's.
The next war. A future US-Iran confrontation is “inevitable” — economic pressure, cyber, proxies, drones — on Iran's timetable, from strength. Advocacy No horizon and no falsifiable content. ISW's reconstitution read is consistent in kind; nothing tests inevitability. His conviction, kept in quotation. The file's architecture already carries the substance — Stalemate modal, the apex re-timed and open-ended — without the inevitability claim.
02 · Scenario test

Live tail risk on a 6–12 month horizon — below the scenario-modelling threshold.

The conclusion is unchanged; the operational decision is to bear this tail in the long-term allocation rather than model it.

The apex first-use event is paradigm-break, so protection is structural rather than model-derived. As David noted, “in short, I probably don’t need to know what the damage would look like.” The deal does not lower the tail — it re-times it. The MOU defers the nuclear question to a 60-day negotiation while the HEU stays inside Iran, so the breakout clock moves from the battlefield to the talks table without resetting. The 25–28 Jun armed exchange was the test case for kinetic escalation short of first-use; the 6–8 Jul exchange a second instance; the 8–12 Jul continuation a third; the 13–16 Jul round a fourth — all conventional, with the apex below threshold throughout. The 17–19 Jul window is a fifth iteration, and it moves two markers the prior four did not: US service members were killed, and a strike reached a nuclear-file site — the Darkhovin plant in Khuzestan, per Iran's AEOI. Both stay inside the conventional channel: Darkhovin is a very-early-construction site with no nuclear material found at the IAEA's last inspection, and the ~440kg HEU core goes unmentioned in any of this window's exchanges. A second adjacency followed on 22 Jul: US strikes hit the Parchin complex in southeastern Tehran Province — the munitions site whose Taleghan 2 facility carries the pre-2003 weapons-test history — with no claim against the HEU itself. Washington chatter about striking Pickaxe Mountain, extending to unattributed tactical-nuclear talk (13D), is logged as apex texture and not carried. The sixth round (20–23 Jul) stayed conventional through the second-chokepoint window; the seventh (24–28 Jul) is a pause — the US flew no strike waves after 24 Jul, Iranian attacks ended with a single Bahrain strike, and the HEU went unmentioned. Two proliferation-adjacent items log without carrying: the NYT reports US intelligence assesses Mojtaba Khamenei more interested in a weapon than his father, favouring a miniaturised thermonuclear design, while Albright (24 Jul) assesses the programme too degraded for that pursuit now. ISW assesses Iran is using the pause to reconstitute, as after the 8 Apr ceasefire — the apex clock runs through the pause. An eighth window (28–31 Jul) ends the pause at Jordan and keeps the exchange conventional — the day's US-Saudi strikes hit militia sites in Iraq (retaliation for attacks on Saudi oil infrastructure and US interests), no US answer to the Jordan attack had landed by the 29 Jul cutoff, and the HEU is again unmentioned. Two nuclear-file signals log without carrying: 13D's “the nuclear issue is no longer viewed as taboo in Tehran” — the full text carries a debate on NPT withdrawal, with the North Korea precedent argued (WILTW, 30 Jul, on file) — and Barclays' framing of a strike on the Isfahan HEU stockpile or Pickaxe Mountain as Washington's “last option.” The re-timing anchor itself has moved: with Tehran's 18 Jul suspension the 60-day track is no longer running, so the apex window is open-ended rather than deadline-bound. The placement is insensitive to that timing — the tail sits in the long-term allocation either way, which is what the structural-protection decision was for. What stays in the long-term allocation is the nightmare convergence — nuclear Iran + reinvigorated Russia + hedging China + exhausted America + isolated Israel arriving together around summer 2026 as oil buffers run dry; Murrin Article 3’s late-stage US-decline thesis sits on the “exhausted America” leg as backdrop conviction, with the US Strategic Petroleum Reserve at 307.6m barrels as of 24 Jul, the lowest since April 1983, with ~25m contracted barrels left and total US crude stocks at their lowest since 1984 — monitored daily at the tip-of-iceberg layer.

Verdict

Murrin Article 2 cannot be ruled out and remains a live tail risk on a 6–12 month horizon. The apex probability sits below the threshold where another modelled scenario would change the prescription. Israeli unilateral action remains the most plausible trigger — and the armed US-Iran exchanges under the MoU show the deal can turn kinetic through Hormuz directly; the strait and the Lebanon front are both live channels for an escalation that the long-term allocation is built to absorb. A fifth round — first US deaths, a formal Iranian suspension, retaliation across seven countries — extends the pattern at higher stakes without displacing Israeli unilateral action as the most plausible apex trigger. Israel is closer to the exchange than in any prior window: intercepting Iranian drones near its borders, detecting the Aqaba launch, and warning it will re-engage if attacked. The trigger has still not fired. A sixth round (20–23 Jul) adds the second chokepoint and the Parchin strike without displacing that read; WSJ (22 Jul) reports Israel deliberately sitting the campaign out while the IDF prepares options. A seventh window (24–28 Jul) is the first pause since the suspension and does not displace that read; Trump met Netanyahu in Washington (Bloomberg, 28 Jul) with Israel still out of the campaign. The eighth window's break does not displace the read: the Jordan attack and the Iraq strikes ran through bases and proxy sites, Israel stayed out, and Barclays flags an Israeli strike on Iranian sites as a live if unlikely risk ahead of the 27 Oct election. The trigger has still not fired.

03 · The scenario set

Stalemate / Resolution / BAU — stance, drivers, and market views.

Per the Eric/team binding decision (22 May): two scenarios modelled, plus the BAU baseline. The apex first-use scenario is held in the long-term allocation. Architecture held through v13 with the tier Suspended pending a convened table; v14 moved it to Hormuz track convened. v15: Iran rejected the proposal (a senior official to Reuters, 29 Jul) — the tier moves to Proposal rejected.

Eric/team · persistent Bad B		 Sustained economic pressure; US–Iran nuclear divide wide; hardliners block Good A; energy tightens Jul–Sep; fiscal pressure lifts yields; S&P EPS holds until demand destruction (~2027). Iran has re-armed Hormuz as leverage and gated the nuclear file behind the relief clauses; it has used force to assert that control repeatedly. The fifth round (17–19 Jul) added the first US troop deaths, a retaliation footprint spanning seven countries, and two-way strikes on civilian critical infrastructure — Kuwaiti power, desalination and oil facilities against an alleged US strike on an Iranian desalination pier near Jask. The IRGC Navy now runs an explicit permission regime in the strait, and the US is interdicting the internal logistics feeding Bandar Abbas rather than coastal assets alone. The sixth round widened the map: the Houthis blockaded Saudi Red Sea ports from 20 Jul and struck two Saudi tankers on 22 Jul (Bab el-Mandeb transits 64 to 30, recovering to 40), the US reached Parchin and western/north-western Iran, and Iran put its first missiles on Dhahran's King Abdulaziz Air Base while degrading US early-warning radar in Kuwait. The seventh window inverted to a mutual pause from 24 Jul, the blockade maintained through it with enforcement turned lethal (the Lavine), the IRGC's mine threat kept deterring non-approved routes, and the Saudi front ran on. ISW's key assessment stands under Stalemate: the strike campaign has likely failed to move Iran off strait control. The eighth round reopened the exchange on Iran's initiative — the Jordan surprise attack, three tankers claimed struck on the southern route, and a widened proxy front (joint US-Saudi strikes on militia sites across Iraq, at least four Quds Force officers reported killed). Iranian regime media claims Iran holds the initiative; ISW's assessment of Iran's war aims is unchanged. Mid-teens downside. Vanda (18 Jul): the US equity right tail has “all but disappeared” — positioning still elevated, systematic flows biased down; SPX ~2% off its highs after the 17 Jul fall. The oil-to-equity beta has compressed to roughly 1.5% S&P downside per 10% oil rise, against ~4% in March. The 23 Jul session: S&P -1.2%, Nasdaq -2.2%, the 10Y at a one-year high — oil pressure compounded by earnings-day falls in Tesla (-14.5%) and Alphabet (-7.1%) (AP). The 23 Jul CTA selling was the largest since 10 Jun; by 27–28 Jul positioning was back to neutral and Treasuries rallied as oil fell (Vanda). The 29 Jul FOMC-day session sold off on the break and the hawkish hold (S&P -1.5% to 7,316, Dow -2.2%); the AI leg turned the 30 Jul session (Microsoft +16%, a record ~$450bn one-day gain), with the Kospi closing a record 18% higher on 31 Jul. The war leg and the AI leg are pulling equities in opposite directions. Tight, elevated, drawing inventory. Settled prints reached the desk this window: Brent closed $82.96 on 16 Jul (desk series) and settled ~$88 on 17 Jul per Bloomberg — up 4.6%, the biggest weekly advance since April — with the front month in backwardation (~-$1.3). Strait traffic: Kpler counted eight transits on 16 Jul. SPR 316.5m barrels at 10 Jul — 307.6m at 24 Jul, ~25m contracted barrels left (lowest since April 1983); total US crude stocks lowest since 1984; the gas complex is the unshielded leg (Asia LNG +94%, TTF +79% versus pre-war). External calls now bracket this file's bands: Barclays $65–80 below the floor (carried watch item), Murrin above $150 (logged at section 01), CE +$15–20 on a dual-chokepoint closure. This window: settled $88.52 on 21 Jul, then $100.69 on 23 Jul (+7%, $102 intraday — AP) as the Red Sea attacks priced; the dual-chokepoint contingency is partially live at Bab el-Mandeb. Then the retreat: roughly 16% over three sessions to $84.09 settled 28 Jul (WTI $79.26), the worst run since April 2020, on de-escalation positioning — flow not normalised, dark transits continuing. The desk series puts the 23 Jul peak at ~$94 (the AP $100.69 divergence is flagged at section 06) and has ~$88 settled 30 Jul; the strait is kinetic again on the IRGC's three-tanker claim, transits under 10/day, and CE (30 Jul) flags a tipping point without a sustained flow recovery.

Eric/team + David · Good A		 Mearsheimer “humble pie”; JPOA 2.0 framework; Ferguson Hormuz Council; HEU export under Security Guarantee; post-war Gulf Treaty Organisation. Stage 1 signed; the first 60-day-track round convened (Switzerland, 21–22 Jun) with IAEA inspectors readmitted; the durable settlement stays gated on that track. The tier moved to Suspended at v12: Tehran formally suspended its MoU commitments on 18 Jul, the diplomatic channel went dead (ISW: “nothing significant to report”), and mediator Oman was itself a target of Iranian strikes and public hostility. ISW's structural read of the collapse: the two sides interpreted Clause 5 on Hormuz in fundamentally different ways — the US as a return to pre-war passage, Iran as affirmation of its authority — and the Gulf states rejected Iranian management and fees. The open question carried forward: whether a successor venue convenes (Qatar, Pakistan and Turkey are floated in intake reporting), and whether the regime split — Rezaei declaring diplomacy over against Ghalibaf and a Nour News op-ed still arguing for talks — resolves toward a reopened channel. v13: the venue question got its first concrete answer — Qatar, Egypt, Pakistan and Oman tabled a written 10-day framework (~20 Jul) with dual-lane reopening and a fee design (Malacca-style Iranian service fees, or an IMO-administered fund), and Iran confirmed receipt. No talks are scheduled; Trump is publicly skeptical while weighing a joint campaign with Israel (Axios). The tier stays Suspended until a table actually convenes. v14: it convened — Iranian and Omani delegations met in Tehran 24–25 Jul on restarting strait traffic, the lead proposal reopening the likely-mined middle passage, with UK/French demining offered once safe, Iran seeking joint management likely to include fees, and Qatar, Pakistan, Egypt and the Witkoff/Kushner channel involved (Bloomberg). A Hormuz agreement would gate resumed war-ending talks; Omani negotiators expected announceable progress within days. The contradictions ride along: Baghaei claims “progress” while saying traffic is unchanged, parliamentarians insist control is not returning to pre-war conditions, and the NYT-reported rejection via the Zaydi channel — denied by his office — is carried as contested. The tier moves to Hormuz track convened; the nuclear file has no table. ISW 28 Jul: the proposal's terms are a formal 10-day truce — Iran reopens the strait; the US lifts the blockade and considers unfreezing assets — with an Omani regional-consortium design for management; Iran is refusing to concede control, and ISW reads the refusal as showing Iran may calculate it can extract the blockade-lift, or further concessions, without giving up the strait. Three factions steer the Iranian side: the IRGC-Vahidi line likely guiding policy, the Pezeshkian/Araghchi/Ghalibaf camp differing over means and defining control less expansively, the Paydari Front against talks entirely. v15: the rejection landed — a senior Iranian official told Reuters (29 Jul) the Omani regional-management and voluntary-fee design is “unrealistic,” Gharibabadi having said (28 Jul, per ISW) Iran would go to war for strait sovereignty; the Jordan attack followed shortly after the refusal, and ISW assesses IRGC commander Vahidi almost certainly supported the strike decision. Both Omani fee designs are now refused — the early-July donations variant and this one — with Iran maintaining its demand to levy fees. The tier moves to Proposal rejected; the Pakistani-Qatari halt-attacks proposal stays under review by both sides (Barclays) as the surviving channel. Mid-single-digit upside. Declines toward $90–110 over months; market below the band until this window — ~$73 at the pre-war anchor by 29 Jun, pushed back up by each escalation round since — now $82.96–~$88 on the first settled prints since v11, below the floor through v12, then into the band: $88.52 settled 21 Jul and $100.69 on 23 Jul, the entry escalation-driven — pricing a clean reopening the substance does not support; trust rebuild takes quarters. v14: three sessions took it back below the floor — $84.09 settled 28 Jul — this time on deal hopes running ahead of the table. v15: ~$88 settled 30 Jul after the rejection — still below the floor, the physical market dire (CE, 30 Jul) and the strait kinetic again.
