04 · The signed MoU

What the text says — the milestone, Article by Article.

The 14-point text Bloomberg published on 16 June and the version a senior US official read aloud on signing (17 Jun); the Article text below is verbatim from those primary publications. Tehran announced on 18 Jul it is suspending its commitments under this text; the table is carried as the reference for whatever track resumes, with the implementation boxes tracking how each obligation has executed and reversed.

ArticleText (close paraphrase)Our reading
1 · Ceasefire Immediate and permanent end to the war on all fronts including Lebanon; no hostile actions; refrain from the threat or use of force; the final agreement confirms this. Signed edit: a Lebanese-sovereignty guarantee was added. Contested — Iran reads it as obliging an Israeli withdrawal; Israel is not a signatory and keeps striking. The one clause advancing: the Lebanese Armed Forces began deploying into the Rome-brokered pilot zones on 16–17 Jul.
2 · Sovereignty Both parties respect each other’s sovereignty and territorial integrity and refrain from interfering in internal affairs. Implicitly constrains US regime-change; the IRGC structure survives. Iran also leans on this to frame “managing” Hormuz as an internal affair.
3 · 60-day window Negotiate and reach a final agreement within a maximum of 60 days, extendable by mutual consent. The clock for every deferred core. Suspended by Tehran on 18 Jul at roughly the window's halfway mark: Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei declared the signature “invalid” and Deputy FM Gharibabadi announced Iran is suspending its commitments. Neither side has formally withdrawn from the text itself.
4 · US obligations Immediate: lift the naval blockade, prevent interference against Iran, restore traffic within 30 days to pre-war capacity proportional to Iran’s pre-war volume. US withdraws forces from surrounding areas within 30 days after the final agreement. Asymmetry: the blockade lifts at once, but force withdrawal waits on the final agreement. Signed term tested and reversed: the US reinstated the blockade on 14 Jul, targeted at Iranian-flagged vessels and Iranian cargo. Enforcement is now kinetic: since 14 Jul the US Navy has redirected three vessels and disabled one, and the 11th MEU boarded the Curacao-flagged M/T Wen Yao off Duqm on 16 Jul. The 30-day full-traffic-restoration deadline passed in breach on 17 Jul.
5 · Iran obligations (Hormuz) Immediate: resume merchant shipping in the Gulf / Sea of Oman within 30 days to pre-war volume, removing technical obstacles and neutralising mines. Signed edit: a joint Iranian-Omani administration and a 60-day toll bar were added. Iran’s win — the text does not bar Iran from “managing” the strait. Tested and unresolved: Oman’s own two-corridor proposal was rejected by Iran on 12 Jul, and shipping avoids the Omani route despite US escorts. The IRGC Navy now states the regime openly — “not a single drop of oil, gas, or chemical fertilizer” passes without its coordination and permission — and a Thai-flagged vessel was struck on the southern route on 17 Jul. Iran's Environmental Protection Agency floated UNCLOS-based “environmental services” fees on 18 Jul, a new variant of the fee ambition.
6 · $300bn development US and regional partners build a rehabilitation and development plan financing at least $300bn; the implementation mechanism is set within 60 days. External Gulf money, not a US outlay (Trump: “no money changes hands”; Reuters: no US government funds). Gulf-funded, routing the financing into the Gulf-state track.
7 · Sanctions US commits to ending all sanctions — UNSC resolutions, IAEA Board resolutions, all unilateral US primary and secondary sanctions — on a schedule set in the final agreement. Exceeds the JCPOA’s scope in principle, but the schedule is deferred to the final agreement. Signed term tested in the other direction: the US Treasury added over 50 sanctions targets on 14–15 Jul (the Shamkhani network, IRGC procurement) — new sanctions rather than the wind-down the Article commits to.
8 · Nuclear Iran reiterates it will never produce nuclear weapons; the fate of enriched material and all nuclear-related issues, including Iran’s “nuclear needs,” is “adequately addressed” in the final agreement. A restatement of Iran’s standing position. The version read aloud on signing adds on-site down-blending under IAEA supervision as the baseline method — gated to the final agreement and cutting against Iran’s simultaneous hardening of its HEU tunnels. The ~440kg core stays in Iran through the window. The file was touched kinetically for the first time this window: Iran's AEOI says a 19 Jul US strike hit the Darkhovin plant in Khuzestan (IAEA: very early construction, no nuclear material found at its last inspection). Araghchi said on 19 Jul that some nuclear demands may remain “unresolvable.”
9 · Status quo Pending the final agreement, Iran holds the status quo on its nuclear programme and the US imposes no new sanctions and does not strengthen its forces in the region. The strategic freeze: Iran’s programme stays intact while US leverage is capped. The most consequential clause for the deferral read. Both halves are now in breach — new US sanctions landed 14–15 Jul, and more F-16s, F-35s and refuellers are moving to the region.
10 · Oil waivers US Treasury issues waivers immediately after signing for Iranian crude, petrochemicals and derivatives, and related services (banking, insurance, transport). Signed edit tested: the waiver was revoked on 8 Jul — the first rollback of a core MoU economic term. The wind-down completed on 17 Jul: General License X1 expired, revoking all Iranian-oil transaction authority.
11 · Frozen assets Frozen and restricted Iranian funds released and made fully available in line with negotiation progress, directed by the Central Bank of Iran. Sequenced to negotiation progress — Iran’s leverage-extraction path. Vance conditions release on “verified steps” on the HEU; Vahidi insists the funds are not barred from military spending.
12 · Implementation A mechanism to oversee implementation and the commitment to the final agreement. Compliance architecture with no specified membership, voting or enforcement — to be designed in 60 days.
13 · Sequencing gate Nuclear negotiations begin only after Iran receives assurances on the implementation of Articles 4, 5, 10 and 11 (blockade, Hormuz, oil waivers, frozen assets); talks then address only the remaining articles. Iran’s verification gate — it can delay the nuclear talks by contesting any of those four implementations. The Article 10 revocation and the blockade's 14 Jul return each gave Iran grounds to invoke it. The 18 Jul suspension goes past the gate: rather than invoking Article 13 to pause the nuclear talks, Tehran suspended its commitments across the text.
14 · UNSC ratification The final agreement is approved through a binding UN Security Council resolution. China and Russia become structural guarantors — neither the US nor Israel can unilaterally exit the final deal without a UNSC crisis. Iran’s durability anchor.
Implementation — weeks 1–5 (carried)

The week-by-week implementation record through 16 Jul — the two-way first week, the kinetic second, the tanker attacks and waiver revocation of the third, the central-Iran strikes and Muscat rejection of the fourth, and the blockade's return in the fifth — is carried in the v7–v11 tabs. The through-line: each week tested a different Article, and by week five a core term (Article 4) had formally reversed.

Implementation — week 6 · v12

The window (Art 3): Tehran suspended its MoU commitments on 18 Jul — Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei declared Trump's signature “invalid,” Deputy FM Gharibabadi announced the suspension, and Rezaei said diplomacy and negotiations “are over,” while Ghalibaf (16 Jul, state TV) and a Nour News op-ed (18 Jul) still argued for the talks track. ISW's negotiations section for the window: “nothing significant to report.” Casualties: two US service members killed at Muwaffaq Salti Airbase, Jordan (17 Jul, ascribed to the IRGC); a third killed in Iraqi Kurdistan (18 Jul); US toll 17 since 28 Feb. Blockade (Art 4): enforcement kinetic — three vessels redirected, one disabled, the M/T Wen Yao boarded; General License X1 expired 17 Jul, completing the Article 10 reversal; the 30-day full-lift deadline passed in breach the same day. Hormuz (Art 5): the IRGC Navy's permission regime stated openly; a Thai-flagged vessel struck 17 Jul; Kpler counted eight transits on 16 Jul; UNCLOS-based “environmental services” fees floated. Strikes: sixth, seventh and eighth consecutive US nights (16–18 Jul) — Bandar Abbas bridges, rail and telecoms severed, Chabahar, Oghab 44, and the IRGC launch infrastructure behind the Gulf-state attacks; three US officials call them “shaping operations.” Retaliation: seven countries — Kuwait (power, desalination and oil facilities struck three days running), Bahrain, Qatar, Oman (US radar assets on the Salamah Archipelago and Ghanem Island), Saudi Arabia (al Kharj missile, sirens at Yanbu), Syria (al Tanf) and Jordan (Muwaffaq Salti, then four missiles at Aqaba on 19 Jul). Nuclear (Art 8): the AEOI says a US strike hit the Darkhovin plant (19 Jul); Araghchi calls some nuclear demands possibly “unresolvable.” Leadership: Araghchi disclosed a six-person SNSC special committee making on-the-spot decisions while Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei — severely injured in the strikes that killed his father — is reportedly hard to reach; ISW assesses Vahidi very likely a member. Yemen: Reuters (16 Jul, single root source) reports Iran instructed the Houthis to prepare to close Bab el-Mandeb if the US strikes Iranian power plants; Houthi-Saudi cross-border attacks resumed for the first time since 2022. Lebanon (Art 1): the LAF began deploying into the pilot zones 16–17 Jul — the one obligation moving forward.

Implementation — week 7 · v13

The window (20–23 Jul): the suspension holds — ISW's negotiations section still reads “nothing significant to report” — while a four-party written framework (Qatar, Egypt, Pakistan, Oman, ~20 Jul) revives the June interim agreement's shape: a 10-day ceasefire, both Hormuz lanes reopened (southern free of Iranian attack, northern free of US blockade), and the fee question posed as Malacca-style Iranian service fees or an IMO-administered fund. Iran confirmed receipt; no talks are scheduled. Second chokepoint: the Houthis blockaded Saudi Red Sea port calls from 20 Jul, citing the Saudi siege of Sana'a, and struck the Encelia and Layla on 22 Jul (one hit UKMTO-confirmed); Bab el-Mandeb transits fell 64 to 30, recovering to 40 by 23 Jul, with at least seven diversions and an EU Naval Force warning. Strikes: the eleventh and twelfth consecutive US nights reached Bushehr Airport, Larak Island, Behbahan (the IRGC 15th Special Forces Brigade base), western and north-western Iran, and the Parchin complex near Tehran; Iran struck Dhahran's King Abdulaziz Air Base (a first), US early-warning radar in Kuwait (an AN/FPS-117), Jordanian bases and Aqaba. Workarounds: Iran-Pakistan trade to expand from $3bn toward $10bn with five-fold truck traffic — the land answer to the naval blockade. Posture (23 Jul): Trump threatened Iran and the Houthis with “major military punishment” and told Axios he may expand strikes (“They haven't received enough pain yet”); equities fell and the 10Y hit a one-year high; dozens of fighters and refuellers continue moving to the region. Axis widening: Iranian-backed Iraqi militias likely droned Kuwait's Abdali border crossing on 23 Jul — the first Axis attack on a regional border crossing since 11 Jul — with explosions reported on Bubiyan Island; the IRGC claims a southern-corridor tanker struck a naval mine, a claim ISW cannot observe and reads as deterrence messaging against the Omani route.

Implementation — week 8 · v14

The window (24–28 Jul): the suspension stands on paper while both sides stop shooting — no US strikes in Iran after 24 Jul (the first pause in 13 nights), Iranian attacks ending with a single Bahrain strike in the first window, and a senior Iranian official telling Reuters (26 Jul) the halt lasts as long as Washington's does, conveyed directly. ISW's 27 Jul edition had “nothing significant to report” on negotiations; its 28 Jul edition leads with them — the pause itself remains behaviour, with no agreed term behind it. Blockade (Art 4): maintained and enforced through the pause — the LPG carrier Lavine disabled in the Gulf of Oman on 24 Jul after repeated run attempts, two sailors reported killed per Iranian media; the tanker Charminar boarded 25 Jul and released. Hormuz (Art 5): the Tehran table works the strait directly — Iran-Oman delegations met 24–25 Jul on restarting strait traffic — the lead proposal reopening the likely-mined middle passage — with the UK and France offering demining once safe and Iran seeking joint management likely to include fees (Bloomberg); Baghaei claims “progress” while saying strait traffic is unchanged, and ISW records no vessel attacks in the strait since 20 Jul with traffic fallen and deterrence running on the mine threat. The proposal's reported terms (27 Jul): a formal 10-day truce — the strait reopened; the blockade lifted, asset-unfreezing considered — with an Omani regional-consortium design echoing the defunct Clause 5 consultation requirement; Iran is refusing to concede control. Lebanon (Art 1): Mojtaba Khamenei reaffirms Hezbollah's preservation and full Israeli capitulation in Lebanon as the “first condition” for resolving the war under the 17 Jun MoU; the LAF accuses the IDF of hindering the pilot zones (27 Jul) while making its first pilot-zone weapons-cache seizure the same day (undocumented by ISW); Rome expansion talks are set for 4–6 Aug. Contested: the NYT reports Iran rejected the US framework carried by Iraqi PM Zaydi on 23 Jul — no temporary deal leaving Hormuz unresolved — and Zaydi's office denies it on 24 Jul; Pakistani officials say both Washington and Tehran “have responded” to the joint Pakistani-Qatari proposal, content uncharacterised.

Implementation — week 9 · v15

The window (28–31 Jul): the pause ends on Iran's initiative — IRGC ballistic missiles at Muwaffaq Salti Airbase (28 Jul, five intercepted per Jordan; CENTCOM: an “attempted surprise attack”), launched shortly after Iran rejected compromise on strait control (ISW). Hormuz (Art 5): the Omani proposal — reopen the strait against a lifted blockade with asset-unfreezing considered, a regional consortium and voluntary fees — is rejected (a senior official to Reuters, 29 Jul, “unrealistic”); Gharibabadi said on 28 Jul that Iran would go to war for strait sovereignty (per ISW); the IRGC Navy claims three tanker strikes on the southern route, the first strait vessel attacks since 20 Jul; transits run under 10/day (desk series). Blockade (Art 4): stands; Iran's Khatam ol Anbia headquarters had already called it an escalation the armed forces “will respond accordingly” to — the Jordan strike appears to be that response. The proxy front: joint US-Saudi strikes on militia weapons and logistics sites across Iraq and the Iran-Iraq border (28 Jul; retaliation for militia attacks on Saudi oil infrastructure and US interests per ISW; at least four Quds Force officers reported killed in Diyala); Trump promises to hit Iran hard; through ISW's 29 Jul cutoff no resumed US strikes on Iran itself, and no US answer to the Jordan attack. Lebanon (Art 1): carried — the first pilot-zone cache seizure stands (undocumented by ISW); Rome expansion talks 4–6 Aug. Second front: the Houthis threaten expanded attacks on Saudi targets and float Red Sea transit fees; Global21 carries a single-source claim of a ~50-nation Saudi coalition to force Bab el-Mandeb open, announcement expected “next week”; an unattributed drone attack targets a US-owned tanker at Damietta, Egypt (29 Jul) — a Mediterranean first, logged as a vector.

05 · Stakeholder map

Actor positions through the Stalemate / Resolution lens.

The Roaring Lion A/B/C framework is the analytical reference: Stalemate ≈ persistent Bad B; Resolution ≈ Good A via JPOA 2.0 + Hormuz Council + Security Guarantee. The US and Iran cells move on the break and the rejection; the GCC cell moves on Riyadh's strikes into Iraq; the Houthis, China, Israel and Europe cells gain texture.

United States
The US version of the terms — dismantle the nuclear programme, end Axis support — is far harder than Iran’s, so the signed ceasefire sat on an unreconciled core throughout. Trump chose the blockade track on 13–14 Jul, reinstating it against Iranian-flagged shipping, and strikes reached roughly 200km inland by 15 Jul. The campaign then widened past every prior round: three consecutive further strike nights (sixth through eighth) interdicting Iran's internal logistics — the bridges, rail junction and telecoms feeding Bandar Abbas, whose port handles ~85% of Iran's container traffic — plus the IRGC launch infrastructure behind the Gulf-state attacks, and, per Iran's AEOI, the Darkhovin nuclear plant on 19 Jul. Two US service members were killed at Muwaffaq Salti Airbase on 17 Jul and a third in Iraqi Kurdistan on 18 Jul — the first US deaths of the conflict, with no US stand-down following them. Three US officials characterise the strikes as “shaping operations” (Reuters); more F-16s, F-35s and refuelling aircraft are moving to the region, plus additional refuellers to Israel (Axios); WSJ reports a Trump threat against Pickaxe Mountain. US officials also told the WSJ they suspect PRC satellite reconnaissance and Russian locational intelligence are supporting Iranian targeting of US assets. Trump formally notified Congress under the War Powers Resolution on 10 Jul, putting a 60-day clock under the campaign. The pattern this file tracked through four rounds — hardline rhetoric giving way to renewed contact — broke in the fifth: Tehran's suspension landed instead. The 20–23 Jul state holds two paths open at once: Washington is exploring the four-party framework (Axios) while massing forces — Trump played the talks down on 22 Jul, then threatened Iran and the Houthis with “major military punishment” on 23 Jul and told Axios he is close to a decision on expanding strikes — the US “prepared to launch a massive attack”; “They haven't received enough pain yet.” The eleventh and twelfth strike nights reached Parchin, Bushehr Airport, Larak Island and western/north-western Iran; blockade enforcement continues. The two-path state resolved toward the talks on 24 Jul: a White House options meeting weighed expansion, then Trump reportedly ordered a strike hold hours after the Omani delegation reached Tehran — no wave has followed since. The blockade stays and turned lethal at the Lavine (24 Jul, two sailors reported killed per Iranian media; the Charminar boarded 25 Jul and released). Cheung frames the stance as diplomacy-preferred with military options live if Iran attacks allies or strait shipping; Trump says the US “is talking,” tells Axios “deep talks” are under way, and met Netanyahu in Washington (Bloomberg, 28 Jul), telling reporters there is a “good chance” talks with Iran make progress. The Jordan attack ended that posture: Trump promises to hit Iran hard (internal desk record), while the same day's joint strikes with Saudi Arabia on militia sites across Iraq were retaliation for militia attacks on Saudi oil infrastructure and US interests (ISW) — no resumed strikes on Iran itself, and no military response to the Jordan attack, through ISW's 29 Jul cutoff. The blockade stands. The Fed's 29 Jul hawkish hold with no forward guidance put the 30Y through its 2007 high, the war's oil leg cited in July's Treasury selloff.
Vows to hit Iran hard ·
joint strikes into Iraq
Israel
Citrinowicz: intent intact, capacity to disrupt constrained. A “complete end to the war in Lebanon” is an explicit Iranian red line, so Israel’s Lebanon campaign is a direct obstacle to any settlement; Israel is not a party to the signed Clause 1 and remains in southern Lebanon. Israeli sources quoted by Nadav Eyal expect a prolonged “war between the wars” rather than full resumption, with Israel not wanting the MoU to collapse outright ahead of the 27 Oct elections. There is still no unilateral Israeli strike on Iran. Israel is nonetheless closer to the exchange than in any prior window: it intercepted an Iranian drone near the Syria border (19 Jul), its air defences near Eilat engaged debris from the Aqaba launch, and Defence Minister Katz warned Iran that Israel will re-engage if attacked. On the Lebanon front the Rome-brokered pilot-zones plan moved from structure to implementation — the Lebanese Armed Forces began deploying into the first zones on 16–17 Jul, with the next zone expected at Zawtar al Charqiyeh. Citrinowicz's current read (19 Jul): the greatest challenge is now escalation management — events slipping beyond anyone's control, mediators exhausted and losing leverage, key IRGC figures convinced by the funeral turnout that they have domestic backing to intensify. WSJ (22 Jul): Israel is deliberately sitting the campaign out — the trigger still unfired — while Axios reports the IDF preparing a possible full-scale joint campaign within days and Netanyahu warning that any Iranian attack draws massive strikes. Netanyahu met Trump in Washington as the pause held (Bloomberg, 28 Jul); ISW records no confirmed Israeli strike through its 27 Jul cutoff — Israel still out of the campaign. Friction returned to the Lebanon track — the LAF accuses the IDF of hindering the pilot zones (27 Jul, the second such accusation in a week) — and Mojtaba's “first condition” statement (26 Jul) prices Hezbollah's preservation into any settlement, keeping the Lebanon file the structural obstacle Citrinowicz named. The pilot zones produced their first result the same window: the LAF's first Hezbollah weapons-cache seizure (27 Jul, per Israeli public media, undocumented by ISW), with Rome talks to expand the zones set for 4–6 Aug. Barclays flags an Israeli strike on Iranian sites as a live if unlikely risk ahead of the 27 Oct election; through the window Israel stays out of the campaign and the trigger stays unfired.
Good A/C →
regime change
Iran
Fractured — and the hardline camp is winning the fracture. Iran’s primary Hormuz objective is recognised sovereignty over the strait; it has used force to assert that control repeatedly, striking vessels on the Omani route and retaliating against the Gulf hosts, and on 16 Jul it named the strait an “inviolable red line.” On 18 Jul Tehran formally suspended the MoU — Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei declared Trump's signature “invalid,” Deputy FM Gharibabadi announced the suspension — and the red-line warning became execution: retaliation reached seven countries, adding Saudi Arabia (first strike on Saudi territory in about three months, with sirens at Yanbu), Syria (al Tanf) and southern Jordan (Aqaba), and Kuwaiti power, desalination and oil facilities were struck three days running. The killing of US troops crossed the one line every prior round had observed. The regime is split: Rezaei declares diplomacy over and threatens “full-scale destruction,” while Ghalibaf — branded a traitor by hardliners — defended the talks track on state TV on 16 Jul and a Nour News op-ed still argues for negotiation; Araghchi says some nuclear demands may remain “unresolvable.” Decision-making has its own new structure: Araghchi disclosed a six-person SNSC special committee (himself, Pezeshkian, Ghalibaf, Zolghadr, two unnamed — ISW assesses Vahidi very likely one) making on-the-spot decisions while Mojtaba, severely injured in the strikes that killed his father and unseen in public since, is reportedly hard to reach. Vali Nasr's structural read (FT): the MoU froze a balance of power both sides set out to change, Tehran has concluded restraint invites pressure, and it bets that closing Hormuz — potentially with Bab el-Mandeb and the Red Sea — forces Washington to blink first. This window Iran confirmed receipt of the four-party framework while vowing to “crush the source of US aggression” (Baghaei), put first missiles on Dhahran's King Abdulaziz Air Base, targeted US early-warning radar in Kuwait, and opened a land-trade workaround with Pakistan ($3bn toward $10bn). From 24 Jul Iran wound its attacks down to a single Bahrain strike — and said so: a senior official told Reuters (26 Jul) the halt lasts as long as Washington's does, conveyed directly. The terms are unmoved: officials tell the NYT Iran accepts no temporary deal leaving Hormuz status unresolved, and the reported 23 Jul rejection via the Zaydi channel is carried as contested after his office's denial. The base-casualty campaign that ran to 24 Jul — sleeping quarters at Sheikh Isa, Muwaffaq Salti, al Udeid, Arifjan and Buehring, aimed at building US domestic pressure — stopped with the pause; Aliabadi's “capture and send to hell” threat (24 Jul) stands unexecuted. Mojtaba is reported out of contact “due to certain issues” (Haddad Adel, 25 Jul), extending the incapacitation question over who directs the file. ISW's 28 Jul three-faction map puts the IRGC-Vahidi group — negotiate, but keep total strait control — likely at the wheel, pressed by a Pezeshkian/Araghchi/Ghalibaf camp that differs over means and by the Paydari Front's outright rejection of talks — a small minority with reach through Saeed Jalili on the Supreme National Security Council. The economic floor is sagging under all three: the rial collapsed, US intelligence assessing the regime struggles to pay its forces, and the first mass protest since January — Malekshahr, Esfahan (28 Jul), over the execution of three winter protesters. The rhetoric stays armed through the pause: the Khatam ol Anbia headquarters called the US blockade an escalation the armed forces “will respond accordingly” to (27 Jul), and Gharibabadi says Iran “is not afraid” of acting to establish strait sovereignty, or of the war resuming (28 Jul). The threatened response followed on 28 Jul: the IRGC Aerospace Force fired ballistic missiles at Muwaffaq Salti — ISW assesses Vahidi almost certainly supported the decision, with the pro-negotiations camp possibly backing it too, sharing the strait objective — and on 29 Jul a senior official told Reuters the Omani design is “unrealistic.” The IRGC Navy claims three tanker strikes; regime media claims the “initiative” and tells the US to “accept defeat.” 13D's read that the nuclear issue is “no longer viewed as taboo in Tehran” logs at section 02. Iran seeks PRC air-defence assistance (ISW).
Pause broken at Jordan ·
Omani design rejected
China
Publicly pushes Resolution (ceasefire, lanes reopened, Xi/Putin Beijing meeting), but the pivot is partly performative — PRC kept sending missile components and fuel precursors during the war. Per Pape, the war already shifted Beijing’s read of US resolve; munitions depletion (FT, Global21) reinforces it. Two new data points this window: US officials told the WSJ they suspect a Chinese reconnaissance satellite is supporting Iranian targeting of US assets — a step past performative neutrality if borne out — and China's state LNG buyers are contracting around the Gulf structurally (Qatari LNG to China down ~98% year on year Apr–Jun; Sinopec and PetroChina exploring 10+ year non-Gulf supply), the first permanent demand-side Gulf de-risking of the war. The intake's China read (Xie, Zakaria; circulated by David for the six-month scenario work): Beijing is absorbing the shock from its ~1.3–1.4bn-barrel reserve rather than cushioning prices for others, imports are down ~3.5–4mn bpd on stockpile draw, and some Hormuz transits reportedly settled in renminbi. Reuters (28 Jul, six sources): Beijing has asked the Houthis to pass its tankers through the Red Sea — one source describing Chinese officials clearing each ship individually — and at least four Saudi-oil cargoes bound for China have transited Bab el-Mandeb since the blockade began, repeating the 2024 arrangement Chinese and Russian diplomats reached with the Houthis in Oman. Self-arranged passage through the second chokepoint extends the absorb-the-shock read. The Atlantic (via David's channel) puts a number on the other half: China abruptly halved oil imports (~5mn bpd, about a quarter of the Hormuz loss) with no visible consumption decline and no explanation the experts can settle — “the most important factor in keeping prices down.” ISW adds that Iran is seeking PRC assistance to rebuild its air defences.
Pushes Resolution
Russia
Aligned with China publicly; has formally offered to take Iran’s HEU (acknowledged by Araghchi, “not currently under discussion”). Accepting HEU under the Security Guarantee architecture would be the technical pathway. Preserves Iran ties privately (drones via Caspian). WSJ (18 Jul): US officials suspect Russia is sharing locational intelligence on US warships and aircraft and advising on drone tactics. Zelenskyy (25 Jul): Russia is supplying satellite imagery of US Gulf bases, with imagery timing aligning to Iranian strikes (four bases surveilled 19–20 Jul); Reuters carries Western officials on “Russian-enhanced” Shaheds in the March CIA-outpost attack. Ukraine struck sanctioned Caspian vessels (25 Jul) that Russia reportedly uses to move military cargo toward Iran.
HEU custodian role available
Houthis / Yemen
The second front has an owner. The blockade of Saudi port calls — declared 20 Jul by spokesman Yahya Saree after two days of warnings to Saudi-owned ships, citing the Saudi siege of Sana'a — is the Houthis' first direct move against Riyadh in this war, and enforcement is calibrated: claimed missile and drone strikes on the Encelia and Layla (22 Jul), one hit confirmed by UK Maritime Trade Operations, a bow fire and no casualties per Saudi state media. Bab el-Mandeb transits fell from 64 to 30 before recovering to 40 by 23 Jul; at least seven vessels diverted and southern-Red-Sea war-risk cover doubled for some carriers. The Iranian hand is documented alongside the Houthis' own agenda: Reuters and ISW report a 13 Jul Mahan Air flight into Yemen carrying 10–21 IRGC personnel including senior commanders, missile- and drone-related equipment, training for “new missile systems” and gold to fund operations — diverted to Hudaydah when Saudi-backed forces struck Sanaa airport to block the landing. ISW: the Houthis “pursue their own agenda, but they remain a key Iranian partner,” and the Red Sea expansion serves Iran's campaign objective — degrading US willingness and ability to keep striking, and coercing acceptance of Iranian control over Hormuz. Trump fuses the two: the Houthis are a “Surrogate and/or Proxy of Iran,” and further attacks bring “major military punishment” on both. The stated off-ramp is bilateral — the blockade holds until Saudi Arabia lifts its blockade on Yemeni ports and airports. Enforcement ran through the US-Iran pause: the NCC Masa attacked (24 Jul), missiles at Yanbu intercepted by Greek-operated Saudi defences and munitions impacting Jizan, claimed strikes on the oil-transport corridor feeding the Red Sea hubs (27 Jul) — the Abqaiq fire unclaimed by any actor — while the Saudi coalition struck Hudaydah, Jawf and Marib and the Houthis downed a Saudi Akinci drone. Abdul Salam (24 Jul): the blockade applies to Saudi Arabia alone; ISW records the Houthis resisting Iran's full-closure ask for months, with NYT-cited officials saying that ask returns if Trump strikes Tehran or critical infrastructure. A third claimed attack on Saudi shipping followed: ballistic missiles on the NCC Ghazal (28 Jul, UKMTO-unconfirmed; the vessel left Yanbu on 23 Jul with AIS off). China's tankers pass by arrangement (Reuters, 28 Jul). The Houthis threaten to expand attacks on Saudi targets and, per regional sources (Reuters via ISW), may impose transit fees on commercial shipping in the Red Sea and Bab el-Mandeb — a fee regime to mirror Iran's strait demand. Against them, Global21 carries the claimed ~50-nation Saudi coalition (single-source); the same piece has Houthi retaliation reaching Abha Airport in southern Saudi Arabia after the mid-July Sana'a strikes, and logs claimed East-West Pipeline drone hits.
Blockade on Saudi calls ·
Iranian hand reported
GCC / Pakistan
Mediation delivered the ceasefire, and all GCC bar Oman reject permanent Iranian control of Hormuz; the bloc’s exposure has widened round by round as Iran coerces the hosts. This window the bloc absorbed the heaviest and widest round yet: Kuwait's civilian critical infrastructure struck three days running, Saudi territory struck for the first time in months with sirens at Yanbu — the Red Sea terminal now carrying ~4mn bpd, ~60% of pre-war Saudi crude exports, as the Hormuz bypass — and Oman moved from rebuffed mediator to target, with US radar assets on Omani territory struck and Tehran accusing Muscat of yielding to US pressure. Saudi Arabia, the UAE and Qatar condemned the infrastructure strikes and Jordan summoned an Iranian diplomat. The Yemen dimension deepened: Houthi-Saudi cross-border attacks resumed for the first time since 2022, and Reuters reports Iran instructed the Houthis to prepare to close Bab el-Mandeb — which would trap the Yanbu bypass and put Abqaiq, the single upstream node behind it, at the centre of the board. Kuwait is reportedly negotiating an expanded defence pact with Pakistan (Reuters, early stage). A Bloomberg feature flags the ~$3tn Saudi-UAE rivalry for regional capital as a fault line between the two bypass-hosting states. The prepared closure executed this window: a declared blockade of Saudi Red Sea port calls from 20 Jul and claimed strikes on two Saudi tankers on 22 Jul (one UKMTO-confirmed), with transits down 64 to 30 before recovering to 40 and war-risk cover doubling — the full picture is in the Houthis / Yemen row above. Saudi territory took its first Dhahran strike, and the bloc's mediators answered at larger scale: the Qatar/Egypt/Pakistan/Oman framework is the bloc's own product. v14: the bloc's mediators are inside the Hormuz track — Qatar, Pakistan and Egypt involved alongside Oman and the US envoys — while the Saudi front stayed the one front still exchanging fire once the US-Iran pause took hold: Saudi-reported shoot-downs of drones from Iraq aimed at Riyadh and Eastern Province petroleum facilities, the oil-transport corridor claimed struck, coalition strikes into Yemen. Iraq's PM is working Hormuz-bypass pipeline diplomacy — Kirkuk-Tripoli with Beirut (27 Jul), Kirkuk-Ceyhan in Ankara (28 Jul), the decommissioned IPSA line to the Red Sea in Riyadh planned for 30 Jul, outcome unreported — a structural de-risking of the strait. On the drone claims Baghdad pushes back: the Islamic Resistance in Iraq calls them “accusations,” the Iraqi NSA sees “no evidence,” Zaydi orders an investigation, and Iraqi-Saudi high-level talks convened 28 Jul; Jordan intercepted further drones from Iraq on 27–28 Jul. v15: Riyadh crossed a line — joint strikes with the US on militia sites across Iraq (28 Jul), the first Saudi offensive strikes beyond Yemen we have on record, with at least four Quds Force officers reported killed; the Islamic Resistance in Iraq had already vowed to respond “harshly” to any “foolish” Saudi action (27 Jul, ISW), the PMF vows revenge for the strikes (Global21), and Baghdad's investigation into the drone claims now runs alongside open warfare on its territory. Global21's claimed coalition — African Red Sea states agreed, five European navies invited, a ~100k proxy ground force waiting — is carried single-source. The Saudi–Pakistan airspace cross-promise still binds Israeli unilateral action.
Blockaded at Bab el-Mandeb ·
mediation retried
Europe
Aligns with the diplomatic track; refused the US request to reopen the Strait, so it is constrained from leading any post-war architecture. A NATO contribution to the HEU Security Guarantee would be the test. Switzerland hosted the formal ceremony and the first implementation round; the post-war security architecture would sit with other parties. The UK moved first: Trump and PM Burnham agreed on 19 Jul that Britain will take an active role in demining and reopening Hormuz — the first operational European alignment with the reopening effort, alongside the defensive-mandate EU naval presence. The UK and France have offered to lead Hormuz demining once conditions allow, and Hegseth is pressing the UK to host a maritime-security summit on strait traffic (Bloomberg, 27 Jul). Global21's coalition claim has France, Germany, Italy, the UK and the Netherlands invited — warships already in the region, said to await a Washington go-ahead (single-source, unverified).
Diplomatic backer
Japan / Korea
Lean on the US for Resolution; ~60% of energy via Hormuz. The US warned Japan of up-to-two-year Tomahawk delays as the Pentagon prioritises Middle East restocking — US overstretch landing on an Indo-Pacific ally, reinforcing the hedge. Dormant nuclear conversations re-open if the scenario shifts to apex. Capital Economics names Japan, with Pakistan and the Philippines, among the economies most exposed to physical fuel shortage under a dual-chokepoint closure.
Hedging

The 8 Jun – 31 Jul: whipsaw, signing, breakdown, a war with US casualties, a second chokepoint, a mutual pause, and the break

8–17 Jun
Ceasefire collapses then resolves onto de-escalation: Israeli and US strikes inside Iran (8–10 Jun); Trump declares the deal “complete” (15 Jun); the US and Iran sign the MoU two days early (17 Jun), with signed edits to Clause 1 (Lebanon) and Clause 5 (Hormuz). The blockade lifts and oil sanctions waive on signing.
19 Jun–12 Jul
Implementation, then four escalation rounds of rising scale: the first 60-day-track round at Bürgenstock (21–22 Jun); the Ever Lovely exchange and stand-down (25–30 Jun); the tanker attacks and oil-waiver revocation (6–8 Jul); the ~140-target wave reaching central Iran, five-Gulf-state retaliation, and Iran's rejection of Oman's two-corridor proposal (9–12 Jul). Talks resume at Iran's request (10 Jul) even as Trump maintains the ceasefire is over.
13–16 Jul
The blockade returns (14 Jul), targeted at Iranian-flagged shipping; the 20% fee floats and drops within a day; strikes reach roughly 200km inland (15 Jul); Iran strikes three vessels on the Omani route, retaliates against Kuwait, Bahrain and Jordan, and on 16 Jul declares the strait an “inviolable red line.” The Iran-Oman channel goes quiet; the mediation-breakdown trigger fires (v11).
17 Jul
An Iranian missile attack on Muwaffaq Salti Airbase, Jordan kills two US service members, with one missing — the first US deaths of the conflict. The sixth consecutive US strike night severs the bridges, rail and telecoms feeding Bandar Abbas; Iran strikes a Kuwaiti power-and-desalination plant and, for the first time, near al Tanf in Syria; a Thai-flagged vessel is struck on the Omani route; Kpler counts eight strait transits the prior day. General License X1 expires, revoking all Iranian-oil transaction authority; the MoU's 30-day full-lift deadline passes in breach. Murrin publishes WW3 116, declaring Chapter Two of the war.
18 Jul
Tehran formally suspends the MoU: Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei declares Trump's signature “invalid” and Deputy FM Gharibabadi announces suspension of commitments. The seventh US strike night; Iran fires a ballistic missile at a US base near al Kharj, Saudi Arabia — its first strike on Saudi territory in about three months, with sirens at Yanbu — and hits Kuwaiti power, desalination and oil facilities a second day. Brent settles ~$88, up 4.6%, the biggest weekly advance since April. A third US service member is fatally wounded in Iraqi Kurdistan disposing of ordnance from a downed Iranian drone.
19 Jul
CENTCOM announces the third US death. Iran's AEOI says a US strike hit the Darkhovin nuclear plant in Khuzestan (IAEA: very early construction, no nuclear material at the last inspection). Four ballistic missiles at Aqaba, southern Jordan — a first; Kuwaiti infrastructure hit a third day running. Araghchi disclosed a six-person SNSC committee deciding on the spot while the Supreme Leader is reportedly hard to reach, and says some nuclear demands may remain “unresolvable.” The eighth consecutive US strike night (18 Jul) included the IRGC unit behind the Jordan attack.
20 Jul
The IRGC Navy states its permission regime openly after halting four vessels: “not a single drop of oil, gas, or chemical fertilizer” passes without its coordination. The US moves more F-16s, F-35s and refuellers to the region; Israel's Katz warns Iran that Israel will re-engage if attacked. Saudi Arabia, the UAE and Qatar condemn the infrastructure strikes; Jordan summons an Iranian diplomat; China and Pakistan urge resumed dialogue. The Houthis declare a maritime blockade on Saudi Arabia effective immediately (Yahya Saree), citing the Saudi siege of Sana'a; the Qatar/Egypt/Pakistan/Oman written framework circulates, and Iran's FM spokesperson confirms receipt.
21–22 Jul
The eleventh and twelfth consecutive US strike nights reach Bushehr Airport, Larak Island, Behbahan, western and north-western Iran, and the Parchin complex near Tehran. Iran puts first missiles on Dhahran's King Abdulaziz Air Base and strikes US early-warning radar in Kuwait. The Houthi blockade turns kinetic: missile and drone strikes claimed on the Saudi tankers Encelia and Layla (one hit later UKMTO-confirmed), Bab el-Mandeb transits down 64 to 30, an EU Naval Force warning out. Ukraine strikes the Caspian Pipeline Consortium, halting ~1mn bpd of Kazakh flow. Brent settles $88.52 on 21 Jul, the highest since 11 Jun.
23 Jul
Brent settles $100.69, up 7%, after touching $102 — the first settle above $100 since May — on the Red Sea tanker attacks. Trump threatens Iran and the Houthis with “major military punishment” if the attacks continue — the Houthis named a “Surrogate and/or Proxy of Iran” — and tells Axios he may expand strikes (“They haven't received enough pain yet”), squaring the desk digest's “major operation” line. US equities fall (S&P -1.2%, Nasdaq -2.2%, Tesla and Alphabet earnings-day falls compounding) and the 10Y moves through 4.65% to 4.69%. Rubio: Tehran's leadership is “not ready to make a deal” (BBC). Dozens of fighters and refuellers continue deploying; no talks are scheduled on the framework. UKMTO confirms a Saudi-tanker hit; transits recover to 40. Iranian-backed Iraqi militias likely drone Kuwait's Abdali border crossing (material damage, no casualties) with explosions on Bubiyan Island; the IRGC claims a southern-corridor tanker mined — unobserved by ISW. The 13th consecutive US strike night follows into 24 Jul, with missiles reported on Qeshm Island in the strait; the House votes to direct a halt to the campaign, and the Senate blocks a similar measure hours later. [The desk Bloomberg series later puts the day's peak at ~$94 — the print divergence with the AP settle is flagged at section 06.]
24 Jul
Strikes reach northern Iran for the first time (the IRGC Navy commando college in Gilan) as Iran's drone campaign against US base housing runs across Bahrain, Jordan, Qatar and Kuwait. US forces disable the LPG carrier Lavine in the Gulf of Oman — two sailors reported killed per Iranian media — the first deaths in this file's blockade-enforcement record. The NYT reports a 23 Jul Iranian rejection of the US framework carried by Iraqi PM Zaydi; Zaydi's office denies it the same day (24 Jul) — carried as contested. Trump convenes an options meeting, an Omani delegation arrives in Tehran, and a strike hold is reportedly ordered within hours: no overnight wave into 25 Jul, the first pause in 13 nights. Unclaimed airstrikes are reported on Hudaydah.
25–26 Jul
The pause holds on both sides — one Iranian attack (Bahrain) in the first window, then none; Iranian officials say the restraint lasts as long as the US hold does. Iranian and Omani delegations meet in Tehran (24–25 Jul) on a Hormuz agreement; Baghaei claims “progress” (26 Jul) while saying strait traffic is unchanged, and parliamentarians insist control is not returning to pre-war conditions. Pakistani officials say both Washington and Tehran “have responded” to the joint Pakistani-Qatari proposal. CENTCOM boards the Charminar (25 Jul). The Saudi-Houthi exchange runs on: coalition strikes on Hudaydah, Jawf and Marib; Houthi missiles at Yanbu intercepted by Greek-operated defences while munitions impact Jizan; the NCC Masa attacked; a Saudi Akinci drone downed over Jawf. Mojtaba Khamenei is reported out of contact “due to certain issues” (Haddad Adel). A senior Iranian official tells Reuters (26 Jul) Iran will halt attacks as long as the US halts, conveyed directly; Mojtaba's 26 Jul statement names Hezbollah's preservation the “first condition” for resolving the war. Ukraine strikes sanctioned Caspian cargo vessels; Zelenskyy says Russia supplies satellite imagery of US Gulf bases.
27 Jul
Saudi Arabia says it shot down drones from Iraq aimed at Eastern Province petroleum facilities and Riyadh — ISW assesses Iranian-backed Iraqi militias, preserving Tehran's deniability — and a fire burns at Abqaiq, unclaimed by any actor; Saree claims strikes on the oil-transport corridor to the Red Sea hubs. The LAF accuses the IDF of hindering the pilot zones. Bloomberg details the middle-passage proposal — UK/French demining offers, joint management with fees, Witkoff and Kushner involved; Trump tells Axios “deep talks” are under way. Iraq's PM works the bypass-pipeline circuit toward Ankara and Riyadh. A US official and mediators tell Western media the Omani track has tabled a formal 10-day truce — the strait reopened against the blockade lifted and asset-unfreezing considered, a regional consortium proposed for management — and Iran is refusing to concede control. The LAF makes its first pilot-zone weapons-cache seizure (unverified by ISW); the Islamic Resistance in Iraq denies the Saudi drone claims.
28 Jul
Trump meets Netanyahu in Washington and tells reporters there is a “good chance” talks with Iran make progress (Bloomberg). Brent settles $84.09, down 4.8% on the day and roughly 16% over three sessions — the worst run since April 2020 — with WTI at $79.26, CTA Brent length cut to 62% from 73%, and CIBC reading the move as positioning on “MOU 2.0” hopes; Hormuz traffic stays muted with transponders dark. Treasuries rally as oil retreats; swaps price roughly one-in-three odds of a hike at the FOMC, with Citadel Securities and PGIM calling for one and August fed-funds futures open interest at records. Macquarie warns a deal would leave Q4 oversupplied by ~2mn bpd; OPEC+ is expected to pause quota hikes after September. The Houthis claim ballistic missiles on the tanker NCC Ghazal — their third claimed attack on Saudi shipping since 22 Jul (UKMTO-unconfirmed). Reuters: China has asked the Houthis for safe passage for its tankers — one source describes each ship being cleared individually — and at least four Saudi-oil cargoes to China have transited Bab el-Mandeb since 20 Jul. The first mass protest since January in Malekshahr, Esfahan, over the execution of three winter protesters. Iraqi-Saudi talks convene on the drone claims; Rome pilot-zone expansion talks are set for 4–6 Aug.
28–29 Jul · the break
IRGC ballistic missiles target Muwaffaq Salti Airbase in Jordan — an “attempted surprise attack” per CENTCOM, five intercepted per the Jordanian Army — ending the four-day pause shortly after Iran rejects compromise on strait control (ISW). The IRGC Navy claims strikes on three tankers on the southern route. The US and Saudi Arabia jointly strike militia weapons and logistics sites across Iraq and the Iran-Iraq border — retaliation for militia attacks on Saudi oil infrastructure and US interests — with at least four IRGC Quds Force officers reported killed in Diyala. On 29 Jul a senior Iranian official rejects the Omani proposal to Reuters (“unrealistic”); Gharibabadi (28 Jul, per ISW) says Iran would go to war for strait sovereignty; an unattributed drone attack targets a US-owned tanker at Damietta, Egypt; the Houthis float Red Sea transit fees. The Fed holds 9–3 with no forward guidance — the 29 Jul US session sells off (S&P -1.5% to 7,316) with the 30Y through its 2007 high and oil surging; Trump promises to hit Iran hard (internal desk record).
30–31 Jul
The AI leg turns the 30 Jul US session — Microsoft +16%, a record ~$450bn one-day add — while Brent settles ~$88 (desk series; its 23 Jul peak ~$94, the AP divergence flagged at section 06) and the SPR ledger shows 307.6m barrels with ~25m contracted barrels left. Global21 carries the claimed Saudi coalition to force Bab el-Mandeb open (single-source). The Kospi closes a record 18% higher on 31 Jul as the AI trade returns. 13D's WILTW reports “the nuclear issue is no longer viewed as taboo in Tehran” and calls August the most dangerous month.
What it confirms: the pause held while the strait compromise was on the table and broke shortly after Iran refused it. What it does not change: the bifurcated architecture, the apex held in the long-term allocation, the Murrin DD, the Brent bands. Watch-list: Iran-formal-response fires again — first fired at v12 on the MoU suspension, now on the Omani proposal: the rejection (a senior official to Reuters, 29 Jul) is uncontested — unlike the denied Zaydi-channel report — and is the formal response the trigger describes. The Israeli-strike trigger stays unfired; nothing new on the Saudi-Pakistan airspace item. To watch: whether and where the US responds militarily to the Jordan attack; whether the Pakistani-Qatari proposal, still under review by both sides, survives the break; the coalition claim, to be checked against the next ISW dailies; the floated Houthi transit fees; and the Lebanon pilot zones — Rome expansion talks 4–6 Aug.
The map — the deal suspends, the contest turns to leverage destruction

Stalemate stays modal because the US–Iran nuclear divide is wide and the hardliner faction blocks Good A. The ceasefire was a Stage-1 instrument that deferred every core; five escalation rounds of rising scale tested it, and in the fifth Tehran suspended its commitments outright while the exchange produced the first US deaths. The question v11 carried — whether hardline rhetoric again gives way to renewed contact — resolved toward breakdown: Ghalibaf's 16 Jul defence of the talks track was followed within two days by the suspension, and ISW records nothing on the negotiations file. What replaces it as the open question is narrower: whether a successor venue convenes (Qatar, Pakistan and Turkey circulate in intake reporting), and whether the split inside Tehran — a six-person committee deciding while the Supreme Leader is reportedly incapacitated, hardliners demanding revenge, Ghalibaf and Araghchi still holding the negotiation brief — resolves toward a reopened channel. The structural fault ISW identifies — Clause 5 read by Washington as restored passage and by Tehran as affirmed Iranian authority — is the same unresolved question the file has carried since v3, now stated by the primary source. v13 answers the venue half: the framework exists, in writing, with receipt confirmed; what has not happened is a table. The test the window sets is near-term — talks convene on the framework, or the escalation the President threatened on 23 Jul launches. v14 answers it: the talks convened — in Tehran rather than on the four-party text, Hormuz-first, with the US strike hold as the entry condition both sides observed. The narrower open question now is whether a middle-passage agreement can carry the weight assigned to it — the strait file is the same one that broke Clause 5, and Iran's stated term is unchanged: no temporary deal that leaves Hormuz status unresolved. ISW's 28 Jul edition sharpens it: the mediators' truce proposal met Iranian refusal on the strait, with ISW reading the refusal as showing Iran may calculate it can extract the blockade-lift without conceding control. v15: Iran rejected the proposal (a senior official to Reuters, 29 Jul) and ended the pause with the Jordan attack — testing whether it can extract the blockade-lift by force. Two questions are open: whether and where the US responds militarily to the Jordan attack, and whether any channel survives the break — the Pakistani-Qatari proposal is the one both sides still hold under review.

06 · Market & thematic implications

Scenario views across equities, Brent, rates, and rotation.

Views only. Brent bands and the scenario architecture are unchanged from v12; the second chokepoint stays live at Bab el-Mandeb, and the prints round-tripped the band — $100.69 on 23 Jul to $84.09 on 28 Jul — the desk series puts the 23 Jul peak at ~$94 (divergence flagged below), with ~$88 settled on 30 Jul.

ScenarioEquitiesBrentRatesSector & thematic rotation
Stalemate
Modal		 Mid-teens downside; EPS holds until ~2027 demand destruction. The 17 Jul session saw a broad US fall with SPX ending the week -1.5%, about 2% off its highs; Vanda (18 Jul) reads the right tail as “all but disappeared” with positioning still elevated and systematic flows biased down — the modal outcome flat, the skew down. The oil-to-equity transmission has compressed: roughly 1.5% S&P downside per 10% oil rise, versus ~4% in March. The 23 Jul session: Dow -1.0%, S&P -1.2%, Nasdaq -2.2% — oil pressure compounded by Tesla (-14.5%) and Alphabet (-7.1%) earnings-day falls — with European energy outperforming (Shell +1.6%, BP +3.1%) and American and Southwest down 8.4% and 6.2% on fuel bills despite earnings beats (AP). By 27–28 Jul the systematic pressure eased with oil: positioning back to neutral (+0.02 SD, the least crowded since early April), CTA risk ran live into the FOMC and big-tech earnings — ES1 below 7,440 restarts selling, above ~7,530 resumes buying — and Treasuries rallied as oil fell (Vanda). The sessions then split by leg: 29 Jul (FOMC day, the break) S&P -1.5% to 7,316 and Dow -2.2% with energy outperforming in Europe and the FTSE at an intraday all-time high on BP/Shell; the AI leg turned 30 Jul (Microsoft +16%, the biggest one-day dollar add in stock-market history) and the Kospi closed a record 18% higher on 31 Jul. Tight, elevated, drawing inventory. The prints landed: Brent closed $82.96 on 16 Jul (desk series) and settled ~$88 on 17 Jul per Bloomberg — up 4.6%, the biggest weekly advance since April, on the troop deaths, the Saudi extension and the Axios refueller report — with the front month in backwardation (~-$1.3), the prompt-tightness signature of a strait that has not reopened. Kpler counted eight transits on 16 Jul. SPR 316.5m barrels (10 Jul, lowest since April 1983, ~34m contracted barrels left); total US crude stocks 730.8m, lowest since 1984; US gasoline ~$4.50–4.56. The gas complex has no bypass shield: Asia LNG +94% and TTF +79% versus pre-war. External calls bracket this file's bands — Barclays $65–80 below the floor (carried watch item), Murrin above $150 (his call, logged at section 01), CE +$15–20/bbl on a dual-chokepoint closure. This window: Brent settled $88.52 on 21 Jul, $89.75 intraday on 22 Jul, then $100.69 on 23 Jul — up 7%, $102 intraday, highest since May, YTD +62% (AP) — the first settled print inside the $90–110 band, on the Red Sea tanker attacks; three weeks earlier it was below $72. Gasoline prints diverge and are flagged: AAA $4.09 per AP (23 Jul) against the desk monitor's $4.65 AAA series (20 Jul), with the DOE weekly at $4.00; UK gas ~150p/therm from 98p at end-June (BBC). TTF took its first EUR60 handle since March and Asia LNG has doubled versus pre-war; the CPC strike subtracts ~1mn bpd of Kazakh supply outside the Gulf. The dual-chokepoint contingency is partially live: Saudi-flagged traffic is blocked at Bab el-Mandeb while Hormuz runs the IRGC permission regime. Then the round trip: Brent fell ~16% over three sessions to settle $84.09 on 28 Jul (WTI $79.26), the worst run since April 2020, on de-escalation positioning — CTA length 73% → 62% in a day, flow not normalised, dark transits continuing; December futures below $82.30. Macquarie projects a ~2mn bpd Q4 surplus on a deal; OPEC+ is expected to pause quota hikes after a final September increase. CE's Ukraine-drone note quantifies the third subtraction: ~4mn bpd of Russian refining capacity hit, CPC ~1mn bpd halted. v15: the desk Bloomberg series (Signaling Reserve v2.9) puts the 23 Jul peak at ~$94.26 against the AP-reported $100.69 settle — a print divergence: both prints are carried, and the desk series is used for the running ledger — and has ~$88 settled 30 Jul. SPR is 307.6m barrels at 24 Jul, a deepening 43-year low, with ~25m contracted barrels left (~5–6 weeks at the current pace, against an in-house operational-floor estimate near 200m); Hormuz transits run under 10/day. CE (30 Jul) calls the physical market “still dire” and flags a tipping point without a sustained flow recovery; the Atlantic's China piece attributes the price containment to a ~5mn bpd Chinese import halving no one can fully explain. Bands hold. The re-closure scenario the market prices as a ~$150 shock remains the near-term, non-nuclear face of the escalation leg. Fiscal pressure lifts yields; energy tightening Jul–Sep adds to the curve. The 10Y sits near 4.60–4.65% (Vanda); CE traces the June rise in the 30-year mortgage rate (6.59%) partly to Iran-driven Treasury yields. The 10Y closed 4.628% on 21 Jul — 2.2bp from the shock monitor's Stage-4 threshold, the war's closest approach — then moved through it on 23 Jul — 4.69%, a one-year high (3.97% pre-war); the 30Y at 5.131%; MOVE back above its pre-war level; Fed-hike odds for the 28–29 Jul meeting jumped to 36% from 12% (CME via AP, 23 Jul), and the ECB held. Into the FOMC (Warsh's second), swaps priced roughly one-in-three for 25bp (28 Jul), Citadel Securities and PGIM called for a hike, August fed-funds futures open interest set records two days running, and a 25bp hike was fully priced by September. Vanda frames the war's rates move as a real-rate shock — the real 2Y near 2%, +112bp since the war's start — with CTAs near max-short the long end; Treasuries rallied 28 Jul as oil retreated, roughly half the prior week's selloff having been oil-driven. The FOMC held 3.50–3.75% (9–3; Logan, Hammack, Kashkari for a hike) with no forward guidance — the 30Y rose through its 2007 high, the 10Y to 4.66%, the 2Y down to 4.23%; September is ~60% priced and December fully priced, with the street split wide (BofA three hikes against Citi three cuts). July's Treasury selloff is attributed in part to the war's oil leg. Iran-axis exposure screens unattractive on the retaliation vectors (UAE data centres, regional fintech, Gulf cable-dependent payments) — Jordan and Oman directly-struck hosts. The screen widens on two fronts: Gulf civilian utilities and energy infrastructure are now realised targets (Kuwait's desalination-dependent water system; the KPC oil facility), and the Red Sea / Bab el-Mandeb vector puts the Yanbu bypass (~4mn bpd, ~60% of pre-war Saudi crude exports) and Abqaiq — the single upstream node behind it — on the board. China's LNG contracting away from Qatar (down ~98% year on year) is the first structural demand-side shift. China sleeve re-rates up on the Beijing read of US weakness; munitions depletion (FT, Global21) is the observable marker. Energy-paradigm shift favours rare earths, security and infrastructure; defensive on regional refining. The undersea-cables / SWIFT non-oil vector carries. Two additions: the Red Sea leg is live risk (war-risk cover pulled on Saudi calls and southern-Red-Sea cover doubled for some carriers on 23 Jul — Reuters; the ~2.5mn bpd Asia-bound Yanbu leg rerouting via the Cape at 10–14 extra days), and a reported IRGC strike on an Amazon data centre in Bahrain (13D, single report) moves Gulf digital infrastructure from screened vector to claimed event. v14 adds: Saudi oil infrastructure inside the screen — the Abqaiq corridor claimed struck and drones on Eastern Province facilities — and, on the other side, Iraq's bypass-pipeline circuit (Kirkuk-Ceyhan, IPSA, Kirkuk-Tripoli) as the structural answer taking shape around the strait. v15 adds: the Mediterranean port vector (the Damietta drone) joins the screen; the claimed coalition puts Red Sea war-risk repricing on both sides of a possible forcing operation; and the Iraq front adds Gulf-adjacent infrastructure (border crossings, pilgrimage routes) to the exposure map. Energy outperformance held through the break (FTSE record on BP/Shell; Total, Eni).
Resolution
Proposal rejected		 Mid-single-digit upside. Declines toward $90–110 over months; market below the band for most of the deal's life — $82.96–~$88 settled 16–17 Jul, escalation-driven with no Resolution progress behind it, then into the band on the 23 Jul spike — pricing a clean reopening the substance does not support; trust rebuild takes quarters. v14: back below the floor at $84.09, this time on talks optimism, ahead of any agreement from the Tehran table. v15: ~$88 settled 30 Jul after the rejection, still below the floor. Yields ease as the energy premium unwinds. Iran-axis exposure screens better selectively; the energy/renewables opportunity set persists on the structural energy-paradigm shift; the China sleeve recalibrates on a firmer Hormuz-access outcome.

Apex / tail: escalation toward $130–160+ Brent is held in the long-term allocation; first-use ($200+, SPX ~3,500) sits below the scenario-modelling threshold as structural tail protection. The deal's re-timing of this tail to the 60-day window is itself suspended as of 18 Jul, so the window is open-ended; the HEU stays in Iran, hardened, and unmentioned in this window's exchanges. The Darkhovin strike is the first kinetic touch on the nuclear file this cycle — an early-construction site with no nuclear material at the last IAEA inspection — and Murrin's $150+ call sits inside this leg as his conviction, ahead of the file's own. Two proliferation-adjacent data enter the log: WSJ reports Washington will greenlight a 30-year US-Saudi civil nuclear agreement potentially permitting Saudi enrichment, and the Pickaxe Mountain chatter extends to unattributed tactical-nuclear talk (13D) — both logged, neither carried. A third enters at v14: the NYT-reported US intelligence read on Mojtaba's weapon interest, against Albright's degraded-programme assessment — logged and not carried. v15 logs two more, neither carried: 13D's “the nuclear issue is no longer viewed as taboo in Tehran… the IRGC is spear-heading a new strategic doctrine” (WILTW, 30 Jul, full text on file — including a reported debate on NPT withdrawal), and Barclays' last-option framing of a strike on the Isfahan HEU stockpile or Pickaxe Mountain. The Taiwan-war and US-default extrapolations are held as backdrop only, outside the modelled set.

The $300bn in the signed terms is external Gulf investment funded by sanctions relief — not a US Treasury outlay — which reconciles the terms with Iran’s frozen-asset demand; the relief flows through GCC capital.

Resolution mechanics

The Resolution path follows the framework David specified: Mearsheimer’s “humble pie” off-ramp; a JPOA 2.0 nuclear architecture; the Ferguson Hormuz Council (Iran as Chair, a 45% / 30% / balance revenue split, transit fees at cost); HEU export under a Security Guarantee with Russia available as custodian; and a post-war Gulf Treaty Organisation with China and Russia as signatories. The realised Stage-1 MOU diverged from the Council mechanic on Hormuz — toll-free reopening under Iranian and Omani management rather than a chaired revenue split — and the deeper cores (HEU ~440kg, enrichment, sanctions sequencing) remained for the 60-day track. Bremmer's independent framing of the eventual off-ramp — institutionalised, divided Iranian management funded by a Gulf collective, on the Bosphorus or Malacca model — carries as a variant of the deferred mechanism. This window put the primary source behind the fault line: ISW assesses the two sides interpreted Clause 5 in fundamentally different ways — Washington as a return to pre-war passage, Tehran as affirmation of Iranian authority with safe passage only through Iran's traffic-separation scheme — and that the divergence contributed to the collapse; the Gulf states rejected Iranian management and fees. Nasr's structural read runs the same direction from the Iranian side: the MoU froze a balance of power both parties set out to change, so the return to war is deliberate policy rather than a misreading of terms. With the track suspended from 18 Jul and Oman then compromised as a channel, the mechanics carried as the reference for whatever venue resumed them — Qatar, Pakistan and Turkey circulated in intake reporting as candidates, none convened through v13. The four-party framework is the first written variant of the deferred mechanism to reach the desk: dual-lane reopening with the fee question posed as Malacca-style Iranian service fees or an IMO-administered fund — the sovereignty-versus-fees question the file has carried since v3, now in proposal text. The Tehran table is working a second variant: reopening the likely-mined middle passage under a joint management system likely to include fees, with UK/French demining offered and the Witkoff/Kushner channel inside the room — the first negotiation since the collapse to put the strait itself first in sequence. Oman's regional-consortium proposal (ISW, 28 Jul) is a third variant — regional management echoing the defunct MoU's consultation clause, the nearest design yet to the Council mechanic — tabled inside a 10-day-truce frame Iran is so far refusing on the control question. Its voluntary-fee predecessor was itself refused: Iran rejected Oman's early-July donations design and maintained its demand to levy fees (WSJ via ISW, 2 Jul). The consortium variant is rejected too (a senior official to Reuters, 29 Jul, “unrealistic”) — every management design short of recognised Iranian control and levied fees has now been refused — answering the sovereignty question this file has carried open since v3. The mechanics stay as the reference for whatever channel survives; the Pakistani-Qatari proposal is the one both sides still hold under review.
